楠溪江中游

The Middle Reach of Nanxi River

引言

Introduction
引言
中文原文重构

引言

在这本书里，我们呈献给读者们的，是我们对一群最美的村落的研究成果，它们在楠溪江中游。

楠溪江在浙江省东南部的永嘉县，是瓯江最大的、也是最后的一条支流。它的流域夹在括苍山脉和雁荡山脉之间，是一个独立的地理区。由于环境封闭，它也是一个独立的经济区和文化区。

1989年12月初，乍寒天气，胡理琛先生把我们带到了楠溪江，他主管着那儿的规划和建设，对那儿的乡土建筑做过多次调查，写过介绍它们的最初几篇文章。

一到楠溪江，我们又惊又喜。古老的村落使我们感到非常新鲜。村子里既没有深宅大院，也没有飞楼杰阁；既没有琐窗网户，也没有雕梁画栋。然而用蛮石原木建成的房屋是那样朴素真实，自然明朗。村子规划得那样严谨和谐，完整统一。大多数房子是外向的，开敞的，四面开门开窗的。它们需要间隔，因此每幢房子保持了它独立的形体，独立的品格。村子的大部分也因此比较疏朗，房前屋后，竹树浓绿。整个村子的水系、街巷，有条有理。多层级的各种公共建筑物和活动中心分布在各处。渠边塘沿的大石条上，妇女们在洗涤，身边缠着孩子；村头路口的小凉亭里，老人们悠闲地聊天唱戏。生活的画卷展开在公众眼前，走在街上还能隔矮墙见到谁家用石臼打年糕，用木架晾面条。村落的建筑环境连我们这些陌生人都觉得亲切祥和。最出乎我们意外的，是许多村落里居然有公共园林，常常以大片的人工湖为核心，湖面上飘着近树远山的倒影。

等我们了解了楠溪江的文化史，我们再一次感到又惊又喜。原来从东晋以还，历代都有代表当时最高文化水平的学者文人们到这里来任地方长官，来隐居，来游览山水，探访当地的名贵高士，来诗文聚，谢灵运的足迹。其中最著名的当然是先后永嘉太守的王羲之，至今谢氏村落已经将近二十座。我们踏着石步，渡过清溪，来到幽谷深处的鹤洋村，祠堂里康乐公的神位赫然尚在。村村溪边浣衣女的"鹅兜"，参差成群，人人都说是右军饲养的白鹅变成。穿梭在楠溪江上的紫燕，背上驮着的夕阳还曾照亮过响应文天祥义军的刀枪。这真是一次奇异的感情经历，千余年的文化史，忽然浓缩到眼前，似乎可以触摸。

于是，我们兴致勃勃，初步调查了楠溪江中游的乡土建筑。我们大体认识到：

第一，楠溪江有一些很古老的村落，有记载可查的，如初建于晚唐的茗岙村、下园村和五代的枫林、花坛、苍坡、西巷、周宅等村。建于两宋的就更多了，如芙蓉、廊下、鹤洋、渠口等村建于北宋，豫章、溪口、岩头、蓬溪、塘湾等村建于南宋。

第二，许多村落经过统一的、综合的规划。村子有围墙寨门，墙里有整齐的街巷网和水系，有礼制中心、文化中心、休闲中心和园林绿化等开放空间，有公共的生活设施。有些村落的规划是宋代制定的，至今还局部甚至大部保存着当年的遗迹，如苍坡、塘湾、芙蓉等村。这些村子的规划把建筑环境形成一个完整的生活服务系统。

第三，不少村落里有许多明、清两代的建筑，甚至还可能有宋代的住宅。在一些明、清时代的建筑上，还有在官式建筑中早就没有了的构件和古老的做法，如生起、侧脚、卷杀以及月梁、圆栌斗、层叠的丁头棋和杠杆式地起结构作用的下昂等等，还流行各种减柱造的构架。许多村子里还有不少房子用木柱础，木抱鼓也可以在一些宗祠里见到。

第四，建筑类型很丰富，几乎包括了商品经济发展之前农村里可能有的全部建筑类型，尤其是文化建筑，如书院、读书楼、文昌阁、文峰塔、文庙和一些起教化作用的牌坊、亭阁等等。园林和它的小品建筑也可以算是一种文化建筑。少数水陆交通要道的村落，如枫林、岩头、桐州、廊下、鹤盛等，晚近也出现了一些商业建筑，甚至形成商业街道。

第五，不论是村落的规划还是个别的房屋设计，都表现出楠溪江人发达的环境意识，表现出他们对山川自然的亲切感和审美能力。村落的选址、布局和入口处理，开放空间的配置，一些公共建筑和祭祀建筑典雅清淡的形式，自然景物如山、水的利用，大型园林的开辟，住宅和文化建筑的形制和风格，粗野的天然蛮石和原木的巧妙运用，建筑物身上精致敏感的曲线曲面，等等，都是这种亲切感和审美能力的明证。

第六，楠溪江建筑的风格非常平淡天然，就像它们的原木、蛮石一样平淡天然。庄子说："朴素而天下莫能与之争美"（《天道》），"淡然无极而众美从之"（《刻意》）。楠溪江建筑，美得就像那些纯朴的农民。楠溪江曾经孕育过谢灵运的诗。钟嵘《诗品》说："汤惠休曰：谢诗如芙蓉出水，颜如错彩镂金，终身病之。"楠溪江建筑的美，也是"芙蓉出水"的美，比之于有些地区，如皖南的"错彩镂金"的建筑，它是一种境界更高的美。两者风格的差别，就是乡民性格与商贾性格的差别。不但个体建筑如此，村落的整体面貌也如此。

第七，楠溪江大多数村落的布局以及它们的建筑物的形制和风格，社会性的分化还不大。最纯朴自然的民间建筑风格占着绝对的主导地位。大多数建筑物是独立的，保持着自己的形象和个性。除了少数宗祠和"进士第"之类的建筑物外，房屋大多外向开敞，不设防、不拒人，因此造成了整个村落的宽畅爽快，亲切安逸，教人感到乡亲们坦诚的胸怀。它大不同于江南有些地方由内向封闭的院落形成的村落，那里小巷夹在高高的封护墙之间，阴暗而狭窄，个体房屋被连续的高墙吞没，失去了独立性，失去了自己的品格。生活场景大多被封闭在院落里。那些小巷里森严的防范性，叫外来人感到压抑不安。

大体得到这些认识之后，我们可以说，楠溪江流域是一个独立的，特点鲜明的建筑文化圈，一个有很高人文意义的建筑文化圈。楠溪江乡土建筑，在我国乡土文化宝库中闪烁着特殊的光辉。

然而，"无可奈何花落去"，近年来农村经济蓬勃繁荣，村民们纷纷营造新屋，于是，大量乡土建筑被拆除。千余年来形成的和谐的建筑环境被破坏，多少高度发达的乡土文化的见证飞快地消失了。我们为新的建设高兴，也为文化遗产的毁灭伤心。

于是我们决心放下从事了几十年的工作，趁楠溪江乡土建筑还没有完全失去之前，对它做一番研究，留下一份资料，聊尽我们对民族文化的赤子之忧。

研究乡土建筑，必须有宽阔的人文视野。虽然搜集一批实例，罗列介绍，也不失为一种方法，但我们希望把工作做得更进一步。一方面，我们打算把乡土建筑本身阐释得更深入；一方面，我们打算把乡土建筑与整个自然和文化环境紧密联系起来阐释。

我们对工作的设想是：

第一，以乡土建筑研究来代替一向流行的民居研究。我们不赞成把民居从乡土建筑中割裂出来，单独地研究，更不赞成把民居之外各种类型的乡土建筑类型弃置不顾。乡土建筑的基本单元是一个个完整的聚落，它所包含的各种类型的建筑形成了它的内部结构。我们不可能不分别地研究各种类型的建筑，但力求把它们放在聚落结构的关联中来研究。我们更要研究聚落系统的整体以及它和外部环境的关系，包括环境中的地理、历史、文化、社会等因素。只有以生活圈为单元，全面研究乡土建筑的各种类型，才能阐明人们生活的建筑环境和文化环境的联系。

第二，为了整体地研究乡土建筑，应该以一个个基本的"生活圈"为单元，而不是孤立地研究单栋的房屋。生活圈有相对的独立性，它有比较牢固的、稳定的内部联系和结构，或者是血缘的，或者是地缘的，或者是宗教的、行业的。它有一个或隐或显的凝聚核心，一个或隐或显的边界。在这个生活圈里，乡土建筑大致能自成一个系统。楠溪江的村落在自给自足的自然经济下形成，一个血缘村落就是一个生活圈，所以，我们在楠溪江可以拿有主姓的血缘村落作为研究单元。

第三，我们打算把乡土建筑当做乡土文化的一部分来研究，乡土建筑研究应该是乡土文化研究的一个篇章。这要求突破就建筑论建筑这个流行的狭隘框框，扩大我们的视野，开放我们的思路，发散我们的探索方向。文化的核心是生活模式和相应的价值观。村落和房舍，是生活的环境，生活的舞台，它们与人们的生活发生着千丝万缕的关系，交换着影响。乡土建筑必然反映出乡村生活的模式和相应的价值观。阐释乡土建筑，不能不阐释它和生活的关系，从而阐释它和乡土文化的全面关系。

第四，应该对乡土建筑系统作动态的研究。一是社会、经济、文化发展所引起的村落和房屋的变化，一是材料、技术的发展所引起的。只有在动态中才能清晰地看出生活与作为生活舞台的大建筑环境的相互影响和相互塑型的作用，就是要研究聚落本身的发育过程。这也就是说，要研究乡土建筑运动着的历史。

第五，要采用比较的方法。一切事物的特色存在于它与其他可比事物的比较之中。只有通过比较才能认识事物的特色。我们确定楠溪江是一个建筑文化圈，就是把当地建筑的类型、形制、构造方法、形式、风格和聚落形态等与其他地区的作比较，认识了特点，才得到的结论。在村子内部，把礼制建筑与居住建筑或者某些文化建筑比较，可以看到上层雅言文化与下层民俗文化在建筑风格上的不同表现和相互影响，看出乡土建筑中的社会性分化与对立。用不同发育程度的村落作对比，也能看出它们发育的过程来，例如从只许在家里偏屋做买卖到商业街道的形成过程。把在各种不同地理条件下的村落拿来比较，也会加深我们的认识。

总而言之，我们希望在楠溪江乡土建筑的研究中，采用整体的、联系的、动态的和比较的方法。

但是，条件很困难。几十年来。农村经历了一次又一次激烈的斗争和变化，竟至于使农村里已经没有人全面而系统地知道本乡本土的历史，传统的礼仪、习俗，连宗祠的作用和活动，也不大说得清楚。我们也找不到真正懂得乡土建筑的老木匠。文字资料已经大多散失，幸存下来的宗谱残缺不全，而且本来编纂的水平就不高，体例不严谨，项目不完备。绝大多数村落的规划年代和现存房屋的建造年代已经无法考证。这种情况大大限制了我们的方法的适用性。

English Translation

Introduction

In this book, we present to readers the research results of a group of the most beautiful villages, located in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River.

The Nanxi River is in Yongjia County, southeastern Zhejiang Province, and is the largest and last tributary of the Ou River. Its basin is sandwiched between the Kuocang Mountains and the Yandang Mountains, forming an independent geographical region. Due to its enclosed environment, it is also an independent economic and cultural region.

In early December 1989, in the early cold weather, Mr. Hu Lichen took us to the Nanxi River. He was in charge of planning and construction there, had conducted multiple surveys of the local vernacular architecture, and written the first few articles introducing them.

Upon arriving at the Nanxi River, we were both surprised and delighted. The ancient villages felt very fresh to us. There were no deep mansions or towering pavilions; no lattice windows or carved beams and painted pillars. Yet the houses built of rough stone and raw timber were so simple, authentic, natural, and bright. The village planning was so rigorous, harmonious, and complete. Most houses were outward-facing, open, with doors and windows on all sides. They needed separation, so each house maintained its independent form and character. Most of the village was therefore relatively open, with dense green bamboo and trees in front of and behind houses. The entire village's water system and streets were well-organized. Multi-level public buildings and activity centers were distributed throughout. On the large stone strips along canals and ponds, women washed clothes with children at their sides; in small pavilions at village entrances and crossroads, elderly people chatted and sang opera leisurely. The scroll of life unfolded before the public eye. Walking down the street, one could see over low walls who was pounding New Year cakes in a stone mortar or drying noodles on wooden racks. The architectural environment of the villages felt warm and auspicious even to strangers like us. Most unexpectedly, many villages actually had public gardens, often centered around large artificial lakes, with reflections of nearby trees and distant mountains floating on the water.

When we learned the cultural history of the Nanxi River, we were once again surprised and delighted. It turned out that since the Eastern Jin dynasty, generations of scholars and literati representing the highest cultural level of their times had come here to serve as local officials, to live in seclusion, to tour the mountains and rivers, to visit local renowned scholars, and to gather for poetry and prose. The most famous were of course Wang Xizhi and Xie Lingyun, who successively served as governors of Yongjia. To this day, there are nearly twenty villages of the Xie clan. We stepped on stone stepping stones, crossed clear streams, and came to Heyang Village deep in a secluded valley, where the spirit tablet of Duke Kangle (Xie Lingyun) was still prominently displayed in the ancestral hall. The "goose baskets" of village women washing clothes by streams were clustered in groups, and everyone said they were transformed from white geese raised by Wang Xizhi (Youjun). The swallows shuttling over the Nanxi River carried on their backs the setting sun that once illuminated the swords and spears of Wen Tianxiang's loyalist army. This was truly a strange emotional experience—over a thousand years of cultural history suddenly condensed before our eyes, seemingly tangible.

Thus, enthusiastically, we conducted a preliminary survey of vernacular architecture in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River. We generally recognized:

First, the Nanxi River has some very ancient villages with verifiable records, such as Ming'ao Village and Xiayuan Village first built in the late Tang dynasty, and Fenglin, Huatan, Cangpo, Xixiang, and Zhouzhai villages from the Five Dynasties. Many more were built during the Northern and Southern Song dynasties, such as Furong, Langxia, Heyang, and Qukou villages built in the Northern Song, and Yuzhang, Xikou, Yantou, Pengxi, and Tangwan villages built in the Southern Song.

Second, many villages underwent unified, comprehensive planning. Villages had walls and gates, with orderly street networks and water systems inside, ritual centers, cultural centers, leisure centers, and garden landscaping as open spaces, as well as public living facilities. Some village plans were formulated in the Song dynasty, and remnants from that time are still partially or even largely preserved today, such as in Cangpo, Tangwan, and Furong villages. The planning of these villages formed the architectural environment into a complete living service system.

Third, many villages contain numerous buildings from the Ming and Qing dynasties, and possibly even Song dynasty residences. On some Ming and Qing buildings, there are components and ancient practices long disappeared from official-style architecture, such as shengqi (corner rise), cejiao (column inclination), juansha (entasis), as well as yueliang (crescent beams), round column capitals, layered dingtou brackets, and lever-style lower ang brackets that play structural roles. Various column-reducing frameworks were also popular. Many villages still have houses using wooden column bases, and wooden drum-shaped stone bases can be seen in some ancestral halls.

Fourth, the building types are very rich, including almost all types that might exist in rural areas before the development of commodity economy, especially cultural buildings such as academies, study towers, Wenchang pavilions, Wenfeng pagodas, Confucian temples, and some archways and pavilions serving educational functions. Gardens and their accessory buildings can also be considered a type of cultural architecture. In a few villages at land and water transportation hubs, such as Fenglin, Yantou, Tongzhou, Langxia, and Hesheng, some commercial buildings appeared in recent times, even forming commercial streets.

Fifth, both village planning and individual house design demonstrate the developed environmental awareness of Nanxi River people, as well as their intimacy with mountains and rivers and aesthetic ability. Village site selection, layout, and entrance treatment; the configuration of open spaces; the elegant and austere forms of some public and ritual buildings; the utilization of natural features such as mountains and water; the creation of large gardens; the forms and styles of residences and cultural buildings; the ingenious use of rough natural stone and raw timber; and the exquisite, sensitive curves and surfaces on buildings—all testify to this intimacy and aesthetic ability.

Sixth, the architectural style of the Nanxi River is very plain and natural, just like its raw timber and rough stone. Zhuangzi said: "With simplicity and authenticity, nothing under heaven can compete in beauty" (The Way of Heaven), and "In infinite simplicity, all beauties follow" (Deliberate Intent). Nanxi River architecture is as beautiful as those simple and honest peasants. The Nanxi River once nurtured the poetry of Xie Lingyun. Zhong Rong's "Critique of Poetry" states: "Tang Huixiu said: Xie's poetry is like a lotus emerging from water, while Yan's is like carved and colored gold, a lifelong illness." The beauty of Nanxi River architecture is also the beauty of a "lotus emerging from water"—compared to the "carved and colored gold" architecture of some regions, such as southern Anhui, it represents a higher realm of beauty. The difference in style between the two is the difference between peasant character and merchant character. This applies not only to individual buildings but also to the overall appearance of villages.

Seventh, in most Nanxi River villages, the layout and the forms and styles of their buildings show little social differentiation. The simplest and most natural folk architectural style occupies an absolutely dominant position. Most buildings are independent, maintaining their own image and personality. Except for a few ancestral halls and "Jinshi Di" (successful candidate mansion) type buildings, most houses are outward-facing and open, not defensive or exclusionary, thus creating an overall village atmosphere that is spacious, comfortable, intimate, and peaceful, teaching people to feel the open hearts of the villagers. This is very different from villages in some parts of Jiangnan formed by inward-closed courtyards, where narrow alleys are sandwiched between high enclosure walls, dark and cramped, with individual houses swallowed by continuous high walls, losing their independence and character. Life scenes are mostly enclosed within courtyards. The stern defensiveness in those narrow alleys makes outsiders feel oppressed and uneasy.

Having generally reached these understandings, we can say that the Nanxi River basin is an independent, distinctly characterized architectural cultural circle, an architectural cultural circle of high humanistic significance. Nanxi River vernacular architecture shines with special brilliance in the treasure house of China's vernacular culture.

However, "there is no help for the falling flowers." In recent years, the rural economy has boomed, and villagers have been building new houses one after another, resulting in the demolition of large amounts of vernacular architecture. The harmonious architectural environment formed over a thousand years has been destroyed, and countless witnesses of highly developed vernacular culture are rapidly disappearing. We rejoice in new construction but grieve at the destruction of cultural heritage.

Thus we resolved to set aside the work we had pursued for decades and, before Nanxi River vernacular architecture was completely lost, conduct a study of it and leave behind a record, doing our humble part for our national culture.

Studying vernacular architecture requires a broad humanistic perspective. Although collecting examples and listing them is a valid method, we hope to take the work further. On one hand, we intend to interpret vernacular architecture itself more deeply; on the other hand, we intend to interpret it in close connection with the entire natural and cultural environment.

Our vision for the work is:

First, replace the traditionally popular "folk residence research" with "vernacular architecture research." We do not agree with separating folk residences from vernacular architecture and studying them in isolation, much less ignoring all other types of vernacular architecture. The basic unit of vernacular architecture is the complete settlement, whose various building types form its internal structure. We cannot avoid studying each building type separately, but we strive to study them in relation to the settlement structure. We must also study the overall settlement system and its relationship with the external environment, including geographical, historical, cultural, and social factors. Only by taking the "life circle" as a unit and comprehensively studying all types of vernacular architecture can we clarify the connection between people's built environment and cultural environment.

Second, to study vernacular architecture holistically, one should take basic "life circles" as units rather than studying individual buildings in isolation. A life circle has relative independence, with relatively firm and stable internal connections and structure—whether kinship-based, geographical-based, religious, or occupational. It has a more or less visible core of cohesion and a more or less visible boundary. Within this life circle, vernacular architecture can roughly form its own system. Nanxi River villages formed under a self-sufficient natural economy, and a kinship village is a life circle. Therefore, in the Nanxi River, we can take kinship villages with a dominant surname as research units.

Third, we intend to study vernacular architecture as part of vernacular culture; vernacular architecture research should be a chapter of vernacular culture research. This requires breaking through the popular narrow framework of "discussing architecture for architecture's sake," expanding our horizons, opening our thinking, and broadening our exploration directions. The core of culture is lifestyle and corresponding values. Villages and houses are the environment and stage of life, and they have countless connections and exchanges with people's lives. Vernacular architecture inevitably reflects rural lifestyle patterns and corresponding values. To interpret vernacular architecture, one cannot but interpret its relationship with life, and thereby its comprehensive relationship with vernacular culture.

Fourth, the vernacular architecture system should be studied dynamically. One aspect is changes in villages and houses caused by social, economic, and cultural development; another is changes caused by developments in materials and technology. Only in a dynamic state can one clearly see the mutual influence and mutual shaping between life and the large architectural environment that serves as its stage—that is, to study the developmental process of settlements themselves. This means studying the moving history of vernacular architecture.

Fifth, comparative methods should be used. The characteristics of all things exist in comparison with other comparable things. Only through comparison can one recognize the characteristics of things. We determined that the Nanxi River is an architectural cultural circle by comparing the local building types, forms, construction methods, forms, styles, and settlement patterns with those of other regions, recognizing their distinctive features. Within a village, comparing ritual architecture with residential architecture or certain cultural architecture can reveal the different expressions and mutual influences of upper-class elegant culture and lower-class folk culture in architectural style, showing social differentiation and opposition in vernacular architecture. Comparing villages at different developmental stages can also reveal their developmental process, such as the transition from conducting business only in side rooms of homes to the formation of commercial streets. Comparing villages under various geographical conditions will also deepen our understanding.

In summary, we hope to adopt holistic, relational, dynamic, and comparative methods in the study of Nanxi River vernacular architecture.

However, conditions are very difficult. Over the past decades, the countryside has experienced repeated intense struggles and changes, to the point that no one in rural areas anymore comprehensively and systematically knows the local history, traditional rituals, and customs, and even the functions and activities of ancestral halls are not clearly understood. We cannot find old carpenters who truly understand vernacular architecture. Written materials have mostly been lost; surviving genealogies are incomplete, and their compilation level was not high to begin with, with lax formats and incomplete entries. The planning dates of most villages and the construction dates of existing houses can no longer be verified. This situation greatly limits the applicability of our methods.

本节术语 Key Terms

楠溪江 永嘉县 瓯江 括苍山脉 雁荡山脉 胡理琛 王羲之 谢灵运 文天祥 乡土建筑 生活圈 血缘村落 公共园林 礼制建筑 文化建筑 建筑文化圈 芙蓉出水 错彩镂金 苍坡村 芙蓉村 岩头村 塘湾村 茗岙村 枫林村 鹤洋村 康乐公 进士第 宗祠 文昌阁 文峰塔

第一篇 人文篇

Part One: Human Culture
一、地理环境和人文背景
中文原文重构

一、地理环境和人文背景

1.地理环境

楠溪江在浙江省东南部，是瓯江下游北侧的最后一条支流。它的干流，由北而南，曲曲折折流经145公里，在今温州市北岸注入瓯江。楠溪江东西两侧支流发达，干流和支流一起，像一棵平躺着的大树，流域面积2472平方公里。这是一个封闭的流域，独立的经济区，范围大致就是现在整个的永嘉县。虽然县治屡经搬迁，辖境也多次调整，但楠溪江流域从唐代以来，一千多年，始终是一个政区，所以它又形成了一个文化区，一个方言区。

永嘉县现在隶属温州市，县治上塘距浙江省会杭州市大约240公里。楠溪江流域是火山岩丘陵区，上游山峰多有700米以上的，中游往下，山峰多不到500米。整个流域里，有1000米高峰8座。到了下游，两岸是大片平展展的冲积平原。楠溪江汇入瓯江的地方，海拔只有4米。

楠溪江的东面是雁荡山脉，西面是括苍山脉，两山风景绮丽如画。楠溪江流域也是山川灵秀。江水在上游奔腾跌扑，形成瀑布和急湍，把丘陵切割成深谷，雕刻出耸立的危峰险崖。到了中游，它任性地左右摆动，轻轻推开两岸的山峦，河谷宽阔了一点，还串连着大大小小的盆地。沉积滩、牛轭湖、浪花闪耀的浅濑和浓绿透明的深潭，使河流变化出一幅又一幅的图画。峰峦嶂壁的奇险依然，层层叠叠，却谦逊地让澄澈的江水占尽风流，悄悄地侍立在两岸，只把黛色的影子投向水面。清初邑人陈遇春有《楠溪道中》诗：

澄碧浓蓝夹路回，崎岖迢递入岩隈；

人家隔树参差见，野径当山次第开。

乱鸟林间饶舌过，好峰天外掉头来；

莫嫌此地成萧瑟，一领茅裾去复回。

楠溪江风景之美，古代就已闻名。东汉末年和三国时代，就有一些求仙问道的人来隐居，其中有梅福。到了刘宋时期，它成了孕育中国第一代山水诗人谢灵运的摇篮之一。稍晚一点，萧梁时期的陶弘景，曾经在楠溪江的大若岩等地修炼。他在《答谢中书书》里描写这里的风光：

山川之美，古来共谈。高峰入云，清流见底，两岸石壁，五色交辉。青林翠竹，四时俱备。晓雾将歇，猿鸟乱鸣，夕日欲颓，沉鳞竞跃。实是欲界之仙都，自康乐以来未复有能与其奇者。

楠溪江流域的地形呈袋状封闭，三面环山，只有南端向瓯江敞开一个口子。介于北纬28度至28.5度之间。北面有山脉挡住冬季的寒风，1月份平均气温8.5℃，极低时-4.2℃，并不很冷。到了夏季，海风循山谷北上，带来好雨，驱走炎热，7月份平均气温29.1℃，极高时40.5℃，也并不很热。年平均气温为18.2℃。年降雨量1698毫米，四季分布不匀。夏秋之交，台风频频袭来，豪雨如注，江水猛涨。决堤的江水和倾盆的大雨常常吞没田畴庐舍，造成灾害。清初康熙年间，豫章村的外宅就被洪水冲走，片瓦不留。但台风带来的暴雨正好能解除常有的伏旱。热季恰逢雨季，温暖加上湿润，楠溪江流域宜于生长亚热带常绿阔叶林。由于地形复杂，土壤种类多，所以植被富有变化。除了山上原生林之外，河滩上长满了人工培植的树木和竹子，郁郁劲劲，成为楠溪江特有的滩林景色。盆地四周的浅山坡上，油桐、油茶、杨梅、板栗、柑橘和柿子，年年结实累累。一到秋季，散落在田野间的乌桕和枫杨，鲜红如火。流域里无霜期长达283天，宜于农业。肥壮的稻麦，从江边一直覆盖到山麓梯田，绿了又黄，黄了又绿，一年可以三熟。

《谷谱》说："温州稻岁两熟"，又引《吴都赋》"国税再熟之稻"，指称就是温州所产。其他如玉米、甘蔗和薏米，也生长得很旺盛。

农业集中在河谷的平川上，那里灌溉便利，土壤肥沃。两侧浅山宜于经济林木。县际的和乡间的道路也多缘江而走，江上又有舟楫之利。因此，富裕的村落大多聚集在楠溪江两岸的谷地里，尤其是比较宽阔的盆地里。楠溪江中游，以岩头村为中心的盆地，是全流域最大的、最富庶的盆地。这里人口稠密，村落星布，比较重要的有港头村、芙蓉村、苍坡村、枫林镇等等。稍小一点的盆地里有溪口村（古名菰田村）、桐州村、五鹈镇、碧莲镇、东皋村、花坛村（花坛）等等。在大小楠溪江的交汇处，有豫章村、坦下（坛下）村、塘湾村（棠湾、棠川）、渠口村（瞿口）等等。田头林梢，时时见到炊烟袅袅，笼罩着参差错落鳞片似的屋顶。几百座散落在楠溪江两岸的村庄，大多是古老的宗族血缘聚落，有杂姓的村子很少，倒往往是一姓数村，联宗合谱，奉祀共同的先祖。这些村子，古貌苍颜，给楠溪江渲染出一层遗世而隐的色彩，像"秦人旧舍"，"不知有汉，无论魏晋"。

这些宁静得近乎停滞的村落，却洋溢着浓郁的人文气息。原来楠溪江的外侧是会稽、婺州和台州。东晋和南宋，中原衣冠两次南渡，文化中心都离楠溪江不远。除了谢灵运、陶弘景曾在楠溪江上徘徊吟诵，王子猷雪夜访戴的剡溪，李清照轻舟填词的双溪，都只和楠溪江隔一道分水岭。那种当时载不动许多国恨家愁的舴艋舟，到现在还在楠溪江上，张着白帆，来来往往。但在楠溪江，它们载不动的，是两岸村落的文化史。

2.从上古到六朝

楠溪江下游的文化史开始得很早，有几处新石器时代的文化遗址，但后来的发展很缓慢。《逸周书·五会解》："正东有沤深，越沤剪发文身。"（王应麟补注：沤深即瓯也，沤亦瓯也）《史记·赵世家》记载："夫剪发文身，错臂左衽，欧（按：即瓯）越之民也。"可见直到春秋战国，如今的温州、台州一带的"瓯越文化"还很原始，而且与中原文化不属于同一个体系。楠溪江下游的文化，当时就是瓯越文化的一部分。西汉初年，有东瓯国（前192-前138年），在楠溪江口西侧建造了"东瓯王城"。后来在东瓯王领地设回浦县，属会稽郡，一度又改为东瓯乡，隶章安县。章安县县治远在现在的黄岩县章安乡，当时人口之稀少可以想见。东汉时在瓯江北岸建永宁县（138年），仍属会稽郡，三国吴时改属临海郡。虽然传说西汉甘露年间傅隐遥和三国时期王玄贞两位道士曾经在小楠溪的大若岩修炼，道教传入楠溪江中、上游很早（见乾隆《大若岩记》），但直到西晋末年，中、上游的人口大约仍旧很少。

西晋末年，中原大乱，却给东南沿海地区的发展带来了转机。南渡的中原士族，用先进的文化改造了瓯越文化，使它统一于中原的正统文化。楠溪江也开始了新的历史。

东晋明帝太宁元年（323年），设立了永嘉郡，辖地包括今温州和丽水部分地区。从此楠溪江隶属于永嘉。同年，大学问家郭璞为郡城在瓯江南岸选定了地址，规划并且筑城（今温州市鹿城区），显然那时候人口增加，经济有了长足的进步。

中原衣冠渡江偏安，一时人文荟萃江右。初建的永嘉郡，在六朝时竟得了中国文化史上几颗灿烂夺目的星辰来任郡守：先后有东晋大文学家、大书法家王羲之，注《三国志》的刘宋史学家裴松之，刘宋玄言诗人、赋家孙绰，诗人、骈文家、文论家颜延之，中国第一位山水诗人谢灵运和萧梁文学家、骈文高手丘迟。这些当时中国文化的最高代表，"助人伦、成教化"，对永嘉的历史发生了深远的影响。

按照儒家理想，"善政不如善教之得民也。善政，民畏之；善教，民爱之。善政，得民财；善教，得民心"（《孟子·尽心上》）。所以，从东汉以来，地方长官为"师"重于为"吏"，已经成为一个传统。那几位先后守永嘉郡的文人名士，未必服膺儒家，但不能逸出这个传统。乾隆《永嘉县志》转引《旧志》说："晋立郡城，生齿日繁，王右军导之以文教，谢康乐继之，乃知向方。自是家为学，至宋遂称小邹鲁。"又说："王羲之治尚慈惠，谢灵运招士讲学，由是人知向学，民风一变。"

政教之余，谢灵运徜徉流连于山水之间，写下了中国第一批真正的山水诗。其中至少有六首写的是楠溪江风景，而且多在中上游。楠溪江对山水之进入中国文化史起过重要的作用。杜甫《送裴虬尉永嘉》有句"隐吏逢梅福，看山忆谢公"。北宋林逋《送僧机素还东嘉》则说："康乐遗踪地，言归已有期"。永嘉与谢康乐永远连在一起了。

王谢风流，氤氲起楠溪江浓厚的人文气息。明末清初，邑人梅调元有《王谢祠》诗：

前守推王谢，荒祠倚郡城。千秋传墨妙，六代擅诗名。

沼古鹅还浴，塘春草自生。风流今古事，俯仰一含情。

丘迟在《永嘉郡教》里说，永嘉"控山带海，利兼水陆，东南之沃壤，一都之巨会"，则萧梁时永嘉已经比较繁荣。不过，楠溪江流域，从东汉到六朝的古窑址仍然集中在下游，推断起来，中、上游的开发程度大约还很低。

3.从隋唐到宋

隋唐时期，人口继续向楠溪江中上游推进，所以在这地区造了几座佛教寺院，例如岩头村北五鹈溪畔长蛇坑的普安寺，初建于唐玄宗先天壬子（712年）。

隋文帝开皇九年（589年）废永嘉郡，更永宁县为永嘉县。隋炀帝大业元年（605年）又复永嘉郡。以后几经变动，唐太宗贞观元年（627年）再建永嘉县，唐高宗上元元年（674年）设温州，治永嘉。从此经五代、宋、元、明至清一千多年没有大的变动，直到如今。

向中、上游开发的人，大多来自外地，为避世乱而寻到这片三面环山的桃源。乾隆《永嘉县志·疆域》引旧《浙江通志》说："楠溪太平险要，扼绝江，绕郡城，东与海会，斗山错立，寇不能入。"环境比较安全，对外地人有吸引力。例如中游芙蓉峰下的下园村，据《下园瞿氏宗谱》记载："晚唐时，黄巢乱，宁波刺史瞿靖避乱来此，鉴于天险奇峰，旷洞清幽，乃定居。"五代末季，各地战乱频繁，独有钱氏吴越境内比较安定。永嘉之南，相去不远的闽国，国王延钧、王延政于933年和943年先后称帝，父子兄弟交相攻杀，大批闽人为避乱北迁属于吴越的永嘉，在楠溪江中游建立了许多村落。苍坡、芙蓉、溪口、枫林、花坛、廊下、岩头等中游最重要的几个村子，就是闽人建立的，而且都来自长溪（即福宁州）。这些移民，大多出自仕宦人家，有过功名，凭借他们的文化优势，后人都成了楠溪江的望族，楠溪江人文之盛，主要以这几个村子为代表。

经过这样长期的发展，到了北宋，温州的经济已经相当发达。北宋时候的温州太守杨蟠有诗咏温州："一片繁华海上头，从来唤作小杭州。"（见乾隆《永嘉县志·风俗》）而杭州这时已是江南繁华胜地，柳永在《望海潮》词中说："东南形胜，江吴都会，钱塘自古繁华。烟柳画桥，风帘翠幕，参差十万人家。"被称为小杭州的温州，当然也颇有可观。到了南宋，温州的农业、手工业和商业飞快进步，一度成为海上商港。

宋代是中国封建文化最辉煌的时期。造成这个文化高潮的原因大致是：士族地主完全消失，庶族地主代之而兴，科举取仕的重要性也大大增加；文官队伍扩大，科举取仕的数量也大大增加。在这样的情况下改革了科举制度，基本杜绝了夤缘奔竞，使平民子弟攀升的机会多了。同时，印刷术发展，书籍普及，也促进了普通人读书博取功名的可能性。因此，温饱的小康之家，大多奔上了科举的道路，从而普遍提高了社会的文化水平。宋朝政府并不采取文化专制政策，学术思想比较自由，学术风气也比较活泼，因而繁荣了上层雅言文化，它的代表就是理学。

楠溪江最灿烂的文化高峰在南宋。宋室偏安江东，又一次大规模的衣冠南渡，把先进的中原文化带到东南沿海。像东晋一样，偏安之地人文荟萃，南宋时来守温州的，如张九成（1042-1159年）、王十朋（1112-1171年）、楼钥（1137-1213年）和杨简（1141-1225年）等人，都是一时的俊彦。他们，以及北宋的温州郡守胡则都很尊重永嘉的人文传统，风流犹存。他们作为儒家正统雅言文化的代表，在任地方官吏的时候，也以礼乐教化为第一要务。对于提高永嘉的文化水平起了很大作用。

乾隆《永嘉县志·学校》说："永嘉于宋，名贤辈出，登洛闽之堂者，后先相望，郁郁彬彬，至称为小邹鲁，何其盛哉！"从唐至清，永嘉一共有过604位进士，其中宋代513位，又其中南宋464位。可以确实考订为楠溪江人的，至少有50多位。南宋咸淳元年乙丑（1265年）榜进士中，永嘉籍的有36位之多，占全数的11.4%。其中有芙蓉村的抗元英雄陈虞之和蓬溪村的李时靖，至少这二位是楠溪江人。溪口村戴氏、豫章村胡氏、塘湾村郑氏、花坛村朱氏、苍坡村李氏、芙蓉村陈氏等，都是"簪缨鹊起、甲第蝉联"的名门望族。苍山碧水之间，田父野老最引为乡土光荣的，是宋朝豫章村胡氏的一门三代五进士、溪口村戴氏的一门四代六进士、花坛村朱氏和塘湾村郑氏的兄弟进士。

相传芙蓉村在南宋有过"十八金带"，就是有十八位高级京官。

永嘉在北宋有程门弟子十三人，在南宋有朱门弟子十六人。最值得楠溪江人骄傲的，是溪口村的戴述、戴蒙、戴溪、戴侗和塘湾村的郑伯熊、郑伯英、郑伯海，都是当时重要的理学家，著作丰富，史籍有传。

4.元、明、清三朝

楠溪江在南宋这种繁花似锦的盛况，经宋末元兵的大烧杀后不再出现。不过，如《县志》所说："遗风余韵，元明间犹时有闻"，豫章村胡氏、鹤垟村谢氏、岩头村金氏、花坛村朱氏、溪口村戴氏等，在明代还有一些科举功名的成就，出了中书舍人胡宗韫，锦衣卫指挥佥事谢廷循这样比较重要的人物。溪口村戴氏大宗祠有一副楹联是：

宋室尚书第；明廷御史家。

花坛村朱氏大宗祠有一副楹联：

宋室衣冠皂盖朱幡推秀；明廷阀阅黄门乌府联芳。

明代前半叶，温州还有曾有过两位很有影响的郡守，一位是何文渊，一位是文林。二位都是有著作传世的文士，以"化民成俗"为己任。文林制定《族范》，在温属各县推广，力求强化家族伦理。

嘉靖之后，倭寇侵扰海疆，温州的经济文化大受打击。嘉靖《永嘉县志·城池·都里》中说楠溪江"宋时人物颇盛，今故家遗族，间有存者，视往昔殊矣！"甚至说小楠溪流域"地旷民贫"，为逋逃之薮。

明清易代之际，楠溪江又一次遭到严重破坏。《茗川胡氏大宗祠谱》中写于康熙二年（1663年）的《重建致爱堂记》说，清代初年，由于官兵剿乱，"室庐资产，烧荡一空，而祠宇株连，闾阎萧条……垂二十年，鸿雁犹悲鸣于旷野。"《豫章胡氏宗谱》康熙三十四年（1695年）《旧序》说："至清鼎革，变起沧桑，兵燹之余，世家右族不无徙迁，集泽哀鸣，未能常聚。"民力凋惫，赋役不供，文化也就衰败了。南宋时期，一榜进士中不下数十人的永嘉，在整个清代将近三百年间，只有十一名进士，其中还有一名是恩榜。好在康乐公的后裔，鹤垟村谢氏，出了两名进士。《重修鹤垟谢氏宗谱序》（道光三十年，1850年）夸耀这件事说："至今犹想见谢家之门第，即为溪山之生色者，历久弗衰矣！"

楠溪江的历史尽管有荣衰兴替，唐宋以还，村落里的深层生活，毕竟是稳定的日子居多。乡民们在宗法制度之下，有耕有读，几百年间，恒常不变。这种社会生活，和它所伴生的心态，在《岩头金氏宗谱·重修族谱叙》（康熙）里描绘得很清楚：

安福公墓三岩之胜，家于岩头。贤哲代生，规模宏远。如英六、升四诸公，开渠筑堤以备旱潦，创谱牒、建宗祠、置祀田以资盥荐，置义田以裕读书。嗣是人文丕振，簪缨继起。……彼汉武玉堂非不贵也，石崇金谷非不富也，吴宫花草、晋代衣冠非不芬芳而赫奕也，然人往风微，徒深感悼，孰若兹之聚族而居，老尔宅，田尔田，涧溪如故，塘堑依然，庙社常新，松楸无恙，士习民风，数百载如一日也乎。

楠溪江的村落，就是这样的社会生活的舞台，显现出宗族关系的强固，生产劳动的艰辛，耕读生活的宁静，以及由此而生的乡愿式自满自足的保守心态。

5.生产经济

关于楠溪江的经济史，几乎没有资料。不过，从仅有的零星资料可见，长期以来，都以自给自足的农业经济为主，工商业受到轻视。《蓬溪谢氏宗谱·赠耕隐公序》里说正德年间的谢朗：

读书尚义士也，怀经济策，寓意于农，遂以耕隐自号。……古之贤士，多出为农，以其业安亩亩，恬静朴茂，非若工与商贾，突骛喧嚣，俯仰尘市，逐十一、较锱铢而不得以宁居也。……故勋业弗成，不如归耕。

这位耕隐公其实是"筑室创业，富望一乡"的人，《序》里写的其实是一种传统的价值观。

农作物首先是稻米、玉米、甘薯、芋艿，然后是络麻、蚕桑。茶叶和柑橘早在唐代就是贡品，柑橘尤其闻名。商品作物也早已有了，如甘蔗、薏米、花生等等。商品中的一大宗是山货，主要的有木材、竹、桐油、笋和板栗等等，还有一些果品和木炭，大多运销温州，这时永嘉县治就在郡城。

大约到了清代中叶，商品经济有了相当规模。光绪《永嘉县志·建置》说，楠溪江"旧用茅竹编筏以通往来，同治三年（1864年），陈令宝善，准民改用梭船，至今称便"，这显然是货运量增大的关系。不过，宋人潘希白《入楠溪》诗里已经写到篷舟，可能同治三年（1864年）前后的变化在于数量的增加。以后航运日益发达，"船过瓯江楠溪开，江上千帆相牵连"，这是《楠溪船歌》里唱的词句。

运输繁忙，有了专业的船老大。《楠溪地名谣》里提到古庙口村和下岸村是船户村，"一篙一桨拼老命，一年到头不停休"。也有了以造船为专业的渔村。

初级的商品经济，刺激楠溪江沿岸发展了手工业，而且也产生了相应的专业村。例如西岸村出粉干，黄岗村出缸钵，等等。与建筑有关的工匠也有了专业村，如蓬溪村。

商品经济的发展，促进了贫富的分化。明代晚期，高利贷已在楠溪江的村落里出现。《棠川郑氏宗谱》载塘湾村郑世益："精于理财，祖、父无多遗业，翁乃勤俭自持，权其子母，量出入，铢积寸累，握算持筹，不数年而粟陈贯朽，富甲一邑。"

English Translation

I. Geographical Environment and Humanistic Background

1. Geographical Environment

The Nanxi River is in southeastern Zhejiang Province and is the last tributary on the northern side of the lower Ou River. Its main stream flows from north to south, meandering for 145 kilometers, and empties into the Ou River on the north bank of present-day Wenzhou City. The Nanxi River has developed tributaries on both east and west sides, and together with the main stream they resemble a tree lying flat, with a drainage area of 2,472 square kilometers. This is an enclosed basin, an independent economic zone, roughly corresponding to present-day Yongjia County. Although the county seat has been relocated repeatedly and its jurisdiction adjusted many times, the Nanxi River basin has remained an administrative unit for over a thousand years since the Tang dynasty, thus forming a cultural region and a dialect region.

Yongjia County now belongs to Wenzhou City, and its county seat, Shangtang, is about 240 kilometers from Hangzhou, the provincial capital. The Nanxi River basin is a volcanic rock hilly area. In the upper reaches, most peaks exceed 700 meters, while downstream, peaks are mostly under 500 meters. Within the entire basin, there are 8 peaks over 1,000 meters. At the lower reaches, both banks are vast alluvial plains. Where the Nanxi River joins the Ou River, the elevation is only 4 meters.

To the east of the Nanxi River is the Yandang Mountain range, and to the west is the Kuocang Mountain range, both with picturesque scenery. The Nanxi River basin is also endowed with beautiful mountains and rivers. In the upper reaches, the river rushes and cascades, forming waterfalls and rapids, cutting hills into deep valleys and carving out towering perilous peaks and cliffs. In the middle reaches, it swings left and right willfully, gently pushing aside the mountains on both banks, and the river valley widens somewhat, strung with basins of various sizes. Depositional beaches, oxbow lakes, sparkling shallows, and deep green transparent pools make the river change into painting after painting. The precipitous peaks and cliffs remain, layer upon layer, yet humbly yield to the clear river, standing quietly on both banks, casting their dark shadows onto the water. In the early Qing dynasty, local scholar Chen Yuchun wrote a poem "On the Road in Nanxi":

Clear blue and deep green line the winding road, rugged and remote into the mountain recesses;

Houses appear intermittently through trees, wild paths open one after another against the mountains.

Chattering birds pass through the woods, fine peaks turn around from beyond the sky;

Do not think this place desolate, a straw cloak goes and returns.

The beauty of Nanxi River scenery has been known since ancient times. In the late Eastern Han and Three Kingdoms periods, people seeking immortality came to live in seclusion, including Mei Fu. During the Liu Song period, it became one of the cradles that nurtured China's first generation of landscape poet Xie Lingyun. Slightly later, Tao Hongjing of the Liang dynasty once cultivated himself at Daluo Rock and other places in the Nanxi River. In his "Letter in Reply to Secretary Xie," he described the scenery here:

The beauty of mountains and rivers has been discussed since ancient times. Peaks soar into clouds, clear streams reveal the bottom, stone walls on both banks shine with five colors. Green forests and bamboo groves are present in all four seasons. When morning mists are about to dissipate, apes and birds chatter chaotically; when the sun is about to set, diving fish leap. Truly this is a fairyland of the mortal world; since Duke Kangle (Xie Lingyun), no one has again been able to appreciate its wonders.

The terrain of the Nanxi River basin is enclosed like a bag, surrounded by mountains on three sides, with only the southern end opening toward the Ou River. It lies between 28 and 28.5 degrees north latitude. Mountains to the north block winter cold winds, with an average January temperature of 8.5°C and an extreme low of -4.2°C, not very cold. In summer, sea breezes blow up the valleys, bringing good rain and driving away heat, with an average July temperature of 29.1°C and an extreme high of 40.5°C, not very hot. The annual average temperature is 18.2°C. Annual rainfall is 1,698 millimeters, unevenly distributed across seasons. At the junction of summer and autumn, typhoons frequently strike with torrential rain, causing the river to rise sharply. Breached riverbanks and pouring rain often flood fields and houses, causing disasters. During the early Kangxi period of the Qing dynasty, the outer settlement of Yuzhang Village was washed away by floods, leaving not a single tile. However, typhoon rains正好 relieve the usual summer drought. The warm season coincides with the rainy season, and warmth plus moisture make the Nanxi River basin suitable for subtropical evergreen broadleaf forests. Due to complex terrain and diverse soil types, vegetation is rich and varied. Besides primary forests on mountains, river beaches are covered with artificially planted trees and bamboo, lush and vigorous, forming the distinctive beach forest scenery of the Nanxi River. On the low hills around basins, tung oil trees, tea-oil camellias, bayberries, chestnuts, tangerines, and persimmons bear abundant fruit year after year. In autumn, Chinese tallow trees and Chinese wingnuts scattered in the fields turn bright red like fire. The frost-free period in the basin is as long as 283 days, suitable for agriculture. Lush rice and wheat cover from the riverbanks to the mountain terraces, turning green then yellow, yellow then green, with three harvests possible in a year.

The "Grain Manual" says: "Wenzhou rice has two harvests a year," and quotes the "Rhapsody on Wu Capital" that "the state tax collects twice-harvested rice," referring specifically to Wenzhou's produce. Other crops such as corn, sugarcane, and Job's tears also grow vigorously.

Agriculture is concentrated in the river valley plains, where irrigation is convenient and soil fertile. The low hills on both sides are suitable for economic forests. Inter-county and inter-village roads mostly follow the river, and the river also provides boat transportation. Therefore, prosperous villages are mostly gathered in the valleys along both banks of the Nanxi River, especially in the wider basins. In the middle reaches of the Nanxi River, the basin centered on Yantou Village is the largest and wealthiest in the entire basin. Here the population is dense and villages are scattered like stars, with relatively important ones including Gangtou Village, Furong Village, Cangpo Village, and Fenglin Town. In slightly smaller basins there are Xikou Village (ancient name Gutian Village), Tongzhou Village, Wuti Town, Bilian Town, Donggao Village, Huatan Village, etc. At the confluence of the Greater and Lesser Nanxi Rivers, there are Yuzhang Village, Tanxia Village, Tangwan Village, Qukou Village, etc. At the edges of fields and among treetops, wisps of cooking smoke are constantly seen, shrouding the staggered, scale-like tiled roofs. Hundreds of villages scattered along both banks of the Nanxi River are mostly ancient clan-based kinship settlements; villages with mixed surnames are rare, and often one surname occupies several villages, united in genealogy and worshipping common ancestors. These villages, with their ancient appearance, cast a layer of reclusive color over the Nanxi River, like "the old homes of the people of Qin," who "knew not of the Han dynasty, let alone the Wei and Jin."

These villages, tranquil to the point of stagnation, nevertheless brim with rich humanistic atmosphere. It turns out that outside the Nanxi River are Kuaiji, Wuzhou, and Taizhou. During the Eastern Jin and Southern Song, the Central Plains aristocracy migrated south twice, and cultural centers were all not far from the Nanxi River. Besides Xie Lingyun and Tao Hongjing who wandered and recited poetry along the Nanxi River, the Shanxi Creek where Wang Ziyou visited Dai Kui on a snowy night, and the Shuangxi where Li Qingzhao filled ci poems on a light boat, are all separated from the Nanxi River only by a watershed. Those small boats that "could not carry the weight of so much national grief and family sorrow" at that time still sail on the Nanxi River today, their white sails coming and going. But on the Nanxi River, what they cannot carry is the cultural history of the villages on both banks.

2. From Antiquity to the Six Dynasties

The cultural history of the lower Nanxi River began very early, with several Neolithic cultural sites, but subsequent development was slow. "Yizhoushu · Wuhui Chapter": "Due east is Oushen, the Yue Ou cut their hair and tattoo their bodies." (Wang Yinglin's supplementary note: Oushen is Ou, and Ou is also Ou) "Records of the Grand Historian · Hereditary House of Zhao" records: "Those who cut their hair and tattoo their bodies, with their arms crossed and left lapels, are the people of Ou (i.e., Ou) Yue." This shows that until the Spring and Autumn and Warring States periods, the "Ou Yue culture" in present-day Wenzhou and Taizhou was still very primitive and did not belong to the same system as Central Plains culture. The culture of the lower Nanxi River at that time was part of Ou Yue culture. In the early Western Han, there was the Eastern Ou Kingdom (192-138 BCE), which built the "Eastern Ou Royal City" on the west side of the Nanxi River mouth. Later, Huipu County was established in the Eastern Ou King's territory, belonging to Kuaiji Commandery, and was once changed to Eastern Ou Township, under Zhang'an County. The Zhang'an County seat was far away in present-day Zhang'an Township, Huangyan County, and the sparse population at that time can be imagined. In the Eastern Han, Yongning County was established on the north bank of the Ou River (138 CE), still belonging to Kuaiji Commandery, and was changed to Linhai Commandery during the Three Kingdoms Wu period. Although legend has it that Daoists Fu Yinyao in the Western Han Ganlu period and Wang Xuanzhen in the Three Kingdoms period once cultivated themselves at Daluo Rock in the Lesser Nanxi River, and Daoism was introduced to the middle and upper Nanxi River very early (see Qianlong's "Record of Daluo Rock"), until the end of the Western Jin, the population in the middle and upper reaches was probably still very small.

At the end of the Western Jin, chaos in the Central Plains brought a turning point for the development of the southeastern coastal region. The migrating Central Plains aristocracy transformed Ou Yue culture with advanced culture, unifying it into the orthodox Central Plains culture. The Nanxi River also began a new history.

In the first year of the Taining reign of Emperor Ming of the Eastern Jin (323 CE), Yongjia Commandery was established, with jurisdiction including present-day Wenzhou and parts of Lishui. From then on, the Nanxi River belonged to Yongjia. In the same year, the great scholar Guo Pu selected the site for the commandery city on the south bank of the Ou River, planned and built the city (present-day Lucheng District, Wenzhou), evidently indicating population growth and considerable economic progress by that time.

The Central Plains aristocracy crossed the river and settled in the south, gathering talents on the right bank of the Yangtze. The newly established Yongjia Commandery, during the Six Dynasties, actually attracted several of the most brilliant stars in Chinese cultural history as commandery governors: successively the great Eastern Jin writer and calligrapher Wang Xizhi, the Liu Song historian Pei Songzhi who annotated "Records of the Three Kingdoms," the Liu Song metaphysical poet and rhapsodist Sun Chuo, the poet, parallel prose writer, and literary theorist Yan Yanzhi, China's first landscape poet Xie Lingyun, and the Liang dynasty writer and parallel prose master Qiu Chi. These highest representatives of Chinese culture at that time, "assisting human relations and completing moral education," had a profound influence on Yongjia's history.

According to Confucian ideals, "Good government is not as good as good education in winning the people. Good government makes the people fear it; good education makes the people love it. Good government obtains the people's wealth; good education obtains the people's hearts" ("Mencius · Jinxin Shang"). Therefore, since the Eastern Han, local officials being "teachers" rather than "bureaucrats" had become a tradition. those famous scholars who successively governed Yongjia Commandery may not have all been devoted Confucians, but could not escape this tradition. Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle" quotes the "Old Chronicle": "When the commandery city was established in the Jin, the population increased daily; Wang Youjun (Wang Xizhi) guided them with culture and education, and Xie Kangle (Xie Lingyun) succeeded him, so they knew the right direction. From then on every family pursued learning, and by the Song it was called a little Zou and Lu." It also says: "Wang Xizhi governed with kindness and benevolence; Xie Lingyun recruited scholars and lectured, hence people knew to pursue learning, and the folk customs changed."

Beyond government and education, Xie Lingyun wandered among mountains and rivers, writing China's first truly landscape poems. At least six of them describe Nanxi River scenery, mostly in the middle and upper reaches. The Nanxi River played an important role in landscape entering Chinese cultural history. Du Fu wrote in "Sending Pei Qiu to Serve as County Defender in Yongjia": "The recluse official meets Mei Fu, viewing mountains recalls Duke Xie." Northern Song poet Lin Bu wrote in "Sending Monk Jisu Back to Dongjia": "The land of Duke Kangle's traces, returning already has a date." Yongjia and Xie Kangle are forever linked.

The elegance of Wang and Xie permeated the Nanxi River with a rich humanistic atmosphere. In the late Ming and early Qing, local scholar Mei Diaoyuan wrote a poem "Wang-Xie Shrine":

Former governors Wang and Xie, a desolate shrine leans by the commandery city. Through a thousand years their calligraphy endures, through six dynasties their poetic fame spreads.

In the ancient pond geese still bathe, in spring pond grass grows by itself. Elegance through past and present, a moment of emotion.

Qiu Chi wrote in "Instruction to Yongjia Commandery" that Yongjia "controls mountains and borders the sea, benefiting from both land and water, fertile land of the southeast, a great metropolis of the region," indicating that Yongjia was already relatively prosperous during the Liang dynasty. However, ancient kiln sites from the Eastern Han to the Six Dynasties in the Nanxi River basin are still concentrated in the lower reaches, suggesting that the development level of the middle and upper reaches was probably still very low.

3. From Sui-Tang to Song

During the Sui and Tang dynasties, the population continued to advance into the middle and upper reaches of the Nanxi River, so several Buddhist temples were built in this area, such as Pu'an Temple at Changshekeng beside Wuti Stream north of Yantou Village, first built in the Renzi year of Emperor Xuanzong of Tang's Xiantian reign (712 CE).

In the ninth year of Emperor Wen of Sui's Kaihuang reign (589 CE), Yongjia Commandery was abolished, and Yongning County was renamed Yongjia County. In the first year of Emperor Yang of Sui's Daye reign (605 CE), Yongjia Commandery was restored. After several changes, in the first year of Emperor Taizong of Tang's Zhenguan reign (627 CE), Yongjia County was reestablished, and in the first year of Emperor Gaozong of Tang's Shangyuan reign (674 CE), Wenzhou was established, with Yongjia as its seat. From then on, through the Five Dynasties, Song, Yuan, Ming, and Qing, over a thousand years, there were no major changes, until today.

Those who developed the middle and upper reaches mostly came from other places, seeking this three-sided mountain-enclosed paradise to escape worldly chaos. Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Territory" quotes the old "Zhejiang General Chronicle": "Nanxi is peaceful and strategically important, controlling the river, surrounding the commandery city, east meeting the sea, with mountains interlocked, bandits cannot enter." The relatively safe environment was attractive to outsiders. For example, Xiayuan Village below Furong Peak in the middle reaches, according to the "Xiayuan Qu Clan Genealogy": "In the late Tang, Huang Chao rebelled; Qu Jing, prefect of Ningbo, escaped the chaos here, seeing the natural danger and strange peaks, broad caves and serenity, then settled." At the end of the Five Dynasties, wars were frequent everywhere, but only the Wuyue kingdom under the Qian family was relatively stable. South of Yongjia, not far away, was the Min kingdom, where kings Wang Yanjun and Wang Yanzheng declared themselves emperors in 933 and 943 respectively, with fathers, sons, and brothers attacking each other; large numbers of Min people migrated north to Yongjia, which belonged to Wuyue, to escape chaos, establishing many villages in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River. Cangpo, Furong, Xikou, Fenglin, Huatan, Langxia, Yantou, and other most important villages in the middle reaches were established by Min people, all from Changxi (i.e., Funing Prefecture). These migrants mostly came from official families with scholarly credentials, and凭借 their cultural advantages, their descendants became the prominent clans of the Nanxi River. The humanistic prosperity of the Nanxi River is mainly represented by these villages.

After such long-term development, by the Northern Song, Wenzhou's economy was already quite developed. The Northern Song Wenzhou governor Yang Pan wrote a poem praising Wenzhou: "A stretch of prosperity on the sea, always called little Hangzhou." (See Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Customs") At that time Hangzhou was already a prosperous place south of the Yangtze; Liu Yong wrote in his ci poem "Looking at the Sea Tide": "A strategic spot in the southeast, a metropolis of Wu and Yue, Qiantang has been prosperous since ancient times. Misty willows and painted bridges, wind curtains and emerald curtains, scattered with a hundred thousand households." Wenzhou, called little Hangzhou, was of course quite impressive. By the Southern Song, Wenzhou's agriculture, handicrafts, and commerce advanced rapidly, once becoming a maritime trade port.

The Song dynasty was the most brilliant period of Chinese feudal culture. The reasons for this cultural climax were roughly: the complete disappearance of aristocratic landlords, replaced by commoner landlords, and the greatly increased importance of the imperial examination for official careers; the expansion of the civil service, and the greatly increased number of examination graduates. Under these circumstances, the examination system was reformed, basically eliminating favoritism and intrigue, giving commoner children more opportunities to rise. At the same time, the development of printing and the spread of books also promoted the possibility for ordinary people to study for official success. Therefore, comfortable middle-class families mostly embarked on the examination path, thereby generally raising the cultural level of society. The Song government did not pursue cultural autocracy, academic thought was relatively free, and the academic atmosphere was lively, thus flourishing upper-class elegant culture, represented by Neo-Confucianism (lixue).

The most brilliant cultural peak of the Nanxi River was in the Southern Song. With the Song court settling in the south, there was another large-scale migration of the Central Plains aristocracy, bringing advanced Central Plains culture to the southeast coast. Like the Eastern Jin, the remote region gathered talents; those who came to govern Wenzhou in the Southern Song, such as Zhang Jiucheng (1042-1159), Wang Shipeng (1112-1171), Lou Yue (1137-1213), and Yang Jian (1141-1225), were all outstanding talents of their time. They, as well as Northern Song Wenzhou governor Hu Ze, all respected the humanistic tradition of Yongjia, and the elegance endured. As representatives of Confucian orthodox elegant culture, when serving as local officials, they also made ritual and music education their first priority. This played a great role in raising Yongjia's cultural level.

Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Schools" says: "Yongjia in the Song produced famous scholars one after another, those who entered the halls of Luo and Min (Neo-Confucian schools) followed one another, cultured and refined, even called a little Zou and Lu—how prosperous!" From Tang to Qing, Yongjia produced a total of 604 jinshi (palace examination graduates), of whom 513 were from the Song, and 464 of those from the Southern Song. At least 50 can be confirmed as Nanxi River people. In the jinshi list of the Yichou year of the first year of Emperor Duzong of Song's Xianchun reign (1265), there were as many as 36 from Yongjia, accounting for 11.4% of the total. Among them were the anti-Yuan hero Chen Yuzhi of Furong Village and Li Shijing of Pengxi Village—at least these two were Nanxi River people. The Dai clan of Xikou Village, Hu clan of Yuzhang Village, Zheng clan of Tangwan Village, Zhu clan of Huatan Village, Li clan of Cangpo Village, Chen clan of Furong Village, etc., were all prominent clans with "successive official ranks and consecutive examination successes." Between green mountains and blue waters, what village elders and farmers were most proud of as local glory were the Song dynasty Hu clan of Yuzhang Village with three generations and five jinshi, the Dai clan of Xikou Village with four generations and six jinshi, and the brother jinshi of the Zhu clan of Huatan Village and Zheng clan of Tangwan Village.

It is said that Furong Village in the Southern Song had "eighteen gold belts," meaning eighteen high-ranking capital officials.

Yongjia had thirteen disciples of the Cheng school in the Northern Song and sixteen disciples of the Zhu school in the Southern Song. What Nanxi River people are most proud of are Dai Shu, Dai Meng, Dai Xi, Dai Tong of Xikou Village, and Zheng Boxiong, Zheng Boying, Zheng Bohai of Tangwan Village—all important Neo-Confucian scholars of their time, with rich works and biographies in historical records.

4. Yuan, Ming, and Qing Dynasties

The flourishing prosperity of the Nanxi River in the Southern Song no longer appeared after the great destruction by Yuan troops at the end of the Song. However, as the "County Chronicle" says: "Remaining elegance and echoes were still occasionally heard in the Yuan and Ming," the Hu clan of Yuzhang Village, Xie clan of Heyang Village, Jin clan of Yantou Village, Zhu clan of Huatan Village, Dai clan of Xikou Village, etc., still had some examination successes in the Ming, producing important figures such as Hu Zongyun, a secretary in the Central Secretariat, and Xie Tingxun, an assistant commissioner of the Embroidered Uniform Guard. The Dai clan's ancestral hall in Xikou Village has a couplet:

Song dynasty secretariat residence; Ming dynasty censor family.

The Zhu clan's ancestral hall in Huatan Village has a couplet:

Song dynasty official robes and red canopies stand out; Ming dynasty prominent families and yellow gates and black offices share glory.

In the first half of the Ming, Wenzhou had two very influential governors, He Wenyuan and Wen Lin. Both were scholars with works passed down, taking "transforming the people and forming customs" as their mission. Wen Lin formulated "Clan Norms," promoted them in all counties under Wenzhou, striving to strengthen clan ethics.

After the Jiajing period, Japanese pirates harassed the coastal areas, dealing a heavy blow to Wenzhou's economy and culture. Jiajing's "Yongjia County Chronicle · City and Walls · Townships" says of the Nanxi River: "In the Song, prominent people were abundant; now old families and surviving clans occasionally remain, far different from the past!" It even says the Lesser Nanxi River basin is "sparsely populated and poor," a haven for fugitives.

At the Ming-Qing transition, the Nanxi River was once again severely damaged. The "Mingchuan Hu Clan Ancestral Hall Genealogy" contains a "Record of Rebuilding Zhi'ai Hall" written in the second year of Kangxi (1663), saying that in the early Qing, due to government troops suppressing chaos, "houses and assets were burned and swept away, ancestral halls were implicated, neighborhoods were desolate... for nearly twenty years, wild geese still悲鸣 in the wilderness." The "Yuzhang Hu Clan Genealogy" preface of the thirty-fourth year of Kangxi (1695) says: "At the Qing dynasty change, times transformed, after the ravages of war, prominent families all migrated, gathering and lamenting, unable to always assemble." The people's strength was exhausted, taxes and corvee could not be supplied, and culture declined. Yongjia, which in the Southern Song had dozens of jinshi in one examination list, produced only eleven jinshi in nearly three hundred years of the entire Qing dynasty, including one conferred by imperial favor. Fortunately, the descendants of Duke Kangle, the Xie clan of Heyang Village, produced two jinshi. The "Preface to Rebuilding the Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy" (the thirtieth year of Daoguang, 1850) boasted of this: "To this day one can still imagine the Xie family's status, which adds luster to the streams and mountains, enduring without decline!"

Despite the rises and falls in Nanxi River history, since the Tang and Song, the deep life in the villages has after all been mostly stable days. Villagers, under the clan system, farmed and studied, remaining constant for hundreds of years. This social life and the accompanying mindset are clearly depicted in the "Preface to Rebuilding the Genealogy" in the "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy" (Kangxi period):

Duke Anfu is buried at the scenic Three Rocks, with his family at Yantou. Sages were born generation after generation, with grand scale. Like Yingliu, Shengsi and other gentlemen, they opened canals and built dikes to prepare for drought and flood, created genealogies, built ancestral halls, established sacrificial fields for worship, and established charity fields to support education. Hence culture flourished and official ranks followed. ... The Han Wudi's Jade Hall was not unnoble, Shi Chong's Golden Valley was not unwealthy, Wu palace flowers and Jin dynasty official robes were not unfragrant and glorious, but when people passed and elegance faded, only deep sorrow remains—how can that compare to living together as a clan, old houses, own fields, streams as before, ponds and moats unchanged, ancestral temples ever new, pines and oaks unharmed, scholar habits and folk customs, the same for hundreds of years!

The villages of the Nanxi River are the stages of such social life, revealing the strength of clan relations, the hardship of productive labor, the tranquility of farming-studying life, and the resulting complacent and conservative mindset.

5. Production Economy

There is almost no information about the economic history of the Nanxi River. However, from the scant available materials, it can be seen that for a long time, the economy was dominated by self-sufficient agriculture, with industry and commerce looked down upon. The "Preface to Gengyin Lord" in the "Pengxi Xie Clan Genealogy" says of Xie Lang during the Zhengde period:

A scholar who valued righteousness, with economic strategies, found meaning in agriculture, thus calling himself Gengyin (Farming-Recluse). ... Ancient wise scholars mostly became farmers, because this occupation is peaceful in the fields, tranquil and simple, unlike craftsmen and merchants who rush about noisily, bowing in the marketplace, chasing one-tenth profit, haggling over pennies without peaceful dwelling. ... Therefore if merit cannot be achieved, it is better to return to farming.

This Gengyin Lord was actually someone who "built a house and established a business, wealthy and renowned in the township," and what the preface writes is actually a traditional value system.

Crops were first and foremost rice, corn, sweet potatoes, taro, then fiber crops, mulberry and silkworm. Tea and tangerines were already tribute items as early as the Tang dynasty, with tangerines especially famous. Cash crops also existed early, such as sugarcane, Job's tears, peanuts, etc. A major category of goods was mountain products, mainly timber, bamboo, tung oil, bamboo shoots, and chestnuts, as well as some fruits and charcoal, mostly transported and sold in Wenzhou, where the Yongjia county seat was then located.

By the mid-Qing, the commodity economy had reached considerable scale. Guangxu's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Establishments" says that the Nanxi River "formerly used bamboo rafts for transportation; in the third year of Tongzhi (1864), Magistrate Chen Baoshan approved the people's use of pointed-bottom boats, which remain convenient to this day," evidently due to increased freight volume. However, Song poet Pan Xibai's poem "Entering Nanxi" already mentions covered boats, so the change around the third year of Tongzhi (1864) may have been in quantity. Later shipping became increasingly developed, "boats pass the Ou River and Nanxi opens, a thousand sails connect on the river," as sung in the "Nanxi Boat Song."

With busy transportation, there were professional boat captains. The "Nanxi Place Name Ballad" mentions Gumiaokou Village and Xia'an Village as boat-owner villages: "With one pole and one oar they risk their lives, never resting all year round." There were also fishing villages specializing in boat building.

The初级 commodity economy stimulated the development of handicrafts along the Nanxi River, and also produced corresponding specialized villages. For example, Xi'an Village produced rice noodles, Huanggang Village produced earthen bowls, etc. Craftsmen related to construction also had specialized villages, such as Pengxi Village.

The development of the commodity economy promoted the polarization of wealth and poverty. In the late Ming, usury already appeared in Nanxi River villages. The "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy" records Zheng Shiyi of Tangwan Village: "Skilled at finance, with little inheritance from grandfather and father, he maintained himself through diligence and frugality, weighing principal and interest, measuring income and expenditure, accumulating penny by penny, calculating with abacus, within a few years grain stored and coins strung, wealthiest in the county.""

本节术语 Key Terms

楠溪江 永嘉县 瓯江 括苍山脉 雁荡山脉 大若岩 陶弘景 谢灵运 王羲之 郭璞 东瓯国 瓯越文化 永嘉郡 温州 闽人移民 科举 进士 理学 戴溪 戴侗 郑伯熊 陈虞之 倭寇 耕读生活 宗族制度 自给自足 商品经济 高利贷 专业村 苍坡村
二、耕读生活与山水情怀
中文原文重构

二、耕读生活与山水情怀

1.耕读生活

耕读生活，早期作为士人的一种理想，起源于隐逸，起源于他们儒家的"退则独善其身"和道家的返回自然相结合的人格结构。但到了宋朝，它被科举制度改造并且大大加强了。

科举制度本为遴选官吏而设，它的推行提高了社会普遍的文化水平。宋代扩大了科举录取名额，改善了考试方法，再加上雕版印刷盛行，从而激发了普通人家对科举的兴趣，造成了文化的普及。连农家子弟也看到了眼前可能的机会，于是牛角挂书，参加到举业的竞逐中去，这就形成了农村中新的耕读生活。

（1）科举与文化普及

宋仁宗有几条政策进一步促进了耕读生活的理想。一是规定士子必须在本乡读书应试，因而地方普设各级学校；一是各科进士榜名，给南方诸省规定了优惠的最低配额。更重要的是，工商业者和他们的子弟不得参与科举考试，而只许士、农参加。农家子弟科名机会大大增加，"朝为田舍郎，暮登天子堂"，由春梦成了希望，茅檐下响起了琅琅的书声。

楠溪江流域，耕读理想深入到山陬水涯的每个村落。各个宗族，在《家训》、《族规》里明定，子弟务必要读书。《鹤垟谢氏宗谱·家训》里说，子孙应该"以耕读为业"；《坦下陈氏宗谱·家训》（康熙本）教导："耕以务本，读以明教。"《云岭潘氏宗谱·家训》说得更周全明白："祖宗家法，以忠孝节义为纪纲，以耕读勤俭为本务。"

为子弟读书，各村纷纷兴学。一方面请老师主持义学或义塾，一方面资助贫寒人家子弟入学。例如《岩头金氏宗谱·家规》写道："每岁延敦厚博学之士以教子弟，须重以学俸，隆以礼文，无失故家轨度。子弟有质士堪上进而无力从学者众，当资以祠租曲成之。"各族的祠下大多规定，凡进县学、府学读书和赴府、省应试，费用由祠下公出。中试以后祭祖和各方打点、花销也由宗祠支付，族中有公有的学田，收入全用来兴学。

子弟取得科举功名，是整个宗族的光荣，一律载入宗谱。有功名的人在家族的各种仪典里都享受特殊的光荣。祠堂里的匾额和楹联，除了颂扬先人辉煌的功业之外，就是炫耀族人的举业和仕途。豫章村胡氏大宗祠有一副楹联写的是：

翰墨流芳百世衣冠开砚沼；

诗书继美千秋裡撰焕文章。

《珍川朱氏合族副谱》里有一篇《如在堂记》，把对子弟科名的期望写得很殷切：

使我拥书万卷，何减积粟千锺，然则后之子若孙，苟不忘此意，必将奋志诗书，骧首云逵，上以绳其祖武，下以贻厥孙谋，无忝先世科甲之荣，不振前朝理学之绪，则不惟有光于近祖，亦且善述乎大宗祠矣！

这种耕读理想成了楠溪江人的传统。偏僻闭塞的楠溪江，小小的村落里，文风之盛，科甲成就之辉煌，在全国的乡村里极为少见。道光三十年（1850年）《重修鹤垟谢氏宗谱序》里说"诗书继美，比户可封；游庠之士，指不胜屈"，并不是浮夸的虚言。

文风之兴，由于科举，但文风兴起之后，必定超越科举。《珍川朱氏宗谱·族范盟辞》里说得明白：

不学则夷乎物，学则可以立，故学不亦大乎。学者尽人事所以助乎天也。天设其伦，非学莫能敦；人有恒纪，非学莫能叙。贤者由学以明，不贤者废学以昏。大匠成室，材木盈前，程度去取而不乱者，由绳墨之素定。君子临事而不骇，制度而不扰者，非学安能定其心哉。是故学者君子之绳墨也。

对文化的这种深刻的理解和热烈的追求，给楠溪江深山幽谷里的村庄笼罩上一层浓浓的书卷气。

（2）理学家

文化水平的普遍提高，扩大了知识分子阶层，也就扩大了宋、明以还理学的社会基础，增强了它的影响。

王十朋《送叶秀才序》里说：永嘉"谊礼之学甲于东南，笔横渠、口伊洛者纷如也。取科第、登仕籍，多自此途出。"永嘉人直接求学于程、朱门下的也不少。在国学里，先后有永嘉籍的元丰九先生和淳熙六君子，"俱以道德性命传程朱之学"（见乾隆《永嘉县志·风俗》）。在南宋，还形成了理学的"永嘉学派"，以薛季宣、郑伯熊、陈傅良和叶适为代表。郑伯熊是楠溪江中游塘湾村人。又据《岩头金氏宗谱》记载，叶适童年由岩头村金姓抚养，在岩头村读书，他和楠溪江有亲密的关系。

除郑伯熊和叶适外，楠溪江文士中还有不少理学名家。溪口村戴氏家族，北宋时有戴述（元符三年进士，即1100年）、戴迅二兄弟，从二程学，世称二戴。到南宋，有戴述子栩（嘉定进士），是叶适的学生，著作《五经说》、《诸子辩论》、《东都要略》等；戴迅之子溪（淳熙五年进士，即1178年），"由礼部郎中凡六转为太子詹事，兼秘书监。献太子命类《易》、《诗》、《书》、《春秋》、《语》、《孟》、《资治通鉴》各为说以进。权工部尚书，除文华阁学士，卒赠端明殿学士，谥文端。太子亲书'明经'扁其堂，有《岷隐集》。"（见嘉靖《温州府志·人物》）溪弟龟年之子蒙（绍熙庚戌进士，即1190年），曾从朱晦庵于武夷，著作有《易、书、四书家说》、《六书故》等；蒙长子仔，不乐仕进，著有《诸经补义》、《通鉴外纪》、《说林文集》等；蒙之次子侗（淳祐辛丑进士，即1241年），著作有《六书故》及其他杂著文集。戴氏大宗祠里有一副楹联写道：

入程朱门迭奏埙篪理学渊源双接绪；

历南北宋并称邹鲁春官第甲六登墀。

夸耀的就是戴氏家族的这份荣誉。

塘湾村郑伯熊三兄弟都是南宋进士。伯熊"德行夙成，尤邃经学。登绍兴第，历官国子司业、宗正少卿。乞外，以龙图阁知宁国府。卒谥文肃。绍兴末，伊洛之学稍息，复于伯熊倡之。弟伯英、伯海皆知名，由是永嘉之学宗郑氏。有《郑景望集》"（嘉靖《温州府志·人物》）。伯英是隆兴癸未（1163年）进士，有《归愚集》。伯海是绍兴辛未（1151年）进士，设帐授徒，生徒常达五百人。

此外，岙底上湾村有陈揆，绍兴癸丑（1133年）与陈亮同榜进士，与叶适相善，有诗集二十馀卷及文集五卷问世。芙蓉村陈宝之，绍兴进士，从吕祖谦学，与陈亮为诗友。宗谱有《送陈同父》和《挽吕东莱》诗各一首。

乾隆《永嘉县志》记载，朱熹在任两浙东路常平盐茶公事的时候，曾经到楠溪江来访问当地的理学家。先到岩头村访问门人"以理学鸣于世"的刘愈，说："过楠溪不识刘进之，如过洞庭不识橘。"不巧没有见到。又到谢岙访门人谢复经，再访戴蒙、戴侗及蓬溪村李时靖，在蓬溪村留有墨迹（摩崖石刻及门头石匾，真伪待证）。

楠溪江地处荒僻，却与当时的主流文化保持着这样密切的关系，达到这样高的水平，这在全国乡村中也是少见的。

虽说宋亡之后楠溪江文运大衰，但元明之间鹤垟村还出了个谢德璵，"博通经史，为时名儒"，所著有《狂斐集》、《家礼会通》等。又如同时期花坛村朱谧，著有《四书述义》、《四书辅注》、《太极图说》、《西铭集》等等，从祀郡庠。

理学盛行，加强了楠溪江的宗族制度和封建的伦理道德观念，也提高了乡人的素质。

（3）乡贤士绅

耕读生活培养出来的乡村知识分子，科场得意的是少数，成为理学家的更是寥寥。他们中的大多数，留在村子里，作为上层雅言文化的代表，与郡县官吏一起，在乡村推行教化。这些人里，有的隐居读书，过着比较丰富的精神文化生活，甚至富有著作，有的掌握宗族大权。在楠溪江，这些人为数不少，对涵养楠溪江村落的文化气息作用很大。

《鹤垟谢氏宗谱·义学条规》里说：

义学之设，原为国家树人至计，非以为后生习浮艳，取青紫已也。凡系生徒，务须以白鹿洞规身体力行，……凡肄业弟子，必须一举足疾徐，一语言进止，事事雍容审详，安雅冲和。

学校对子弟的操守品行提出很严格的要求，读书人成了农村敦品励行的榜样。他们的文化生活和精神追求往往被理想化而记载在宗谱里，世代传诵。例如，《棠川郑氏宗谱》里记载着乡绅郑公谔，"旷达多才，好稽古，善词赋。筑美室，置图书，列古画玩物以供清赏。读诵之暇，惟以弹琴栽花为乐，遇风日晴和，则汲泉煮茗，拂席开樽，与二三知己，啸傲于烟霞泉石间，不复知有人世荣辱事。且课子有程，义方之外，更以诗书陶镕其气质"。花坛村朱谧，"读书好古，淳朴自持，利欲不能移其心，荣禄不足夺其志。孝以事亲，友以处弟"（见《明义堂处士墓志铭》）。宗谱和墓志有夸饰，但从中可见价值观的取向。

有些乡绅直接担任教育工作。宋末元初鹤垟村的谢梦符，"博学经史，推重当时，宋咸淳中为郡庠学宾，继升经谕。丰仪整肃，衣冠严雅，为缙绅表率。时称为宿儒长者。有《问樵》等集行世"（《鹤垟谢氏宗谱》）。明代嘉靖时岩头村的金九峰，"性闲静，每日闲坐一室，凡经史以及诸子百家书无不娴，而且究心于星学，陶情音律。至于制艺歌词，皆其余事耳。……设绛帐以诲生徒，春风四座，化雨一庭，洵是师儒领袖"（《九峰先生五旬寿序》）。这些人对传布儒家伦理，维系传统秩序，是很自觉的。他们对乡村事务很有实际的影响。

不过，乡村知识分子，也是上层文化的代表，他们的人格结构是双重的。按照儒家的理想，他们进则廊庙，退则山林，当他们处于山林之间的时候，淡泊恬适，精神间又饱含着道家返回自然的思想。《珍川朱氏宗谱》有一篇《廊下即景诗序》，说到是乡"秀士成群，多含英咀华之彦，古怀如晤，有庄襟老带之风，可谓文质彬彬，野处多秀者已"。

正是正统的儒学和"庄襟老带之风"的结合，造就了楠溪江人文的特色和双重性格。折射到建筑上，也同样表现出上层文化和民俗文化的矛盾共存。而返回自然和恬淡冲和，则涵养得楠溪江建筑特别地亲切有人情味。

2.山水情怀

楠溪江风光秀丽。楠溪江又是小邹鲁。秀丽的风光，与文化中热爱自然之美的传统一起，哺育了楠溪江人的山水情怀。每当丝雨迷濛、烟云舒卷的春季，遍青山开满了杜鹃。山红初谢，又是洁白的桐花烂漫。到了秋深，乌桕树浓艳如血。澄澈的江水，随着时序，映照各种鲜明的颜色。深深受到传统文化熏染的楠溪江文士们，对自然之美很敏感，一种清醒的环境审美意识，在楠溪江人的心中氤氲，并且转化为村落的魅力。它们与美的自然融为一体，还引用自然来装饰点缀，房舍的风格又最朴实。自然是楠溪江乡土建筑的魅力所在。

（1）江山有幸

江山多娇，江山也有幸。公元422年，刘宋永初三年，谢灵运来到永嘉郡任太守。他陶醉于永嘉的山水，"肆意游遨"，写出了中国第一批山水诗。谢灵运守永嘉不到一年，却在楠溪江留下了不少足迹。斤竹涧、白岸、石室涧、绿嶂山，他都题过诗。

乾隆《永嘉县志·舆地》说："往者谢康乐为郡，好游名山，由是此郡山水闻于天下。天下之士行过是邦者亦莫不俯仰留连，吟咏不辍，以诧其胜。"在这些俯仰流连的人里，据乾隆《永嘉县志·名胜志》载，有萧梁的陶弘景。他的名诗《答齐高帝诏》就写于绿嶂山。诗道：

山中何所有？岭上多白云；

只可自怡悦，不堪持寄君。

所以后来就有地名白云岭。乾隆《大若岩记》又说他在小楠溪的大若岩修炼，写了重要的道教典籍《真诰》和《本草集注》、《补阙肘后百一方》两部医书。他修炼的地方叫陶公洞，附近也有一座白云岭。不远的水云村里还造了一座白云亭。传说千古名篇《答谢中书书》描写的就是楠溪江风光。

唐代诗人孟浩然、罗隐和崔道融到过永嘉。白居易到过楠溪江，在大若岩捐钱开道，筑了两处堤防（见《大若岩记》）。宋代到过永嘉的有陆游，明代则有李东阳。清初学者朱彝尊抗清失败后来到楠溪江，曾住在廊下村。《曝书亭集》里的《永嘉杂咏》，有几首是在廊下村写的。如《华坛望雁荡山歌赠方十三朱生、朱十八振嘉》：

登华坛之绝顶，眺雁岩之回峦。云容容兮欲雨，水嘈嘈兮下山。遥岭出没不可胜数，但见哀禽离兽日暝而俱还。

自康乐公启端，历代诗人们对楠溪江山水"吟咏不辍"。苏轼又钦佩又自信地写诗道："自言长官如灵运，能使江山似永嘉。"永嘉的江山真是幸运。

生活在锦绣河山之中，心田里受着千余年文化的滋润，楠溪江的乡村文人们，对山川草木的美非常敏感也非常热爱。以耕读自娱的读书人，"寄志林泉"，"或临流而歌啸，或倚石而垂钓"，以他们的整个生活和诗文表现对大自然的感情。花坛村的朱伯清，明代人，"丰神秀逸，嗜学有文，不乐仕进，志存林壑。……家事付诸子，惟以文墨自娱。凡石、树、虫、鱼、水泉、花药之会心寓目者，咸属吟咏其间。遇风和日暖，角巾鹿裘，从以弟子，徘徊乎水光山色。拂云坐石，手挥丝桐，目送飞鸿，逍遥自乐"（《珍川朱氏宗谱·伯清公珍川十咏序》）。所以乾隆《永嘉县志》说，楠溪江"山峰挺秀，涧水呈奇，人生其地者，皆惠中而秀外，温文而尔雅"。

奇秀的山水涵养出楠溪江乡民独有的气质和人文的特色。

这种气质，化而为楠溪江建筑的风格。他们的村落、房舍和园林都朴实无华，然而都舒展开朗。他们善于利用天然材料的本形、本性、本色，使建筑与天地和谐。家家使用不加錾凿的蛮石墙，弧形放足，粗犷有力，矗立在大块卵石铺就的地面上，整个村落仿佛从荒古时代与山岩同时生成。他们也爱用天然原木，不加斤斧，随弯就曲，巧妙地把它们安装在恰当的位置上，它们就好像应该有那样的弯曲和粗糙。带着生命原有的形状，它们与庭前的树木和一切有生命的相呼应。

从上到下，建筑绝少雕琢，木石最大限度地保存着天然本色；少量的加工，出自人类双手天然的能力。这就孕成了楠溪江建筑给人的亲切感。

建筑物的形式也是很自然的。宜廊则廊，宜堂则堂，轻巧的披檐，自在地遮挡着一切应该遮挡的地方。翘曲飘洒的屋顶，像搏击长空的鹰翼那样灵动。

楠溪江人的环境审美意识，也表现在村落的选址、规划、绿化、园林、构图等等上面，使人为居住环境与美丽的山川构成统一的景观。

（2）意在山水之间

开发楠溪江的先人们，没有例外，都把村落建在风景优美的地方。谢灵运的后裔本来定居在郡城，有一次："选五五公游楠溪，见鹤垟之胜，又自郡城迁居鹤垟。"（《重修鹤垟谢氏宗谱序》）其他各村的宗谱里，也都记载着类似的选址故事。如塘湾村，始迁祖"爱楠溪山水之胜"，而来落户；渠口村始迁祖也是"爱其山水之胜，遂家焉"，等等。

豫章村在小楠溪南岸，进村之前，先要穿密林，过荒江野渡，再穿密林。村后如刻如削的峰峦，层层叠叠，向两侧涌伸展。山脚下平畴如带，竹树掩映中，参差百十户人家。江水在村左村右萦回成宽阔的碧潭，隔江正对一座青翠的狮子山。《豫章胡氏宗谱·旧序》（康熙三十四年，1695年）说：

永嘉山水，秀丽无如楠源大小二岩，巉岩耸挺，空兀崆峒，历有仙灵凭居托迹。下此而称名胜，莫如豫章。文峰现沼锺其奇，玉笏横头著其异。其间降岳发祥，代多伟烈，居其下者，则有胡氏。……宗族殷蕃，子孙秀蔚……宗支派衍，霞蒸林郁。

珍溪上游的廊下村，在一条山沟里，四周山形奇突。溪水从东北来，掠过村北、村西，由西南流向花坛村。《珍川朱氏合族副谱·廊下即景诗序》里说：

山连雁荡，入径已觉清幽；地肖龙头，过岭方知奥旷。水环如带，可救游鳞；峰列为屏，时度飞鸟。桑麻蔽其野阡，枫授藏乎盈山。仿佛乎桃源之幽隐，盘谷之窈深焉。

楠溪江两岸古村落的风光大多如此，傍山就水，充分展现环境的美。

（3）保护、点缀、增益

楠溪江人，以他们的文化素养感受环境的美，细腻鲜明。《坦下陈氏宗谱》载康熙时人阶侯先生五言律，描绘坦下村的风景：

团团一派石，绿竹间青松。沙外溶溶水，门前叠叠峰。

山花开屿岸，野鸟唤春风。锄犁能读史，振飞鹏。

经过文人们揣摩品评，楠溪江许多村落都有"十景"、"八景"之类，宗谱里也多有《名胜》专篇。谢道宁《鹤垟八景诗》之一《锦嶂春晖》：

万仞屏环仰照临，阳和随见破群阴。岩花呈秀分高下，野树浮光间浅深。

适兴岂无人蹑屐，寻幽还有客抚琴。西郊只隔疏林外，多少红尘乱扑襟。

诗把风光的美和一种自然的生活态度融合在一起。通过追慕先祖康乐公，又把这种生活态度和千年的文化传统联系在一起。

经这些在乡文士倡导，楠溪江人很重视保护自然环境，包括树木。村村有风水林，不许砍伐。村头村尾，古树浓荫蔽天。《棠川郑氏宗谱·新宫坳樟树记》载，新宫坳里有太阴宫，"宫右侧有樟木一株，其大可丈围，其高难尺计。……葱茏在望，经雨露而弥新；新秀可餐，阅风霜而不改。斯固刍荛之所栖息，抑亦竖牧之婆娑也。然蔽烈日，御罡风，位置得宜，其又有关于风水乎？"但是，竟有一些见利忘义之徒，企图砍伐这棵树。"于是村中知事者不敢袖手以旁观，斟酌再三，集款买归老宗祠之业，立有字据，永后并不许砍断。"

流连，吟咏，保护，都不足以充分表达楠溪江人的山水情怀，他们进而要为山川生色。大小村落，往往都有台榭亭阁之类的风景小品建筑物。记载最早的是北宋进士、宝章阁待制陈师在蓬下村，"筑一笑、拨云二亭于富岩以观瀑"（见《两源陈氏大宗祠谱》）。比较著名的则有谢公后裔的鹤垟村里几座小品建筑。宗谱载，元代至正年间，谢毅孙"襟怀潇洒，雅爱宾客。……善音乐，喜与人吟咏，……与西席陈先生宾义治，相与筑台于东山之上，植兰种竹，取康乐公遗言镌其岩曰兰玉台。师生经暇，憩息其间，以消长夜。朋友倡和，成《兰玉台集》"。到了明代中叶，任职锦衣卫的谢廷循"创楼于鹤溪之西，因楼傍清流，故名曰'临清'，以为宴宾吟咏之所"，同时在"村北水溪"造了一座"临流亭"。廷循绘临流亭图呈明宣宗，宣宗竟题了一首诗：

临流亭馆净无尘，落涧泉声处处闻。半湿半干花上露，飞来飞去岭头云。

翠迷洞口竹千个，白占林梢鹤一群。此地清幽人不到，惟留风月与平分。

这座亭子因此也叫"宝翰亭"。

对山水点缀增益，仍然不能满足文士们的一往情深，他们动手创建。楠溪江的"隐士"大多喜欢"锄园种花，凿池开圃"，布置私家小园。《乐安珍川朱氏宗谱》载廊下村朱映峰作《隐居歌》道：

非士亦非农，半耕还半读。傍山数顷田，临水几间屋。筑园又凿池，栽花复种竹。……花自吐清香，竹亦言芳郁，池水漾芰荷，园蔬借耆苜。

明代中叶，花坛村的朱逊，"家颇饶，经营堂构，馀址凿池开圃，植花养鱼，以为宗戚朋旧壶觞吟咏笑傲处"。约略同时，同村朱圃轩有《石假山》诗一首，首联是"谁欤挺秀若天然，叠翠层峦景万千"（均见《珍川朱氏宗谱》），可见当时山村造园已经有叠假山技艺。岩头村的上花园，苍坡村的水月堂，芙蓉村芙蓉书院山长住宅和"大宅"都有小园，至今古木参天，花墙和假山残迹依稀可见。

山水情怀，造就了楠溪江村落最大的特色之一，大型的公共园林。岩头、苍坡、溪口、渠口、埭头、珠岸、西岸、鹤盛等村的公共园林大体还保存着。埭头村、鹤盛村和鹤垟村的公园在山坡上，西岸村的在江边，古木森森，远远就能望见。岩头村的公园，纯藉人力，内容最丰富、布局最多变化，是明代嘉靖年间兴造的。它包含一座小小的汤山，一片镇南湖、一片进宦湖，和它们间的琴屿半岛，还有一条300米长的丽水湖。两个湖都由人工堤坝拦蓄溪水形成，堤上古木数株，绿荫下一座精巧玲珑的小亭，汤山顶上有塔，山麓有楼阁和庙。湖中荷花盛开，岛上芙蓉灿烂如霞。

公园是村民们公共活动场所，尤其是乡村文人们的。岩头村的《十景诗》描绘了他们在公园里的活动。《长堤春晓》诗句有："结伴连朝频载酒，行吟不惜绕长堤"；《丽桥观荷》诗有句："坐对嫣然如解语，乘风散步纳凉时"；《清沼观鱼》诗有句："绍堤花柳可行歌，笑看游鳞跳锦波"；《曲流环碧》诗有句："不知把钓垂竿子，坐对渔矶乐若何？"这些诗里，乡村文士们的生活和意趣宛然可见，也可见他们如痴如醉的山水情怀。

这些乡村文士在公园里过着高品位的文化生活，必然会把他们深湛的文化修养带到公园的一草一木中去。所以这些园林和它们的建筑的风格都很轻逸淡雅。诗情画意，不脱乡土本色。

贯穿在楠溪江中游村落建设中的山水情怀和生活文化，是楠溪江中游乡土建筑不朽的魅力所在。

English Translation

II. Farming-Studying Life and Landscape Sentiment

1. Farming-Studying Life

The farming-studying life, early as an ideal of scholars, originated from seclusion, from the personality structure combining the Confucian "in retirement, cultivate oneself" and the Daoist return to nature. But by the Song dynasty, it was transformed and greatly strengthened by the imperial examination system.

The imperial examination system was originally established to select officials, and its implementation raised the general cultural level of society. The Song dynasty expanded the number of examination graduates, improved examination methods, and with the prevalence of woodblock printing, stimulated ordinary families' interest in the examinations, causing cultural popularization. Even farmer's children saw possible opportunities before them, so they hung books from ox horns and joined the competition for official careers, forming a new farming-studying life in the countryside.

(1) Imperial Examinations and Cultural Popularization

Emperor Renzong of Song had several policies that further promoted the ideal of farming-studying life. One required scholars to take examinations in their hometowns, leading to the establishment of schools at all levels locally; another set favorable minimum quotas for southern provinces in the jinshi examination lists. More importantly, merchants and craftsmen and their children were prohibited from taking the examinations, only scholars and farmers were allowed. Farmer's children's opportunities for examination success greatly increased; "in the morning a farmer's boy, in the evening a palace official" turned from a spring dream into hope, and the sound of reading aloud rang out under thatched eaves.

In the Nanxi River basin, the farming-studying ideal penetrated every village in remote mountains and river valleys. Each clan explicitly stipulated in Family Instructions and Clan Rules that children must study. The "Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy · Family Instructions" says descendants should "make farming and studying their occupation"; the "Tanxia Chen Clan Genealogy · Family Instructions" (Kangxi edition) teaches: "Farming serves the fundamental, studying illuminates the teachings." The "Yunling Pan Clan Genealogy · Family Instructions" puts it more comprehensively: "Ancestral family law takes loyalty, filial piety, chastity, and righteousness as the framework, and farming, studying, diligence, and frugality as the fundamental tasks."

For children's education, villages enthusiastically established schools. On one hand, they hired teachers to run charity schools or charity academies; on the other hand, they subsidized poor families' children to attend school. For example, the "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy · Family Rules" writes: "Each year employ honest and learned scholars to teach children, must pay generous salaries, treat them with courtesy and ceremony, not losing the standards of old families. Children with talent capable of advancement but lacking means to study should be supported with clan rents to complete their education." Most clans stipulated that expenses for attending county or prefecture schools and taking prefecture or provincial examinations were paid from clan funds. After passing examinations, costs for ancestor worship and various gifts and expenses were also paid by the ancestral hall. Clans had public school fields, whose income was entirely used for education.

Children obtaining examination success was the glory of the entire clan, recorded in the genealogy. Those with examination honors enjoyed special glory in all clan ceremonies. The plaques and couplets in ancestral halls, besides extolling ancestors' brilliant achievements, boasted clan members' examination success and official careers. The Hu clan's ancestral hall in Yuzhang Village has a couplet:

Ink and calligraphy spread fragrance for a hundred generations, official robes open the inkstone pond;

Poetry and books continue beauty for a thousand autumns, writings illuminate literature.

The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy" contains a "Record of Ruzai Hall" expressing earnest expectations for children's examination success:

If I possess ten thousand books, how is that less than storing a thousand bushels of grain? Then my descendants, if they do not forget this intention, will surely strive in poetry and books, rise to the clouds, above continuing their ancestors' footsteps, below leaving plans for their grandchildren, not disgracing the glory of former generations' examination success, reviving the Neo-Confucian tradition of the previous dynasty—then not only will they bring glory to recent ancestors, but also well continue the great ancestral hall!

This farming-studying ideal became the tradition of Nanxi River people. In the remote and enclosed Nanxi River, in small villages, the flourishing literary culture and brilliant examination achievements were extremely rare in rural areas nationwide. The "Preface to Rebuilding the Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy" (the thirtieth year of Daoguang, 1850) says "poetry and books continue beauty, every household could be enfeoffed; students in county schools are too numerous to count," not an exaggerated boast.

The rise of literary culture came from the examinations, but once it arose, it必定 transcended them. The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy · Clan Norm Oath" puts it clearly:

Without learning, one becomes like a thing; with learning, one can stand—so is learning not great? The learner does human duty to assist heaven. Heaven established human relations, without learning none can cultivate them; people have constant order, without learning none can arrange them. The wise become clear through learning, the unwise become dull through abandoning learning. A great craftsman building a house, with timber filling the space, measures and selects without confusion because the ink line was set beforehand. A gentleman facing affairs without alarm, establishing institutions without disturbance—without learning how could his heart be settled? Therefore learning is the gentleman's ink line.

This profound understanding and passionate pursuit of culture enveloped the villages in the deep mountains and secluded valleys of the Nanxi River with a thick atmosphere of books.

(2) Neo-Confucian Scholars

The general improvement in cultural level expanded the intellectual class, thereby expanding the social basis of Neo-Confucianism (lixue) from the Song and Ming dynasties and enhancing its influence.

Wang Shipeng wrote in "Preface to Sending Scholar Ye": Yongjia's "learning of righteousness and ritual ranks first in the southeast, those who write like Hengqu (Zhang Zai) and speak like Yiluo (Cheng Hao and Cheng Yi) are numerous. Obtaining examination degrees and entering official service mostly comes from this path." Many Yongjia people directly studied under the Cheng and Zhu schools. In the National Academy, there were successively the "Nine Gentlemen of Yuanfeng" and "Six Gentlemen of Chunxi" from Yongjia, "all transmitting the learning of Cheng and Zhu through moral nature and destiny" (see Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Customs"). In the Southern Song, the "Yongjia School" of Neo-Confucianism was formed, represented by Xue Jixuan, Zheng Boxiong, Chen Fuliang, and Ye Shi. Zheng Boxiong was from Tangwan Village in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River. According to the "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy," Ye Shi was raised in childhood by the Jin clan of Yantou Village and studied there, having an intimate relationship with the Nanxi River.

Besides Zheng Boxiong and Ye Shi, there were many other Neo-Confucian scholars among Nanxi River literati. The Dai clan of Xikou Village: in the Northern Song, Dai Shu (jinshi of the third year of Yuanfu, 1100) and Dai Xun were brothers who studied under the Cheng brothers, known as the "Two Dais." In the Southern Song, Dai Shu's son Dai Xu (jinshi of Jiading) was a student of Ye Shi, authoring "Five Classics Discussions," "Debates of Various Thinkers," "Eastern Capital Essentials," etc.; Dai Xun's son Dai Xi (jinshi of the fifth year of Chunxi, 1178), "from secretary in the Ministry of Rites promoted six times to Crown Prince's Supervisor, concurrently Secretary of the Imperial Library. Presented to the Crown Prince classified discussions of the Changes, Poetry, History, Spring and Autumn, Analects, Mencius, and Comprehensive Mirror for Aid in Government. Served as acting Minister of Works, appointed as Wenhua Pavilion Scholar, posthumously granted Duanming Palace Scholar, posthumous name Wenduan. The Crown Prince personally wrote 'Understanding Classics' for his hall, and he has the 'Minyin Collection'." (See Jiajing's "Wenzhou Prefecture Chronicle · Figures") Dai Xi's younger brother Guinian's son Dai Meng (jinshi of the Gengxu year of Shaoxi, 1190) once studied under Zhu Xi at Wuyi, authoring "Family Discussions of Changes, History, and Four Books," "Six Books Origins," etc.; Dai Meng's eldest son Dai Zai, not seeking official service, authored "Supplementary Meanings of Various Classics," "Outer Records of the Comprehensive Mirror," "Shuolin Collected Works," etc.; Dai Meng's second son Dai Tong (jinshi of the Xinchou year of Chunyou, 1241), authored "Six Books Origins" and other miscellaneous works. The Dai clan's ancestral hall has a couplet:

Entering the Cheng-Zhu school, playing the xun and chi (ancient instruments), Neo-Confucian origins doubly connected;

Across Northern and Southern Song, called Zou and Lu, spring official ranks six times ascending.

This boasts the Dai clan's honor.

The three Zheng brothers of Tangwan Village were all Southern Song jinshi. Zheng Boxiong "had established virtue, especially profound in classical learning. Obtained the Shaoxi degree, served as Director of the National Academy, Vice Minister of the Imperial Clan. Requested external post, served as Longtu阁 Scholar and Governor of Ningguo Prefecture. Posthumous name Wensu. At the end of Shaoxing, the Yiluo (Cheng) learning slightly declined, and Boxiong revived it. His younger brothers Boying and Bohai were both renowned, hence Yongjia learning followed the Zheng clan. He has the 'Zheng Jingwang Collection'" (Jiajing's "Wenzhou Prefecture Chronicle · Figures"). Zheng Boying was jinshi of the Guiwei year of Longxing (1163), with the "Guiyu Collection." Zheng Bohai was jinshi of the Xinwei year of Shaoxing (1151), established a teaching tent, with students often reaching five hundred.

Additionally, Chen Kui of Aodi Shangwan Village was jinshi of the Guichou year of Shaoxing (1133), same list as Chen Liang, friendly with Ye Shi, with over twenty volumes of poetry and five volumes of prose published. Chen Baozhi of Furong Village, Shaoxing jinshi, studied under Zu Qian, was a poetry friend of Chen Liang. The genealogy contains poems "Sending Chen Tongfu" and "Mourning Lü Donglai" each.

Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle" records that when Zhu Xi served as Commissioner of Salt and Tea for the Two Zhe East Road, he once came to the Nanxi River to visit local Neo-Confucian scholars. He first visited Liu Yu, a disciple "renowned for Neo-Confucian learning" in Yantou Village, saying: "Passing Nanxi without knowing Liu Jinzhi is like passing Dongting without knowing oranges." Unfortunately he did not meet him. He then visited disciple Xie Fujing at Xie'ao, then visited Dai Meng, Dai Tong, and Li Shijing of Pengxi Village, leaving calligraphy in Pengxi Village (cliff inscriptions and door stone plaques, authenticity pending verification).

The Nanxi River, though remote, maintained such close relations with the mainstream culture of the time and reached such a high level—rare in rural areas nationwide.

Although literary fortune greatly declined after the Song fall, between Yuan and Ming, Heyang Village produced Xie Deyu, "broadly versed in classics and history, a renowned Confucian of the time," authoring "Kuangfei Collection," "Comprehensive Family Rituals," etc. Similarly, Zhu Mi of Huatan Village in the same period authored "Four Books Expounded Meanings," "Four Books Supplementary Annotations," "Taiji Diagram Explanation," "Ximing Collection," etc., and was worshipped in the prefecture school.

The prevalence of Neo-Confucianism strengthened the clan system and feudal ethical and moral concepts in the Nanxi River, and also improved the quality of villagers.

(3) Local Worthies and Gentry

Among rural intellectuals cultivated by the farming-studying life, those who succeeded in examinations were few, and those who became Neo-Confucian scholars even fewer. The majority remained in villages, as representatives of upper-class elegant culture, together with county and prefecture officials promoting moral education in the countryside. Among these, some lived in seclusion reading, enjoying relatively rich spiritual and cultural life, even authoring works; some held clan power. In the Nanxi River, these people were numerous and greatly influenced the cultural atmosphere of Nanxi River villages.

The "Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy · Charity School Regulations" says:

The establishment of charity schools was originally for the nation's great plan of nurturing people, not for young people to learn flashy writing and obtain official honors. All students must personally practice the White Deer Grotto Academy rules... All students must, in every step's speed, every word's advance and halt, be composed and careful in all things, peaceful and refined.

Schools imposed strict requirements on children's conduct and character, and educated people became models of moral cultivation in rural areas. Their cultural life and spiritual pursuits were often idealized and recorded in genealogies, passed down through generations. For example, the "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy" records local gentry Zheng Gong'e, "broad-minded and talented, fond of antiquity, skilled in poetry and rhapsody. Built a fine house, placed books, displayed ancient paintings and curios for refined appreciation. In leisure from reading, only enjoyed playing the qin and planting flowers; when the weather was fine and warm, drew spring water to brew tea, spread mats and opened wine cups, with two or three close friends, roaming freely among mist, clouds, springs, and rocks, no longer knowing worldly glory or shame. And teaching children with a schedule, beyond proper upbringing, further refined their temperament with poetry and books." Zhu Mi of Huatan Village, "studied well and loved antiquity, simple and self-possessed, profit and desire could not move his heart, glory and salary could not seize his will.
