Filial to parents, friendly to brothers" (see "Epitaph of the Scholar of Mingyi Hall"). Genealogies and epitaphs have exaggeration, but from them the value orientation can be seen.

Some local gentry directly engaged in educational work. At the end of Song and beginning of Yuan, Xie Mengfu of Heyang Village, "broadly learned in classics and history, respected in his time, in the Xianchun period of Song served as guest scholar at the prefecture school, then promoted to classics instructor. His bearing was neat and solemn, his dress strict and elegant, a model for officials and gentry. At the time called an elderly Confucian scholar. He has the 'Asking the Woodcutter' and other collections published" ("Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy"). In the Jiajing period of Ming, Jin Jiufeng of Yantou Village, "by nature quiet and leisurely, each day sat alone in a room, all classics, histories, and various schools of thought he was well-versed in, and furthermore studied astrology, enjoyed music. As for examination essays and song lyrics, they were his remaining skills. ... Established a teaching tent to instruct students, spring breeze filling the seats, transforming rain filling the courtyard, truly a leader among scholar-teachers" ("Preface to Mr. Jiufeng's Fiftieth Birthday"). These people were very conscious of spreading Confucian ethics and maintaining traditional order. They had practical influence on village affairs.

However, rural intellectuals, also representatives of upper-class culture, had a dual personality structure. According to Confucian ideals, in advancement they served the court, in retirement they returned to the mountains; when among mountains and forests, they were indifferent and content, their spirits full of Daoist thought of returning to nature. The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy" has a "Preface to Langxia Scenery Poems," saying of this township "scholars in groups, many with refined talent, ancient sentiments as if meeting, with the bearing of dignified elders, can be called cultured and refined, rural places with many outstanding people."

It was the combination of orthodox Confucianism and "dignified elder bearing" that created the characteristics and dual personality of Nanxi River humanistic culture. Reflected in architecture, it similarly shows the contradictory coexistence of upper-class culture and folk culture. And returning to nature and being indifferent and harmonious nurtured Nanxi River architecture to be especially intimate and human.

2. Landscape Sentiment

The Nanxi River has beautiful scenery. The Nanxi River is also a "little Zou and Lu" (cultural heartland). The beautiful scenery, together with the cultural tradition of loving natural beauty, nurtured the landscape sentiment of Nanxi River people. Whenever in spring with misty rain and rolling clouds, azaleas bloom across the green mountains. After the mountain red fades, white paulownia flowers bloom luxuriantly. In deep autumn, Chinese tallow trees are as vivid as blood. The clear river water, following the seasons, reflects various bright colors. Nanxi River literati, deeply influenced by traditional culture, were very sensitive to natural beauty, and a clear environmental aesthetic consciousness permeated Nanxi River people's hearts, transforming into the charm of villages. They integrated with beautiful nature, also using nature for decoration and embellishment, while the style of houses was the simplest. Nature is the charm of Nanxi River vernacular architecture.

(1) Fortunate Mountains and Rivers

The mountains and rivers are beautiful, and they are also fortunate. In 422 CE, the third year of Liu Song's Yongchu reign, Xie Lingyun came to Yongjia Commandery as governor. Enchanted by Yongjia's landscapes, he "wandered freely," writing China's first landscape poems. Xie Lingyun governed Yongjia for less than a year, but left many traces in the Nanxi River. He wrote poems at Jinzhu Stream, Bai'an, Shishi Stream, and Lüzhang Mountain.

Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Geography" says: "Formerly when Xie Kangle (Xie Lingyun) was governor, he loved touring famous mountains, hence this commandery's landscapes became known worldwide. Scholars passing this prefecture all lingered, composing poetry without ceasing, to boast of its beauty." Among those who lingered, according to Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle · Scenic Spots," was Tao Hongjing of the Liang dynasty. His famous poem "Reply to Emperor Gao of Qi's Edict" was written at Lüzhang Mountain:

What is in the mountains? Many white clouds on the ridges;

Only for self-enjoyment, cannot be sent to you.

Hence later there was a place named Baiyun (White Cloud) Ridge. Qianlong's "Record of Daluo Rock" also says he cultivated himself at Daluo Rock in the Lesser Nanxi River, writing the important Daoist text "Zhengao" and two medical books "Collected Commentaries on Materia Medica" and "Supplementary Elbow-Hundred Prescriptions." The place where he cultivated was called Tao Gong Cave, and nearby there was also a Baiyun Ridge. In the nearby Shuiyun Village, a Baiyun Pavilion was built. It is said that the famous masterpiece "Letter in Reply to Secretary Xie" describes Nanxi River scenery.

Tang dynasty poets Meng Haoran, Luo Yin, and Cui Daorong visited Yongjia. Bai Juyi visited the Nanxi River, donating money to build roads and two embankments at Daluo Rock (see "Record of Daluo Rock"). In the Song, Lu You visited Yongjia; in the Ming, Li Dongyang. Early Qing scholar Zhu Yizun came to the Nanxi River after failing in anti-Qing resistance, once living in Langxia Village. The "Collection of Baishuting" contains "Yongjia Miscellaneous Poems," several written in Langxia Village. Such as "Song of Viewing Yandang Mountain from Huatan, Dedicated to Zhu Sheng Thirteen and Zhu Zhenjia Eighteen":

Climbing to the summit of Huatan, gazing at the winding peaks of Yandang. Clouds gathering about to rain, waters rushing down the mountain. Remote peaks appearing and disappearing beyond count, only seeing sad birds and beasts returning at dusk.

Since Duke Kangle began it, generations of poets "composed without ceasing" about Nanxi River landscapes. Su Shi, both admiring and confident, wrote a poem: "Saying the governor is like Lingyun, can make mountains and rivers like Yongjia." Yongjia's mountains and rivers are truly fortunate.

Living amid splendid mountains and rivers, with hearts nourished by over a thousand years of culture, Nanxi River rural literati were very sensitive to and deeply loved the beauty of mountains, rivers, plants, and trees. Scholars who entertained themselves with farming and studying "lodged their aspirations in forests and springs," "sometimes sang and howled by flowing water, sometimes fished leaning on rocks," expressing their feelings for nature through their entire lives and poetry. Zhu Boqing of Huatan Village, a Ming dynasty person, "had elegant bearing, loved learning and literature, did not enjoy official advancement, his aspirations stored in forests and valleys. ... Family affairs entrusted to sons, only amusing himself with writing and ink. All rocks, trees, insects, fish, springs, and flowers that caught his eye and heart, he composed poetry about. When the wind was warm and the sun bright, wearing a cornered scarf and deerskin robe, followed by disciples, he wandered among water and mountain colors. Brushing clouds and sitting on rocks, playing the qin, watching flying geese, carefree and happy" ("Preface to Boqing Lord's Ten Poems of Zhenchuan" in "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy"). Therefore Qianlong's "Yongjia County Chronicle" says the Nanxi River "has elegant peaks and wondrous streams, people born there are all wise within and refined without, gentle and elegant."

The wondrous landscapes nurtured the unique temperament and humanistic characteristics of Nanxi River villagers.

This temperament transformed into the architectural style of the Nanxi River. Their villages, houses, and gardens were all simple and unadorned, yet open and bright. They were skilled at using the natural form, nature, and color of natural materials, making architecture harmonious with heaven and earth. Every household used uncarved rough stone walls, with curved bases, rugged and powerful, standing on ground paved with large cobblestones—the entire village seemed to have been born simultaneously with the rocks from ancient times. They also loved using natural raw timber, without axe work, following the curves, cleverly installing them in appropriate positions, as if they should have those curves and roughness. Carrying the original shapes of life, they echoed with the trees in the courtyard and all living things.

From top to bottom, architecture was rarely carved; wood and stone preserved their natural colors to the maximum; the small amount of processing came from the natural ability of human hands. This nurtured the亲切感 of Nanxi River architecture.

The forms of buildings were also very natural. Where a corridor was appropriate, a corridor; where a hall was appropriate, a hall. Lightweight eaves freely sheltered whatever needed sheltering. The curved and flowing roofs were as agile as eagle wings striking the sky.

Nanxi River people's environmental aesthetic consciousness was also expressed in village site selection, planning, greening, gardens, composition, etc., making the human living environment and beautiful mountains and rivers form a unified landscape.

(2) Intent Amid Mountains and Waters

The ancestors who developed the Nanxi River, without exception, built villages in places with beautiful scenery. Xie Lingyun's descendants originally settled in the commandery city; once: "Lord Wuwu toured Nanxi, saw the beauty of Heyang, then moved from the commandery city to Heyang" ("Preface to Rebuilding the Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy"). Other villages' genealogies also record similar site selection stories. For example, Tangwan Village's founding ancestor "loved the beauty of Nanxi River landscapes" and settled there; Qukou Village's founding ancestor also "loved its landscape beauty, then made his home there," etc.

Yuzhang Village is on the south bank of the Lesser Nanxi River; before entering the village, one must first pass through dense forests, cross a desolate river crossing, then pass through dense forests again. Behind the village, peaks as if carved and chiseled, layer upon layer, spread to both sides. At the foot of the mountains, flat fields like belts, among bamboo and trees, some hundred households scattered. The river winds into broad green pools on both left and right of the village, across the river facing a verdant Lion Mountain. The "Yuzhang Hu Clan Genealogy · Old Preface" (the thirty-fourth year of Kangxi, 1695) says:

Yongjia's landscapes, none as beautiful as the Greater and Lesser Rocks of Nan source, with towering cliffs and empty caves, where immortals have historically resided. Below this, as a scenic spot, none compares to Yuzhang. The literary peak reflected in the pool captures its wonder, the jade tablet across the head shows its uniqueness. Among it, auspicious births, generations of great achievements, those dwelling below are the Hu clan. ... The clan is prosperous, descendants are outstanding... clan branches spread, like rosy clouds and lush forests.

Langxia Village, upstream of Zhenxi Stream, is in a mountain valley with strangely shaped surrounding mountains. The stream comes from the northeast, passing north and west of the village, flowing southwest to Huatan Village. The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy · Preface to Langxia Scenery Poems" says:

Mountains connect to Yandang, entering the path already feels secluded; the land resembles a dragon head, crossing the ridge one knows its depth. Water encircles like a belt, where fish can swim; peaks form a screen, where birds occasionally fly. Mulberry and hemp cover the fields, maples hide the mountains. Like the seclusion of Peach Blossom Spring, the depth of Pan Valley.

The scenery of ancient villages on both banks of the Nanxi River is mostly like this—nestled by mountains and waters, fully displaying environmental beauty.

(3) Protection, Embellishment, and Enhancement

Nanxi River people, with their cultural cultivation, felt environmental beauty delicately and vividly. The "Tanxia Chen Clan Genealogy" records a five-character regulated poem by Mr. Jiehou of the Kangxi period, describing Tanxia Village's scenery:

Round and round a stretch of stone, green bamboo interspersed with green pine. Beyond the sand flowing water, before the door layered peaks.

Mountain flowers bloom on the islet bank, wild birds call in the spring breeze. With plow and hoe can read history, soar like rocs.

After literati's appreciation and evaluation, many Nanxi River villages had "Ten Scenes," "Eight Scenes," and genealogies often had special "Scenic Spots" chapters. Xie Daoning's "Heyang Eight Scenery Poems" one of which "Spring Sunshine of Brocade Peak":

Ten-thousand-ren screen surrounds looking up at the sun, warmth follows breaking the group shade. Rock flowers show beauty in high and low, wild trees float light between shallow and deep.

For enjoyment how can no one wear walking shoes, seeking seclusion there are still guests playing the qin. The western suburb only separated by sparse forest, how much red dust rushes to the lapel.

The poem integrates scenic beauty with a natural attitude toward life. Through admiring ancestor Duke Kangle, it connects this life attitude with a thousand-year cultural tradition.

Advocated by these local literati, Nanxi River people valued protecting the natural environment, including trees. Every village had fengshui forests, forbidden to cut. At village entrances and ends, ancient trees cast dense shade. The "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy · Record of the Camphor Tree at Xingong'ao" records that at Xingong'ao there was a Taiyin Palace, "to the right of the palace there was a camphor tree, its circumference a zhang, its height hard to measure. ... Lush and visible, more fresh after rain and dew; new beauty to enjoy, unchanged through wind and frost. It was where woodcutters rested, and where shepherds wandered. Yet it shaded the fierce sun, resisted strong winds, well-positioned, and was it not related to fengshui?" However, some profit-seeking people attempted to cut this tree. "Therefore village leaders did not dare stand by with folded arms, after careful consideration, collected funds to buy it as property of the old ancestral hall, with written evidence, forever after not allowed to cut."

Wandering, composing, protecting—none were enough to fully express Nanxi River people's landscape sentiment; they further wanted to add beauty to mountains and rivers. Large and small villages often had scenic accessory buildings such as terraces, pavilions, and towers. The earliest recorded is Northern Song jinshi and Baozhang Pavilion Attendant Chen Shi in Pengxia Village, who "built Yixiao and Boyun two pavilions at Fuyan to view waterfalls" (see "Liangyuan Chen Clan Ancestral Hall Genealogy"). More famous were several accessory buildings in Heyang Village of the Xie clan descendants. The genealogy records that during the Zhizheng period of Yuan, Xie Yisun "had an open and elegant mind, loved guests. ... loved music, enjoyed composing with people, ... with his tutor Mr. Chen Binyi, together built a terrace on East Mountain, planted orchids and bamboo, taking Duke Kangle's last words and carving the rock 'Lanyu Terrace' (Orchid-Jade Terrace). Teacher and student in leisure rested there, to pass long nights. Friends composing together, forming the 'Lanyu Terrace Collection'." By the mid-Ming, Xie Tingxun who served in the Embroidered Uniform Guard "built a tower west of Hexi Stream, because the tower was beside clear flow, named it 'Linqing' (Near Clarity), as a place for feasting guests and composing poetry," and simultaneously built a "Linliu Pavilion" (Near-Flow Pavilion) at "the village's north stream." Tingxun painted the Linliu Pavilion and presented it to Emperor Xuanzong of Ming, who actually inscribed a poem:

The near-flow pavilion is pure without dust, the sound of falling streams heard everywhere. Half wet half dry dew on flowers, flying and coming clouds over the ridges.

Green confuses the cave mouth with a thousand bamboos, white occupies the forest top with a flock of cranes. This place is secluded and people do not come, only leaving wind and moon to share equally.

This pavilion was hence also called "Baohan Pavilion" (Precious Calligraphy Pavilion).

Embellishing and enhancing landscapes still could not satisfy literati's deep affection; they took to creating. Nanxi River "recluses" mostly liked "hoeing gardens and planting flowers, digging ponds and opening plots," arranging private gardens. The "Le'an Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy" records Zhu Yingfeng of Langxia Village writing a "Song of Seclusion":

Neither scholar nor farmer, half farming and half studying. By the mountain several qing of fields, by the water several rooms. Building gardens and digging ponds, planting flowers and bamboo. ... Flowers emit their own fragrance, bamboo also speaks of fragrance, pool water ripples with lotus, garden vegetables lean on alfalfa.

In the mid-Ming, Zhu Xun of Huatan Village, "his family was fairly prosperous, managing halls and structures, on remaining land dug ponds and opened plots, planted flowers and raised fish, as a place for relatives, friends, wine cups, poetry, and laughter." Around the same time, fellow villager Zhu Puxuan had a poem "Rock Artificial Mountain," the first couplet being "Who stands so elegant as if natural, layered green peaks with myriad scenery" (both in "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy"), showing that mountain village gardening already had artificial rockery techniques by then. Yantou Village's Upper Garden, Cangpo Village's Shuiyue Hall, Furong Village's Furong Academy mountain master residence and "Great Mansion" all had small gardens, where ancient trees still reach the sky, and remnants of flower walls and rockeries are faintly visible.

Landscape sentiment created one of the greatest features of Nanxi River villages: large public gardens. The public gardens of Yantou, Cangpo, Xikou, Qukou, Daitou, Zhu'an, Xi'an, and Hesheng villages are largely preserved. The parks of Daitou, Hesheng, and Heyang villages are on hillsides; Xi'an Village's is by the river, with ancient trees dense, visible from afar. Yantou Village's park, purely by human effort, has the richest content and most varied layout, built during the Jiajing period of Ming. It includes a small Tang Mountain, a Zhennan Lake, a Jinhuan Lake, the Qinyu Peninsula between them, and a 300-meter-long Lishui Lake. Both lakes are formed by artificial dams blocking stream water; on the dams several ancient trees, under their shade an exquisite small pavilion; on Tang Mountain peak a pagoda, at the foot pavilions and temples. In the lake lotus blooms abundantly, on the island hibiscus shines like rosy clouds.

Parks were public activity places for villagers, especially for rural literati. Yantou Village's "Ten Scenery Poems" depict their activities in the park. "Spring Dawn on the Long Embankment" has lines: "Gathering together for days carrying wine, walking and composing not minding circling the long embankment"; "Viewing Lotus at Li Bridge" has: "Sitting facing her as if understanding words, riding the wind walking to enjoy coolness"; "Viewing Fish in the Clear Pool" has: "The willow embankment and flowers allow walking and singing, laughing at swimming fish leaping in brocade waves"; "Winding Flow Encircling Green" has: "Not knowing the fisherman with rod and line, sitting facing the fishing rock how joyful?" In these poems, the life and interests of rural literati are vividly visible, as well as their intoxicated landscape sentiment.

These rural literati lived a high-grade cultural life in the parks, inevitably bringing their profound cultural cultivation to every plant and tree in the parks. Therefore these gardens and their buildings have a very light and elegant style. Poetic and picturesque, without departing from rural character.

The landscape sentiment and life culture running through village construction in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River are the immortal charm of Nanxi River vernacular architecture."

本节术语 Key Terms

耕读生活 科举制度 义学 学田 理学 永嘉学派 薛季宣 郑伯熊 陈傅良 叶适 戴溪 戴侗 朱熹 乡贤士绅 白鹿洞规 山水情怀 谢灵运 陶弘景 孟浩然 白居易 陆游 朱彝尊 风水林 风景小品建筑 私家小园 公共园林 岩头村公园 丽水湖 镇南湖 进宦湖
三、村落规划和建设的工作机制
中文原文重构

三、村落规划和建设的工作机制

楠溪江一些村落的建设，经过统一的规划，并且在很长时期里坚持规划管理。这就必须有集中的、强而有效的行政权力和它的稳定的社会基础，必须有能够推动和胜任这些工作的人。正是这些条件，使楠溪江乡土建设必以聚落为单元。

1.宗族组织的作用

楠溪江的村落几乎都是血缘村落，一村一姓，一个宗族。虽然有政府的行政系统，但是在小农经济的农业社会，宗族组织实际上是血缘村落的政权机构。所以，尽管在村子里有大大小小的宗祠，有些村子甚至有宗祠十几座，却没有一个村子有地方行政机构的公廨。

两汉、南北朝和隋唐沿袭了将近一千年的门阀制度，到了宋代彻底消失。代之而起的社会组织力量就是宗族。范仲淹、欧阳询和苏轼都曾经顺应历史潮流，为加强宗族地位和作用有所创建。欧阳询和苏轼倡导宗谱学，各自设计了谱式，沿用了一千来年。范仲淹则提倡宗族设义田，抚老恤贫，保证族人"日有食、岁有衣、嫁娶婚葬皆有赡"，从而加强了宗族的社会功能，加强了它的凝聚力。宋代的理学家重修身，吕大钧首先提倡《乡约》，在家乡陕西蓝田推行之后，"关中风俗为之一变"。乡约是理学家用他们所阐释的儒家礼乐来教化乡民的生活公约，它规范乡民们的思维方式、行为方式和价值观念。宗族是推行乡约的组织力量。

宋室南渡之后，朱熹在南方推行吕氏乡约。楠溪江的主要宗族大都在这时期形成，产生了自己的理学家，并与朱熹有来往。

到了明代，先任永嘉县令，后来又任温州知府的文林，在知府任上于弘治十一年（1498年）制定了一套《族范》，基本内容与吕氏《乡约》一脉相承，在温属五邑推行，楠溪江"各族应之"，纷纷写入宗谱，成为"家训"、"族规"或者"宗范"。

《族范》是宗族的"基本法"，它的《掌教名数》则是宗族的组织法，主要内容是：

一、族宾（按：无说明）。

二、族献（按：无说明）。

三、族长：族长乃一族之统领，必先正己而后正人，务秉至公以御群子弟。……必使上和下睦，同敦雍厚之风。

四、族正：设学行兼优四人为族正，以匡族长之不逮也。凡族内有事，先当咨于族正，然后白于族长之前，为之分辨曲直，析正是非，庶有统属而无紊乱，抑亦正风俗而免事端矣！

五、主籍：择公直子弟二人为主籍，掌管善恶文簿……如此则劝惩之法立。

六、司学：择取性行端庄、学识明达者一人主之，专一训诲弟子，讲习诗书礼义等事。……

七、司讼：推族内洞明事理二人为司讼。凡族之有讼者凭其禀白族长、族正，劝谕不止乃率于官治之，毋得轻造公庭。

八、司恤：司恤二人。凡族人及乡里亲戚贫穷患难，丧葬嫁娶力不赡者，司恤为之白于族长前，劝谕族中之有力者周恤之。……

九、司直：推选性刚直，遇事敢言者二人为司直，凡族长有过，从容指陈于族正前，以凭劝勉。族正有过，陈于族长前，以凭规戒。族众有过，轻则直接以屈之，重则白于族长斥之，使之改过自新而后已。

十、司纠：推举铁面无私、不避嫌怨者七人司之。凡族人为恶不听劝谕者，司纠率至家庙，由族长重责。再不听，从官惩之。

十一、值月：举老幼各一人为值月，凡一月之内，一应大小族人所为善恶及词讼等事不报于主籍者，值月必询访而报于各执事，各执事书于簿籍，以白于族长。

这个《掌教名数》把宗族组织规定成很完备的政权机构。《枫林徐氏宗谱·族范八条》里，甚至规定可以动用"祖宗家法"，把"孽深害大，素性又终不肯改移"的盗窃犯处死，"令其全身自毙"。宗族也可以在必要时组织"乡勇"、"义兵"这样的武装力量，进行战争。

宗族组织要负责非一家一户所能办的农田水利的建设和管理。《珍川朱氏宗谱·宗法》里规定，由族中派人"包灌稻田水，节省人工、水料，避免争端，此乃联宗睦族之道"，并且详细写明了各种气候情况下各类地块的灌溉顺序和方法。

宗族有公有经济，包括公田、祭田、学田、义田之类的收入。宗谱里严格规定，公有田产不得盗卖。

宗族又是培植和维护社会意识的强大力量。文林的《族范》规定：

凡遇春秋祭祀之时，朔望参谒之日，族长、族正以下，依次而坐，令弟子三人北面而立，读太祖高皇帝《旧制》。其词曰："孝顺父母，尊敬长上，和睦乡里，教训子孙，各安生理，毋作非为。"族属皆跪听。又读古灵陈先生《劝谕文》，曰："为吾民者，父义、母慈、兄友、弟恭、子孝；夫妇有恩、男女有别、子弟有学、乡闾有礼；贫穷患难、亲戚相救，婚姻死丧、邻保相助；毋惰农业，毋作盗贼，毋学赌博，毋好争讼；毋以恶凌善，毋以富骄贫；行者逊路，耕者让畔，斑白者不负戴于道路，则为礼义之俗矣。"族属立听。

有些宗谱里规定得更隆重，朗诵每篇之前要击鼓三声。朗诵时"各要悚然而听，如有在班诒笑闲谈者，族正举于族前，量行责罚，以警将来"。

物质力量加上精神力量，使得宗族组织的力量非常强大，吞没了政府机构。这样强大的宗族力量，足可以管理关系到整个宗族利益的村落规划和建设了。这是楠溪江村落大多有完整的规划，有管理得很好的公共生活中心的主要原因。

但是，除了族长的任命按惯例"以齿不以爵，以齿不以尊"之外，其他从族正以下各种职司其实都只能由读书有文化的人担当。其结果就是，农村的各项权力基本都掌握在士绅乡贤手里。可以说，他们的素质决定村落的命运，决定它的面貌。从各村的宗谱里可以见到，村落里比较重要的建设成就，都是由几位热心公益的乡绅文人的创议和主持，并且得到宗族的组织保证。《棠川郑氏宗谱》里有三篇重要的文章：《长堤记》、《池塘记》和《新城记》，它们很详明地记述了事关全村安危的重要工程的建设和管理规矩。

2.士绅的作用

楠溪江村落的士绅乡贤，主要是辞官还乡的、绝意仕进的和举业不成的读书人，他们组成了乡里社会的领导阶层。他们受过良好的文化教育，在论学吟诗之余，大多关心乡里，乐于"善举"，从而把他们的文化修养带进了村落的建设。

村落的规划一般都在建村之初进行，大多失于记述，早已没有可靠的资料。但各族宗谱里，都记载许多有功于乡里建设的人，也记载了一些有长远影响的建设工程。

例如岩头村，《金氏宗谱》记载了第二世祖日新公（生元中统丙子，卒元至正戊子，即1348年）建设水利的事："时厥土苦于旱潦，频岁不登，府君相地宜，顺水性，浚两渠于渎头之南，达泉下灌，常获丰稔，两都农业，迄今赖之。"其后又有桂林公（生明弘治甲寅，卒隆庆己巳，1494-1569年），宗谱说他："由始迁岩头以来，列祖非无建造，而兴利之多，功德之盛，应推府君为第一。"他的作为是"培风水、兴地利、置祭田、建公业。""本族地址颇高，田苦旱涸，开四公(按即桂林公)捐旧废资，开凿长河一带，以备蓄泄，并筑高埠，培闸风水。建亭造塔于其上，垂成，归之大宗祠，为通族公业。"约略与桂林公同时，嘉靖进士金昭（霞峰公）建设了上花园、下花园、大宗祠，还为自己建了个牌楼。先后有几个人从事乡里建设，所以岩头村的水系、街道、公共建筑、园林等等，在楠溪江都是最出色的。

苍坡村和芙蓉村，规划严谨、水系完备、街道整齐，苍坡村还有大片的园林，这些也都靠两村历来有热心建设的人士。筑长堤蓄水成湖，在苍坡大约是南宋时代（1178年）的事，早于岩头几近四百年。芙蓉村陈氏的有绪公（生康熙壬申，卒乾隆癸酉，1692-1753年），"有经济才，读书知大义。……乐于济施。创神庙，建祖祠，增置祀田，造舟以济往来，筑防以利灌溉。公平生所为，有功于族党者未易枚举"（《两源陈氏宗谱》）。

《棠川郑氏宗谱》里有一篇《长堤记》，记的是清代初年，乾隆丁卯（1747年）春，宗人郑西献捐资并组织人力建造堤堰的事。记中写道：

棠川胜区在双溪会所，衔远山、吞长流，前有雷峰九嶂之异，后有马石天岩之险，至若屏风纳日，和合留云，在在称奇。惟路尚缺一堤，虽乔木荫翳，水声潺湲，而往来行人，往往苦此。今则累石为堤，不惜工程，自东岭至西岭，约数十馀丈，状如江塘，平坦可以车行。

除了水利、宗祠、庙宇、道路之外，书院历来是乡里建设的重点，吸引了士绅们最多的注意。《珍川朱氏合族副谱·慎轩公传》说，公"生平乐善好施，而于读书一事尤其所笃，尝虑族子弟辈无肄业所，不克振长人材，作兴后学，爱捐重赀，襄诸乡之知名者创建文昌阁两庑，以为讲学之所"。也有一些对环境绿化有特殊兴趣的，如花坛村的朱复翁，"好读书，朴而不滞，惟亲贤取友以自励。……洪武初，诏山林隐逸，郡县强荐于京，授朝列大夫，乞归，许之，声名益重。建宗祠，置祭田，以奉祀先人。筑室芳岙，植松树数万株以自蔽，因以云松自号。……啸傲云松间，商榷古今，抚琴作诗"（《明征授朝列大夫云松公墓志铭》）。

这些乡贤士绅，在乡土建设中主要起的作用当然是倡议、擘划、捐资，至于工程的主持，就要依靠宗族的力量。例如，康熙二十一年（1682年）六月，为重建渠口叶氏大宗祠，由宗人叶健等发起，"会同两房商议，仰体先人创建之心，各出己资，重建宗祠。择本年八月初六起工切木，来春二月初一日竖造。当日估算，材木几何，椽木几何，考工几何，约用银陆百两"。两房依人口比例分担，长房出肆百两，二房出二百两。粗工由长房负责。这件事显然是由宗族主持的（见《渠川叶氏宗谱·叶氏合同议据》）。

有些宗族，把修桥补路当作一种义务写进家规，如《岩头金氏宗谱·家规》里写道："桥、路、渡舟倾坏，子孙倘有馀资，当助修治。"在《渠口叶氏宗谱》里有《石马岩石栏杆志》和《前山楼梯岩志》两篇文字。前者记一条"上峻壁、下深潭"的宽仅四尺许的山路，"时有夜行客人至此倾跌而下，其危险莫可言状"。于是，一批村人"醵集钜资，建造石栏数十丈。自斯以往，该处变险为夷"。后者记渠口村前山"有名楼梯岩者，高六丈许，陟降必经之处，峻无阶级。樵夫至此，莫不栗栗危惧"，也有一批善人，集资雇石匠，"将高峻处开凿阶级如楼梯焉"。这个宗谱里还有关于修路的记载二十几则。

把这些善举记录在宗谱里，是对当事人的榆扬，"传之永久"，以为子孙后代表率。宗族用这个崇高的荣誉来激励有余财的人从事乡里建设。如《岩头金氏宗谱·家规》载，"祠堂、坟屋稍遇倾圮，亟当议修，量以坟租、祠租内暂行抽贮，以给支。如有贤孝子孙捐资乐助者，当登记于谱"。更有在记入宗谱之外再加碑刻。例如《鹤垟谢氏宗谱》记第五世端先公"敦尚德义，轻财好施，凿深港以疏壅滞，乡里赖之。因勒石于鹤岭溪滨，内镌：'宋绍熙五年（1194年）谢十八居士开港'十二字，以志其绩"。一般修造庙宇和石桥也往往把捐资人的姓名刻在碑上或桥上。最突出的是岩头村的桂林公，在他去世之后，村人们为纪念他对村子建设的重大贡献，把他创建的书院改成了奉祀他的专祠，称为水亭祠。

在乡文士，或是直接、或是通过宗族组织，从事乡里建设，他们的文化素养和山水情怀就转化在这些建设之中了。楠溪江村落里文化建筑的类型和数量很多，建筑的风格大方素雅，不事奢华的雕琢，而且追求建筑与自然山水的融洽。例如乾隆年间造的花坛村文昌阁，"门临曲水，地耸云端，拱群峰之突屹，面万木之郁葱，珍川之胜，于此称第一焉。登斯阁者，晨夕之赏心各异，四时之兴趣不穷，文思秀发，颖悟宏开"（《珍川朱氏合谱·慎轩公传》），要的是在山川胜景中秀发文思，当是清新俊逸。

3.堪舆师的作用

堪舆风水之说对楠溪江村落的规划和建设有很大的影响，而阴阳师或者地理师，大都出自乡土文人这个阶层。所以，风水堪舆之说是乡贤士绅影响农村规划和建设的又一个途径，阴阳师或者地理师，实际在一定程度上起着类似规划师的作用，但他们所依据的是迷信。

明代嘉靖年间对岩头村的建设作了重大贡献的桂林公，其实就是一位地理师。《岩头金氏宗谱·桂林公行状》说他"屡试不中，转而习青囊，相宅卜地"。传说他还得到宁王幕下谋士"国师"李自实的帮助。他所兴建的，可能都经过堪舆风水的考究。与桂林公大致同时，还有些士绅好星象之学，如嘉靖年间岩头村还有一位九峰先生，他"凡经史以及诸子百家无不娴，且究心星学，陶情音律，至于制艺歌词，皆其余事耳"（《岩头金氏宗谱·九峰先生五旬寿序》）。清代中叶咸丰年间，蓬溪村有一位谢文波，《宗谱》有一篇《谢公文波六旬初度寿序》说："近百年来，俗尚武力，独文波笃内行，嗜书史，学涉赅博，家多藏书，插架林立，于壬奇、星数、音韵反切之学，旁及琴棋八法，无不精通，风雅过人远甚。"这位先生的著作有《东瓯杂俎》、《因音求字》、《四声正误》、《反切法》和《草药谱》等等。

这些记载说明，当时习风水和壬奇、星数的人，都是些饱读经典诗书的儒者。儒学是上层的雅言文化，术数是下层的民俗文化，本来属于不同的两类。不过，早在汉代独尊儒术的时候，就已经把它与阴阳五行之类结合起来了。从那时以后，儒者把堪舆风水和壬奇、星数当做一般文化知识来学，并不以为与儒学的正统精神有根本的矛盾。只在迷信得太似乎"怪、力、乱、神"，有悖于情理的时候，才加以驳斥，例如长期停厝不葬，"以死者为生者祈福"，就受到严厉批评。

堪舆风水是迷信邪说，但阴阳师和主持村落建设的士绅们都是当时当地有知识的人。他们是双重人格的，他们会在一些问题上愚妄可笑，但由于他们富有阅历、经验，也会对一些问题作出合乎实际的判断，如对村落选址、水系规划、村落结构布局、防御工程、街道网、重要建筑物的位置和朝向等等，可能有一些比较好的建议。为了保护他们的职业，或者仅仅为了增强他们对自己的实际判断的自信心和他们的建议对公众的说服力，他们便使他们的经验知识附会风水堪舆的神秘"理论"。塘湾村的筑城和凿池，显然都是为了迫切的实际需要，但都附会了一套风水说法。看来这说法虽然是些无根游谈，但有助于号召村民们同心同德，出钱出力，来办成一些公益事业。

楠溪江中游的村子，例如苍坡、岩头和芙蓉，都流传着"国师"李自实规划村落的故事。例如说，南宋淳熙五年（1178年），苍坡村九世祖李嵩请李自实规划了寨墙、街道和池塘、水渠。他使主街直指西方远处的笔架山，命名主街为笔街，以利于发荣科甲。但笔架山又像火焰，为防笔街引火烧村，就在它东端造了两口池塘，以水克火。池塘之一称砚池，再以村子附会纸张，两块石条附会墨锭，与笔街一起组成"文房四宝"，这就更加有利于科甲了。宗族便以这种无根游谈激励子弟刻苦读书。

又例如说，明代嘉靖年间，桂林公请李自实规划岩头村。他问桂林公，希望"紧发"还是"慢发"。紧发即将寨墙包住全姓聚居范围，数代之内就可以发达，但前途有限；慢发即把杂姓聚居地也围在寨墙里，发达虽比较慢，但前途更加繁荣。桂林公选择了"紧发"，寨墙就是现存的状况。李自实还故弄玄虚说，村东的屿山是一条大蟒幻化的，会对村人不利，所以，在村里规划了四条东西向的窄巷，象征四支"箭"，两条在东端分叉的小巷，和一条东端曲折的小巷象征两把半"镗"，这七件"武器"就把屿山大蟒镇住了，可以保护村人平安。

据传说，元至正元年（1341年）重建芙蓉村的时候，也是李自实看风水，做规划的。

这些故事荒诞无稽，而且一个"国师"李自实，从南宋历元代而直到嘉靖年间还在，显然也是虚构。但这些故事却说明，当时确实有这类人对村子的规划性问题提出建议，不论是合理的，还是虚妄的。

虽然有些士绅乡贤从事堪舆风水之术，但对它的宿命论的消极作用也已经有人注意到了。不语"怪力乱神"的儒家文化，对属于俗文化的风水术是很有批判能力的。例如，伪托朱熹写的《雪心赋》大肆鼓吹风水，说"将相公侯，胥此焉出；荣华富贵，何莫不由"。但是，这种宿命论不但没有丝毫的伦理教化意义，甚至有碍于儒家的伦理教化，破坏了儒家教化的价值观。因此，朱熹在《大学衍义补·家乡之礼》里写道："世有选择之法存焉，不能不用之以代卜筮。但其所谓福禄不可信，其趋吉避凶之说亦不可。"堪舆家也有"灵山灵穴，有德者居之"，"山地好，不如心地好"这类话。连《雪心赋》也说："欲求滕公之佳城，须积叔敖之阴德"，"穴本天成，福由心造"，甚至说："积德必获吉迁，积恶还招凶地"，又把风水的决定作用否定了。风水理论的这种自相矛盾，反映出正统的雅言文化与民俗文化的矛盾。民俗文化里多巫术因素，而雅言文化则不能不重视儒家的礼乐教化，因为这是维持社会秩序所必需的。

楠溪江的乡绅们，也是一方面迷信风水，一方面又要或轻或重地批评宿命论，以维持儒家的伦理。例如，豫章村到清代中叶以后，文运渐衰，在《重修豫章胡氏宗谱序》里，怀念宋代"斯时一门三世登进士者五"，到明代又出了"十三世祖宗韫公升文渊阁大学士、中书舍人"，受到皇帝赐馔的恩宠，然后说："人咸曰豫章山川秀甲两源所由致此。予曰，允若兹，今何不古若也？岂山川灵淑之气独钟于昔而不钟于今耶？虽本朝康熙年间地被水坏，而人事尽则天心可回，诚能起而读孔圣书，法周公礼，犹可易否为泰，转剥为复焉。"这些话对风水的批驳鲜明而又有力。

又如，乾隆年间廊下村朱朋轩为文昌阁选了个风水吉壤，改迁之后，得意地说："顾翊运者神，钟灵者地，含英揽秀，未必非吾乡之一大裨益也。"但又不得不回过头来说几句救漏补罅的话："吾乡朱氏，自宋明以来，登科第者代不乏人，然其时未有祠也。士苟穷经学古，志趣不诡于圣人，则翼思启行，神自降祥以助之，区区庙貌云尔哉广。"（见《珍川朱氏合族副谱·重建文昌阁记》）

据光绪《永嘉县志》，明代郡守文林、何文渊等和地方上比较有学识的乡绅，曾经对巫风淫祠、卜筮堪舆等等作过斗争，明令禁止。《县志》把这些迷信的风行看做是人心浇漓的一种现象。不过，在科举水平极低的时代，这种斗争收效甚微，后来只剩下按照文林的指示写就的《珍川朱氏合族副谱·族范》里的几句话：

死者以穸穸为安宅，死而未葬犹行而未得其归也。是以孝子虽爱亲而留之不敢久者，以亲未获所安，故寝苫块，己亦不敢安也。为子孙者当执正理，毋或泥于堪舆风水之拘及惑于阴阳家时日之忌，……致令丧灵久露，反伤于义，获罪于亲。

儒家的伦理是封建社会中最高的行为规范，它是理性的、现实的，以维持社会生活的正常秩序为目的。而堪舆风水，毕竟是迷信，无从验证，虽然可以利用人们的愚昧，似乎处处在起作用，但是，它的存在和作用，都以不损害儒家伦理的实际运行为条件。所以，堪舆风水之术，只能在缈不可知的阴宅的选穴上起重要决定作用，而在村落的规划建设上，它的作用常常是附会、修正，偶然被用来推动有实际利益的公共事业。

4.工匠的作用

楠溪江的建筑工匠体制和他们传统的设计、计算、施工等等方法以及有关的民俗行为，已经无从查考。不过，推断在长期小农自然经济社会中，工匠的专业化程度不高，没有组成经济实体、以父子师徒方式传承、有一些行业习俗，大致合乎实际。

工匠大多不是士人、富人，也无从襄赞善行义举，所以不载于宗谱。偶然有几位因为有功于建设，品行超卓，才得以占几行楮墨。例如《两源陈氏宗谱》载明代的工匠："有福公，字佑如，号兰轩，智巧绝伦，有造凤阁龙楼之技。家贫不能读书，然颇知大义。岁戊子建文广公祖祠，公匠心独运，不惮艰辛。"另一位也是明代的，见于《坦下陈氏宗谱》："讳士商，字兆霖。气象温和，与人无忤。少习公输之业，精专其事，相材度木，适中其度。所以遐迩创厦新宇，皆任于君。至今缺蛸有继，家道渐宁，然能颇知尊宗敬祖，凡有义举，靡不竭力。"从"匠心独运"、"相材度木"来看，这两位匠人是兼任了设计者的大匠。

由于父子师徒传承，建筑行业的各作都有了专业村。例如"大木老司出罗坑，石头老司出中堡，烧瓦老司出敬仁，泥水老司出绿嶂"。

虽然有专业村，但工匠的专业化程度还不高。大木老司也做床柜、书桌、琴凳，甚至织布机和风车；泥水老司也垒锅灶；打石老司也做捣臼、磨盘、石柱、牌坊、各种石器，甚至刻碑文。砌墙老司的行当尤其驳杂，要造石拱桥、铺路、筑矶步、垒城墙、挖地道、砌田坎等等。最使这些老司的手艺发挥得淋漓尽致的，是做各种装饰。泥水老司会塑飞禽走兽、人物山水、狮子麒麟。"堆龙就像龙一色，塑凤就像凤飞腾。"打石老司会打这些题材，还要把"狮子捧球打镂空"。砌墙老司则会用卵石在地面镶出各种图案，叫做"插石花"。

关于这些匠作，楠溪江都有歌谣吟咏。雅言文化的宗谱所不取的，在民俗文化中却代代流传。民俗文化，基本上是劳动者的文化。一首《大木老司》的歌谣是：

大木老司手艺精，手控丈杆量得清。

曲尺木斗线弹准，墨刻梁柱分寸明。

双退大屋两边轩，能造高楼大厦厅。

梁上叠梁斗叠斗，红油栏杆雕花名。

宫庙寺宇造古董，亭台楼阁八角井。

……

丈杆或称制尺，在上面划定整幢房子的全部大小尺寸。它相当建筑设计图，工匠依靠它能造出双退住宅、官庙寺宇和亭台楼阁各种类型和形制的房屋，包括相当复杂的斗拱。不过以造"古董"为能，则是农业社会中的保守心理，反映出生活的停滞和小农眼光的狭隘。

有一首长长的歌谣详细描述了房屋的兴建过程和工匠的劳作：

深山采来沉香木，鲁班祖师造新厅。

择定黄道吉庆日，起木发兴做不停。

大木老司来做工，画好图样定屋形。

石匠老司定磉石，阴阳定向遇吉星。

清吉良辰来拚木，多少功夫利排成。

黄道吉日开柱眼，竹匠柱头箍得紧。

竖柱喜遇黄道日，上梁巧逢紫微星。

梁上重梁斗叠斗，四面花窗映花明。

屋顶盖落滚椽瓦，地砌玉砖斗七星。

四面搭起禽兽头，墙头嵌镜发光明。

九曲游廊团圈走，平池着板大和珍。

玲珑花窗腾落阔，窗下书桌摆现成。

前后镇落对门，开门关门凤凰声。

前有亭栽栖凤竹，后有池养化龙鱼。

兴造房屋关风水，财聚小康振家声。

这首歌谣值得注意的两点是：一、明说木匠老司"画好图样定屋形"；二、明说石匠老司"阴阳定向遇吉星"，这工作是在"定磉石"的时候做的。看来，石匠老师在单幢房屋范围里掌管着风水，可能是遵照《鲁班经》里的一些规矩，因为前面说："鲁班祖师造新厅。"《三十六行歌》开篇唱："鲁班祖师是亲师父，传授老司手艺精。"

这首歌谣也表达了乡人们对建筑的审美理想和对匠人技艺的称赞。

English Translation

III. Working Mechanism of Village Planning and Construction

The construction of some Nanxi River villages underwent unified planning and maintained planning management over a long period. This required concentrated, strong, and effective administrative power and its stable social foundation, as well as people capable of promoting and undertaking this work. It was precisely these conditions that made Nanxi River vernacular construction necessarily take settlements as units.

1. The Role of Clan Organizations

Almost all Nanxi River villages were kinship villages, one village with one surname, one clan. Although there was a government administrative system, in an agricultural society of small-scale peasant economy, the clan organization was actually the political institution of the kinship village. Therefore, although there were ancestral halls of various sizes in villages, some even having over a dozen ancestral halls, no village had a yamen for local administrative institutions.

The aristocratic clan system that persisted for nearly a thousand years through the Han, Northern and Southern Dynasties, and Sui-Tang periods completely disappeared by the Song dynasty. The social organizational force that replaced it was the clan. Fan Zhongyan, Ouyang Xun, and Su Shi all顺应 historical trends and made contributions to strengthening clan status and role. Ouyang Xun and Su Shi advocated genealogy studies, each designing a genealogy format that was used for over a thousand years. Fan Zhongyan promoted clans establishing charity fields to care for the elderly and relieve the poor, ensuring clan members "had food daily, clothing yearly, and support for marriage, burial, and funeral," thereby strengthening the clan's social function and cohesion. Song Neo-Confucian scholars emphasized self-cultivation; Lü Dajun first advocated the "Village Covenant," which after implementation in his hometown of Lantian, Shaanxi, "transformed the customs of Guanzhong." The Village Covenant was a living convention by which Neo-Confucian scholars used their interpreted Confucian rituals and music to educate villagers, regulating their ways of thinking, behavior, and values. The clan was the organizational force for implementing the Village Covenant.

After the Song court moved south, Zhu Xi promoted the Lü-style Village Covenant in the south. The main clans of the Nanxi River mostly formed during this period, producing their own Neo-Confucian scholars who had contacts with Zhu Xi.

By the Ming dynasty, Wen Lin, who first served as magistrate of Yongjia and later as prefect of Wenzhou, formulated a set of "Clan Norms" in the eleventh year of Hongzhi (1498) during his tenure as prefect. Its basic content followed the same lineage as Lü's "Village Covenant," and was implemented in the five counties under Wenzhou. In the Nanxi River, "all clans responded," writing them into genealogies as "family instructions," "clan rules," or "clan norms."

The "Clan Norms" were the "fundamental law" of the clan, and its "Number of Instructors and Officials" was the clan's organizational law, with main content:

1. Clan Guest (note: no explanation).

2. Clan Worthy (note: no explanation).

3. Clan Head: The clan head is the leader of the clan, must first rectify himself then rectify others, must uphold perfect fairness to govern all clan members. ... Must ensure harmony between superiors and inferiors, jointly cultivating a generous atmosphere.

4. Clan Rectifiers: Establish four persons of both learning and conduct as clan rectifiers, to assist the clan head in his shortcomings. Whenever there is a clan matter, first consult the clan rectifiers, then report to the clan head, to distinguish right and wrong, analyze truth and falsehood, so that there is order without confusion, and also rectify customs and avoid disputes!

5. Chief Registrar: Select two upright young men as chief registrars, in charge of good and evil record books... Thus the methods of encouragement and punishment are established.

6. Director of Studies: Select one person of upright character and clear learning as director, solely responsible for instructing disciples, lecturing on poetry, books, ritual, and righteousness. ...

7. Director of Litigation: Select two persons of clear reasoning in the clan as directors of litigation. Whenever clan members have disputes, they report to the clan head and rectifiers; if persuasion fails, then lead them to official resolution, not lightly bringing cases to court.

8. Director of Relief: Two directors of relief. Whenever clan members or relatives and neighbors suffer poverty and hardship, and cannot afford funerals, marriages, etc., the director of relief reports to the clan head, persuading capable clan members to assist. ...

9. Director of Straightness: Select two persons of upright character and bold speech as directors of straightness. Whenever the clan head has faults, calmly point them out before the clan rectifiers for persuasion. Whenever clan rectifiers have faults, present them before the clan head for admonition. Whenever ordinary clan members have faults, lightly correct them directly, or heavily report to the clan head for reprimand, until they reform.

10. Director of Correction: Select seven impartial persons without fear of resentment as directors of correction. Whenever clan members do evil and ignore persuasion, the director of correction leads them to the ancestral temple for severe punishment by the clan head. If they still do not listen, then official punishment follows.

11. Monthly Rotation: Select one elder and one youth for monthly rotation. Within one month, all good and evil deeds and litigation matters not reported to the chief registrar must be inquired and reported to the respective officials, who record them in books and report to the clan head.

This "Number of Instructors and Officials" regulated the clan organization into a very complete political institution. The "Fenglin Xu Clan Genealogy · Eight Clan Norms" even stipulated that "ancestral family law" could be used to execute thieves "whose sins are deep and harmful, and whose nature ultimately refuses reform," ordering them to "end their own lives." Clans could also organize armed forces such as "local braves" and "righteous soldiers" when necessary to wage war.

Clan organizations were responsible for the construction and management of farmland water conservancy that no single household could handle. The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy · Clan Law" stipulated that clan-appointed persons "manage irrigation of rice fields, saving labor and water, avoiding disputes—this is the way to unite clans and harmonize kinship," and detailed the irrigation order and methods for various plots under different weather conditions.

Clans had public economies, including income from public fields, sacrificial fields, school fields, and charity fields. Genealogies strictly stipulated that public field property could not be illicitly sold.

Clans were also a powerful force for cultivating and maintaining social consciousness. Wen Lin's "Clan Norms" stipulated:

Whenever at spring and autumn sacrifices, or on the first and fifteenth days for visits, the clan head and rectifiers and below sit in order, having three disciples stand facing north, reading the Emperor Taizu's "Old Regulations." The text says: "Be filial to parents, respect elders, harmonize with neighbors, teach descendants, each settle in their livelihood, do not do wrong." All clan members kneel and listen. Then read Mr. Chen Guling's "Admonition Text," saying: "As our people, fathers are righteous, mothers kind, elder brothers friendly, younger brothers respectful, sons filial; husbands and wives have affection, men and women have distinction, young people have learning, villages have ritual; in poverty and hardship relatives rescue each other, in marriage and death neighbors help each other; do not be lazy in farming, do not be thieves, do not learn gambling, do not love litigation; do not use evil to bully good, do not use wealth to骄傲 poverty; travelers yield the road, farmers yield the field borders, gray-haired people do not carry burdens on the road—then it becomes a ritual and righteous custom." All clan members stand and listen.

Some genealogies stipulated even more solemn procedures, with three drum beats before each recitation. During recitation, "all must listen with awe; if anyone jokes or chats in the ranks, the clan rectifier brings it before the clan, meting out punishment as a warning."

Material force plus spiritual force made clan organizations very powerful, swallowing government institutions. Such powerful clan strength was sufficient to manage village planning and construction concerning the entire clan's interests. This was the main reason most Nanxi River villages had complete planning and well-managed public life centers.

However, except for the clan head appointed by convention "by age not by rank, by age not by status," all other positions from clan rectifier down could actually only be filled by educated, literate people. The result was that rural power was basically in the hands of local gentry and worthies. It can be said that their quality determined the village's fate and appearance. From various village genealogies, it can be seen that the more important construction achievements in villages were all proposed and directed by several public-spirited local gentry scholars, with organizational guarantee from the clan. The "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy" contains three important articles: "Record of the Long Embankment," "Record of the Ponds," and "Record of the New Wall," which detailed the construction and management rules of important projects concerning the whole village's safety.

2. The Role of Gentry

The local gentry and worthies of Nanxi River villages were mainly scholars who had resigned and returned home, those who had abandoned official aspirations, and those who had failed the examinations. They formed the leading stratum of rural society. They had received good cultural education and, in their leisure from scholarship and poetry, mostly cared for their villages and were happy to do "good deeds," thereby bringing their cultural cultivation into village construction.

Village planning was generally conducted at the initial founding, mostly lost in records, with no reliable materials long ago. But each clan's genealogy records many people who contributed to village construction, as well as some construction projects with long-term influence.

For example, in Yantou Village, the "Jin Clan Genealogy" records the second-generation ancestor Rixin Lord (born in the Bingzi year of Zhongtong of Yuan, died in the Wuzi year of Zhizheng of Yuan, i.e., 1348) constructing water conservancy: "At that time the land suffered from drought and waterlogging, with frequent poor harvests. The lord assessed the land and followed the water's nature, dredging two canals south of Dutou, reaching springs for irrigation, always obtaining abundant harvests; agriculture in both districts relies on it to this day." Later there was Guilin Lord (born in the Jiayin year of Hongzhi of Ming, died in the Jisi year of Longqing, 1494-1569), of whom the genealogy says: "Since the first migration to Yantou, ancestors had construction, but in the abundance of benefits and the greatness of merit, this lord should be ranked first." His actions were "cultivating fengshui, promoting geographical advantages, establishing sacrificial fields, building public enterprises." "The clan's land is rather high, fields suffer from drought; Lord Kai (i.e., Guilin Lord) donated old funds, excavating a long river for storage and drainage, and building high embankments, cultivating fengshui. Building pavilions and pagodas on it, upon completion, donated to the major ancestral hall as public property for the whole clan." Around the same time as Guilin Lord, Jin Zhao (Xiafeng Lord), a jinshi of Jiajing, built the Upper Garden, Lower Garden, and Major Ancestral Hall, and also built a memorial archway for himself. With several people successively engaging in village construction, Yantou Village's water system, streets, public buildings, and gardens are the most outstanding in the Nanxi River.

Cangpo Village and Furong Village have rigorous planning, complete water systems, and orderly streets; Cangpo Village also has large gardens—these also relied on historically enthusiastic builders in both villages. Building a long embankment to store water into a lake in Cangpo was around the Southern Song (1178), nearly four hundred years earlier than Yantou. Youxu Lord of the Chen clan in Furong Village (born in the Renshen year of Kangxi, died in the Guiyou year of Qianlong, 1692-1753), "had economic talent, studied and understood righteousness. ... Happy to give. Created temples, built ancestral halls, increased sacrificial fields, built boats for crossing, built embankments for irrigation. What he did in his life, benefiting the clan, is too numerous to list" ("Liangyuan Chen Clan Genealogy").

The "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy" contains a "Record of the Long Embankment," recording that in the early Qing, in the spring of Dingmao of Qianlong (1747), clan member Zheng Xixian donated funds and organized labor to build a dike. The record writes:

Tangchuan's scenic area is at the confluence of two streams, embracing distant mountains and swallowing long flows, with the wonder of nine Leifeng peaks in front and the precipice of Horse Stone Heavenly Rock behind; as for Screen Mountain receiving the sun and Harmony Clouds lingering, everywhere is remarkable. Only the road lacked a dike; although tall trees cast shade and water murmurs, travelers often suffered. Now stones are piled into a dike, sparing no effort, from East Ridge to West Ridge, about several tens of zhang, shaped like a river embankment, flat enough for carts.

Besides water conservancy, ancestral halls, temples, and roads, academies were traditionally the focus of village construction, attracting the most attention from gentry. The "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy · Biography of Lord Shenxuan" says the lord "was happy to do good throughout his life, and was especially devoted to study; once concerned that clan youth had no place to study, unable to cultivate talent and promote later learning, he generously donated funds, assisting renowned local scholars to build the two wings of Wenchang Pavilion as a place for lectures." Some had special interest in environmental greening, such as Zhu Wengweng of Huatan Village, "loved reading, simple but not stagnant, only seeking worthy friends for self-improvement. ... In the early Hongwu, when hermits were summoned, the county strongly recommended him to the capital, appointing him Court Gentleman; he begged to return, was permitted, and his reputation grew even greater. Built ancestral hall, established sacrificial fields to worship ancestors. Built a house at Fang'ao, planted tens of thousands of pine trees for shelter, hence calling himself Yunsong (Cloud Pine). ... Roaming freely among clouds and pines, discussing past and present, playing the qin and composing poetry" ("Epitaph of the Ming-appointed Court Gentleman Lord Yunsong").

These local worthies, in vernacular construction, of course mainly played roles of proposal, planning, and donation; as for project direction, it relied on clan strength. For example, in the sixth month of the twenty-first year of Kangxi (1682), to rebuild the Qukou Ye Clan Major Ancestral Hall, initiated by clan member Ye Jian and others, "consulting with both branches, respecting the ancestors' founding spirit, each contributed own funds to rebuild the ancestral hall. Selected the sixth day of the eighth month of this year to start cutting wood, and the first day of the second month of next spring to erect. On that day estimated: how much timber, how much rafters, how much labor, approximately six hundred taels of silver." The two branches shared proportionally by population: the major branch four hundred taels, the second branch two hundred taels. Rough labor was the major branch's responsibility. This matter was clearly directed by the clan (see "Quchuan Ye Clan Genealogy · Ye Clan Contract Agreement").

Some clans wrote bridge and road repair as an obligation into family rules, such as the "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy · Family Rules": "When bridges, roads, or ferry boats fall into disrepair, descendants with surplus funds should assist in repair." In the "Qukou Ye Clan Genealogy" there are two texts: "Record of the Stone Railing at Shima Rock" and "Record of the Stair Rock at Qianshan." The former records a mountain road only four chi wide with "steep cliffs above and deep pools below," where "night travelers sometimes fell, the danger beyond description." So a group of villagers "collected large funds, building stone railings for several tens of zhang. From then on, the place turned danger into safety." The latter records that in front of Qukou Village there was "a famous Stair Rock, six zhang high, the only path for ascent and descent, steep without steps. Woodcutters reaching there all trembled with fear"; also a group of good people raised funds to hire stone masons, "carving steps into the steep place like a staircase." This genealogy also contains over twenty records of road repair.

Recording these good deeds in genealogies was to praise the parties, "passing them on forever," as examples for descendants. The clan used this lofty honor to encourage people with surplus wealth to engage in village construction. For example, the "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy · Family Rules" records: "When ancestral halls and grave houses slightly fall into disrepair, promptly discuss repair, drawing temporarily from grave rents and hall rents for expenses. If filial descendants donate to assist, record them in the genealogy." Some even added stone inscriptions beyond genealogy records. For example, the "Heyang Xie Clan Genealogy" records the fifth-generation ancestor Duanxian Lord "valued morality and justice, was generous with wealth, dredged deep harbors to clear blockages, benefiting the village. Hence carved stone at Heling Stream bank, inscribed: 'In the fifth year of Shaoxi of Song (1194), Xie the Eighteenth Lay Buddhist opened the harbor'—twelve characters to record his merit." Generally, temple and stone bridge construction also had donors' names carved on steles or bridges. Most outstanding was Guilin Lord of Yantou Village; after his death, villagers, to commemorate his great contribution to village construction, converted the academy he founded into a dedicated shrine in his honor, called the Water Pavilion Shrine.

Local scholars, either directly or through clan organizations, engaged in village construction, transforming their cultural cultivation and landscape sentiment into these constructions. In Nanxi River villages, cultural buildings were many in type and number, with architectural styles that were generous and elegant, without luxurious carving, and pursued harmony between architecture and natural landscapes. For example, the Wenchang Pavilion in Huatan Village built during Qianlong, "its gate faces winding water, its ground rises to the clouds, embracing the sudden rise of peaks and facing the lush green of ten thousand trees—the beauty of Zhenchuan is ranked first here. Those ascending this pavilion find different morning and evening delights, endless seasonal interests, literary thoughts flourishing, insight expanding" ("Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy · Biography of Lord Shenxuan"), aiming for literary inspiration amid scenic mountains and rivers, naturally fresh and elegant.

3. The Role of Fengshui Masters

Fengshui (geomancy) theories had great influence on the planning and construction of Nanxi River villages, and yin-yang masters or geomancers mostly came from the local scholar class. Therefore, fengshui theory was another途径 by which local gentry influenced rural planning and construction; yin-yang masters or geomancers actually played a role similar to planners to some extent, but what they relied on was superstition.

Guilin Lord, who made major contributions to Yantou Village's construction during the Jiajing period of Ming, was actually a geomancer. The "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy · Biography of Guilin Lord" says he "repeatedly failed examinations, then turned to study the green bag (fengshui), selecting house and grave sites." Legend says he also received help from Li Zishi, a "national teacher" and advisor under the Prince of Ning. What he built may all have undergone fengshui consideration. Around the same time as Guilin Lord, some gentry studied astrology, such as a certain Jiufeng Lord in Yantou Village during Jiajing, who "was well-versed in all classics, histories, and various schools of thought, and furthermore studied astrology, enjoyed music; as for examination essays and song lyrics, they were his remaining skills" ("Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy · Preface to Lord Jiufeng's Fiftieth Birthday"). In the mid-Qing during Xianfeng, there was a certain Xie Wenbo in Pengxi Village; the genealogy has a "Preface to Lord Xie Wenbo's Sixtieth Birthday" saying: "In recent hundred years, customs valued force, but only Wenbo was devoted to inner conduct, loved books and history, learned broadly, had many books at home with shelves full; in the studies of Renqi (divination), astrology, phonology, and fanqie, and also in qin, chess, and calligraphy, he was proficient, far surpassing others in elegance." This gentleman's works include "Dongou Miscellany," "Finding Characters by Sound," "Correction of Four Tones," "Fanqie Method," and "Herbal Manual," etc.

These records show that those who studied fengshui, Renqi, and astrology were all Confucian scholars well-read in classics. Confucianism was upper-class elegant culture, while numerology was lower-class folk culture, originally belonging to different categories. However, as early as when Confucianism was made the sole doctrine in the Han, it had already been combined with yin-yang and five elements theories. From then on, Confucian scholars studied fengshui, Renqi, and astrology as general cultural knowledge, not seeing fundamental contradiction with orthodox Confucian spirit. Only when superstition seemed too much like "strange forces, chaos, and spirits," contrary to reason, was it refuted—for example, long-term postponement of burial, "using the dead to pray for blessings for the living," was severely criticized.

Fengshui is superstitious heresy, but yin-yang masters and gentry directing village construction were knowledgeable people of their time and place. They had dual personalities: they could be foolish and ridiculous on some issues, but due to their rich experience, they could also make practical judgments on some issues, such as village site selection, water system planning, village layout, defense works, street networks, and the position and orientation of important buildings—possibly giving some good suggestions. To protect their profession, or merely to增强 their confidence in their practical judgments and the persuasiveness of their suggestions to the public, they attached their experiential knowledge to the mysterious "theory" of fengshui. The wall-building and pond-digging in Tangwan Village were obviously for urgent practical needs, but both were attached with a set of fengshui explanations. It seems these explanations, though baseless talk, helped call villagers to unite, contribute money and labor, and accomplish some public welfare projects.

Villages in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River, such as Cangpo, Yantou, and Furong, all circulate stories of "national teacher" Li Zishi planning villages. For example, it is said that in the fifth year of Chunxi of Southern Song (1178), Li Song, the ninth-generation ancestor of Cangpo Village, invited Li Zishi to plan the village wall, streets, ponds, and canals. He made the main street point directly at the distant Brush Rack Mountain to the west, naming it Brush Street to benefit examination success. But Brush Rack Mountain also resembled flames; to prevent Brush Street from bringing fire to the village, two ponds were built at its eastern end, using water to overcome fire. One pond was called Inkstone Pool; then the village was associated with paper, two stone strips with ink ingots, together with Brush Street forming the "Four Treasures of the Study"—even more beneficial for examination success. The clan used this baseless talk to激励 descendants to study diligently.

Another example: during the Jiajing period of Ming, Guilin Lord invited Li Zishi to plan Yantou Village. He asked Guilin Lord whether he wanted "rapid prosperity" or "slow prosperity." Rapid prosperity meant enclosing the entire surname's settlement within the village wall, allowing prosperity within several generations but with limited future; slow prosperity meant enclosing mixed-surname settlements within the wall, with slower prosperity but more prosperous future. Guilin Lord chose "rapid prosperity," and the village wall is in its existing condition. Li Zishi also mystified by saying that Yushan (Islet Mountain) east of the village was transformed from a giant python, harmful to villagers; therefore, four east-west narrow lanes were planned in the village, symbolizing four "arrows," two lanes branching at the east end, and one winding lane at the east end symbolizing two and a half "halberds"—these seven "weapons" subdued the Yushan python, protecting villagers' safety.

According to legend, when Furong Village was rebuilt in the first year of Zhizheng of Yuan (1341), it was also Li Zishi who read fengshui and made the plan.

These stories are absurd, and one "national teacher" Li Zishi existing from Southern Song through Yuan until Jiajing is obviously fictional. But these stories do show that there were indeed such people making suggestions on village planning issues, whether reasonable or delusional.

Although some local gentry engaged in fengshui, some had already noticed its fatalistic negative effects. Confucian culture, which does not speak of "strange forces, violence, chaos, and spirits," had strong critical ability toward fengshui as folk culture. For example, the "Snow Heart Fu," falsely attributed to Zhu Xi, vigorously advocated fengshui, saying "ministers and marquises all come from this; glory and wealth all come from it." However, this fatalism not only had no ethical educational significance but even hindered Confucian ethical education, destroying Confucian educational values. Therefore, Zhu Xi wrote in "Supplement to the Great Learning · Rituals of Home and Village": "The world has selection methods existing, which cannot but be used to replace divination. But its so-called fortune and blessing cannot be trusted, and its sayings of seeking good and avoiding evil cannot be accepted." Fengshui practitioners also had sayings like "spiritual mountains and spiritual caves are inhabited by the virtuous," and "a good mountain site is not as good as a good heart." Even the "Snow Heart Fu" says: "To seek Lord Teng's good city, one must accumulate Lord Shu's hidden virtue," "the cave is naturally formed, fortune is created by the heart," and even says: "accumulating virtue必定 obtains auspicious relocation, accumulating evil still brings凶 land"—again negating fengshui's decisive role. This self-contradiction in fengshui theory reflects the contradiction between orthodox elegant culture and folk culture. Folk culture contains many shamanistic elements, while elegant culture cannot but value Confucian ritual and music education, as this is necessary for maintaining social order.

Nanxi River gentry, on one hand superstitious about fengshui, on the other hand more or less criticized fatalism to maintain Confucian ethics. For example, by the mid-Qing, Yuzhang Village's literary fortune declined; in the "Preface to Rebuilding the Yuzhang Hu Clan Genealogy," recalling the Song dynasty when "at that time one family had three generations with five jinshi," and by Ming producing "the thirteenth-generation ancestor Zongyun Lord promoted to Grand Secretary of Wenyuan Pavilion and Secretary of the Central Secretariat," receiving the emperor's favor of bestowed food, then saying: "People all say Yuzhang's mountains and rivers are the most beautiful in the two sources, causing this. I say, if so, why is the present not like the past? Is it that the spiritual beauty of mountains and rivers only favored the past and not the present? Although in the Kangxi period of this dynasty the land was damaged by water, yet when human effort is exhausted, heaven's will can be turned; if one can rise to read Confucius's books and follow the Duke of Zhou's rituals, one can still turn misfortune into fortune and decay into renewal." These words refuted fengshui clearly and forcefully.

Another example: during Qianlong, Zhu Pengxuan of Langxia Village selected a fengshui auspicious site for Wenchang Pavilion; after relocation, he proudly said: "But what assists fortune is the divine, what nurtures spirituality is the land; embracing beauty and gathering elegance, is it not a great benefit to our village?" But he had to turn back and say some remedial words: "Our village's Zhu clan, since Song and Ming, has had examination graduates generation after generation, yet at that time there was no shrine. If a scholar thoroughly studies classics and ancient learning, with aspirations not deviating from sages, then when thoughts arise and actions begin, the divine naturally descends with auspiciousness to assist—what does a mere shrine matter?" (See "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy · Record of Rebuilding Wenchang Pavilion").

According to Guangxu's "Yongjia County Chronicle," Ming prefects Wen Lin, He Wenyuan, and other learned local gentry once struggled against shamanistic cults, divination, and fengshui, explicitly banning them. The "County Chronicle" regarded the prevalence of these superstitions as a sign of moral degeneration. However, in an era of very low examination success rates, this struggle had little effect, later leaving only a few sentences in the "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy · Clan Norms" written according to Wen Lin's instructions:

The dead find peace in the grave; dying without burial is like traveling without reaching destination. Therefore, although filial sons love their parents, they dare not keep them long unburied, because the parents have not found peace; hence they sleep on straw and blocks, themselves not daring peace. As descendants, one should uphold correct reason, not be trapped by fengshui restrictions or confused by yin-yang masters' taboos on dates, ... causing the deceased's spirit to be long exposed,反而 harming righteousness and offending one's parents.

Confucian ethics was the highest behavioral norm in feudal society; it was rational and realistic, aiming to maintain normal social life order. Fengshui, after all, is superstition, unverifiable; although it can exploit people's ignorance and seem to work everywhere, its existence and role were conditional on not harming the actual operation of Confucian ethics. Therefore, fengshui could only play an important decisive role in the unfathomable selection of grave sites; in village planning and construction, its role was often to附会, revise, and occasionally be used to promote public works with practical benefits.

4. The Role of Craftsmen

The craft system of Nanxi River architecture and their traditional methods of design, calculation, construction, and related folk customs can no longer be researched. However, it is reasonable to infer that in a long-term small-scale peasant natural economy, craftsmen's specialization was not high, they did not form economic entities, passed skills through father-son and master-apprentice relationships, and had some trade customs.

Craftsmen were mostly not scholars or wealthy people, nor could they assist in good deeds, so they were not recorded in genealogies. Occasionally a few, because of contributions to construction and outstanding character, gained a few lines of ink. For example, the "Liangyuan Chen Clan Genealogy" records a Ming craftsman: "Lord Youfu, courtesy name Youru, art name Lanxuan, incomparably ingenious, with the skill to build phoenix pavilions and dragon towers. Poor and unable to study, yet understood righteousness. In the Wuzi year, when building the Wenguang Ancestral Hall, he used unique craftsmanship, not sparing hardship." Another, also Ming, recorded in the "Tanxia Chen Clan Genealogy": "Named Shishang, courtesy name Zhaolin. Gentle in manner, harmonious with all. From youth learned the trade of Lu Ban (master carpenter), specializing in it, assessing materials and measuring timber, perfectly fitting dimensions. Therefore, for building new houses near and far, he was employed. To this day his successors continue, and the family gradually prospers; yet he respected ancestors and clan, and for any righteous undertaking, spared no effort." From "unique craftsmanship" and "assessing materials and measuring timber," these craftsmen were master builders who also served as designers.

Due to father-son and master-apprentice传承, each trade of the building industry had specialized villages. For example, "major carpenters come from Luokeng, stone masons from Zhongbao, tile kilns from Jingren, plasterers from Lüzhang."

Although there were specialized villages, craftsmen's specialization was still not high. Major carpenters also made beds, cabinets, desks, qin stools, even looms and windmills; plasterers also built stoves; stone masons also made mortars, millstones, pillars, memorial archways, various stone implements, even carved inscriptions. Wall masons' trade was especially mixed: building stone arch bridges, paving roads, building stepping stones, piling village walls, digging tunnels, laying field banks, etc. What most brought these craftsmen's skills into full play was making various decorations. Plasterers molded birds, beasts, figures, landscapes, lions, and qilin. "Piling a dragon looks exactly like a dragon, molding a phoenix looks like a phoenix in flight." Stone masons carved these themes, and also "hollowed out lions playing with balls." Wall masons used cobblestones to inlay various patterns on the ground, called "inserted stone flowers."

About these crafts, the Nanxi River has ballads. What elegant-culture genealogies did not record, folk culture passed down generation after generation. Folk culture was basically the culture of laborers. A ballad "The Master Carpenter" goes:

The master carpenter's skill is exquisite, controlling the measuring rod with clarity.

The square and ink line are accurate, marking beams and columns with precise dimensions.

Double-retreat houses with side wings, can build tall buildings and great halls.

Beams stacked on beams, brackets stacked on brackets, red-oil railings with carved flower names.

Palaces, temples, and monasteries built as antiques, pavilions, towers, and octagonal wells.

...

The measuring rod, or design ruler, had all the dimensions of the entire house marked on it. It was equivalent to an architectural design drawing; craftsmen relied on it to build double-retreat residences, official temples, monasteries, pavilions, and towers of various types and forms, including fairly complex bracket sets. However, taking pride in building "antiques" reflects the conservative psychology of agricultural society, showing life's stagnation and the narrow vision of small peasants.

A long ballad details the house-building process and craftsmen's labor:

Deep in the mountains agarwood is harvested, Lu Ban the patriarch builds the new hall.

Choosing an auspicious day, construction begins without stopping.

The master carpenter comes to work, drawing the design and setting the house form.

The stone mason sets the foundation stones, yin-yang orientation meeting an auspicious star.

On a clear auspicious day timber is assembled, much effort arranged.

On an auspicious day mortises are cut, bamboo craftsmen bind column tops tight.

Erecting columns meets an auspicious day, placing the beam meets the Purple Star.

Beams stacked on beams, brackets stacked on brackets, flower windows on all sides reflecting brightness.

Roof covered with rolling rafters and tiles, floor paved with jade bricks in the Big Dipper pattern.

On all sides beast heads are erected, mirrors embedded in walls shining brightly.

Nine-bend winding corridors go around, flat pools with boards are most precious.

Exquisite flower windows are wide, desks under windows ready-made.

Front and back suppress the door, opening and closing makes phoenix sounds.

In front a pavilion plants phoenix-perching bamboo, behind a pool raises dragon-transforming fish.

Building houses concerns fengshui, gathering wealth and prospering the family.

Two points in this ballad are noteworthy: first, it explicitly says the carpenter "drew the design and set the house form"; second, it explicitly says the stone mason "did yin-yang orientation meeting an auspicious star," this work done when "setting foundation stones." It seems stone masons managed fengshui within individual houses, possibly following rules in the "Lu Ban Jing," because earlier it says "Lu Ban the patriarch builds the new hall." The "Song of Thirty-Six Trades" opens: "Lu Ban the patriarch is the true master, transmitting craftsmen's exquisite skills."

This ballad also expresses villagers' aesthetic ideals for architecture and praise for craftsmen's skills."

本节术语 Key Terms

宗族组织 血缘村落 门阀制度 义田 乡约 吕大钧 朱熹 文林 族范 族长 族正 乡勇 义兵 学田 祭田 士绅 桂林公 金昭 水亭祠 堪舆师 地理师 阴阳师 李自实 文房四宝 笔架山 鲁班经 工匠 大木老司 丈杆 专业村

第二篇 规划篇

Part Two: Planning
四、选址和水系
中文原文重构

四、选址和水系

楠溪江中游古村落的建设大都经过完整的规划。建设得比较早，并且把早期的规划基本保存到现在的，有苍坡村、塘湾村、芙蓉村和岩头村等。苍坡于955年建村，从北宋至和二年（1055年）起，重要的建设《苍坡李氏宗谱》都有记载，包括南宋淳熙五年（1178年）的一次全面的规划。这些记载的情况与苍坡村的现状大体符合，水系、主街和公共建筑一一都在。例如淳熙五年九世祖李嵩在村东南筑坝蓄水形成东池、西池。李嵩死后，夫人浣溪刘氏继续完成了寨墙、笔街和寨门，并挖了环坪水渠。塘湾村始建于宋代，《棠川（按即塘湾）郑氏宗谱》里有一则资料，提到绍兴年间的前街、中街，它们至今还是塘湾村的主要街道。芙蓉村也初建于宋代，南宋末因抗元而被完全焚毁，元至正元年（1341年）重建，大体保持了原来的格局。岩头村的规划在明代中叶，清代中叶被大规模破坏，但原状还可以追迹，它的水系和园林绿地在楠溪江村落里是成就最高的。

虽然没有文献资料，从现状看，规划的主要内容包括选址、水系（沟洫渠道、池塘、井）、布局（街巷网、功能分区、公共中心、园林绿地）、防御系统（寨墙、溪门、谯亭）等。这些规划内容的一部分项目，在其他地区的村落里也可能见到，但全面综合地实施它们，而且如此整齐，在其他地区就很少见，因而成为当今楠溪江村落的重要特色。至于局部地保存下来的规划建设就几乎村村都有了。

1.选址

楠溪江的村落，在建村之初，都很重视选址。选址的主要考虑，是自给自足的自然经济之下的农业生产和生活。影响生产和生活的基本元素是水、地、山。附会水、地、山对居民凶吉祸福关系的堪舆风水之说相当活跃。

楠溪江人对生存环境的认识是很全面而有综合性的。《渠川叶氏宗谱·重合族谱牒告成附书于后》写道：

渠口，吾祖光宗公发祥之所也。阅世三十有三，历年千百有馀。围绕者数百家，沿缘者七八里。凤山翥其西，雷峰峙其东，南有屿山，而其外有大溪环之。中穿一渠，可以灌田。而其北则层峦叠翠，不一其状。有径可通四处，田高下横邃，布列如画挂然。泉流涓涓，声与耳谋。地僻非僻，山贫不贫。有檀可采，有秫可种，有美可茹，有鲜可食。桑麻蔽野，禾稼连畦。迴笋而地挑衣，越茶天而焙牛，民赖其利益已久矣！（道光二十年三十一世裔孙举东志）

这一段描写，写到山川形势、交通、水利、农业、林业和渔业。《荀子·强国》里说："其固塞险，形势便，山林川谷美，天材之利多，是形胜也。"渠口正是这样一个形胜之地，它现在是楠溪江中游的大村之一。

（1）村落选址的第一个考虑是要有足够的可耕地，使农产可以自给。正如《管子·度地》所说："圣人之处国者，必于不倾之地而择其形之肥饶者，……乃以其天材，地之所生，利养其人，以育六畜。"

堪舆名著《雪心赋》说到"发福之大居"应是"明堂平旷，万象森罗，众水归朝，诸山聚会，草盛木繁，水深土厚"，也是这个意思。

楠溪江中游的村落，大多分布在河谷冲积平地上。这里土层肥沃，水源丰沛，交通便利，农业生产的条件最好。两侧的山坡，又可以育薪炭林和果木林。条件尤其好的是地形比较开阔的盆地，其中最大的是以岩头村为中心的盆地，这个盆地里村落最多、最密、港头村比较大。除了岩头村之外，重要的有苍坡村、霞美村、周宅村、港头村、渡头村、芙蓉村、溪南村和枫林村等等，都是楠溪江人文最发达的村落。它们的主姓从福建迁来，建村比较早，而且经济、文化一直处于领先地位。其他几个人文发达的大村落，也大多位于小盆地中心，如花坛村、溪口村、桐州村、渠口村、豫章村等等。《珍川朱氏合族副谱》里有一篇《伯清公珍川十咏序》，描写花坛村的环境道：

陵阜夹川，陂陀下弛，衍为原隰。林麓蔽荫，水田环绕，居民耕植其中，熙熙如也。……是盖乾坤清澈之气所锺聚融结，必有玮瑰俊秀杰出乎其间。

由于水土好，有利于农业经济的发展，就能培育出人才来。花坛村确实是个人才辈出的地方，尤其在明代。

宽敞的盆地里，村落大多占用平地，形态集中而且方正。狭窄的河谷平川里，村落大多傍山坡建造，让出宝贵的耕地。而且村落规模不大，顺等高线呈带形。例如蓬溪村、埭头村和水云村。水云村的谷地稍稍宽一点，以致《大岩岩志》说它："村民多集于溪西大岩岩北马鞍山麓，土地平旷，山泉下注，涧别渠分，阡陌交通。屋舍比连，安居乐业者二百馀户，大小宗祠檐垣相望也。"

有少数村落，例如周宅村，它北侧的滩头村和南侧的港头村，为了让出耕地，造在河滩上。它们相接呈带形，甚至与南侧的方巷村连接了起来，长度在两公里以上。

有一些沿江村落很少农田，就靠发展手工业，如西岸村，位于一座小山和江流之间，村民以制作粉干，运销温州，辅助收入。还有一些则做木工、烧窑、使船等。

（2）村落选址的第二个考虑是避水害，抗洪排涝。楠溪江流域年年夏季都有台风暴雨。每逢暴雨，江水骤涨，奔腾咆哮，摧毁力极强。康熙年间的一次大水，就把豫章村临江的"外宅"部分一冲而光，至今还是一个积沙的高地。

为了防洪，除了要有足够的高程之外，弯弯曲曲的江流两岸，村落大都造在沉积岸一侧而避开冲刷岸。按照堪舆风水的说法，就是村落前面要有"腰带水"，不可以有"反弓水"。堪舆书《山龙语类》说："反背水，形如反弓，一名反跳水。此水漏泄堂气，无情之水也。"相传为刘基著的《堪舆漫兴》里写道："金城弯曲抱身，如如月弓产凤麟；若是反弓不揖冢，石崇富豪亦须贫。"金城水也叫玉带水或腰带水。《阳宅十书》说："门前若有玉带水，高官必定容易起；出入代代读书声，荣显富贵耀门闾。"鹤湾、鹤垟、鹤盛、东皋、枫林、西岸、桐州、碧莲、豫章、花坛、廊下等等许多大一点的村子都处于"腰带水"的位置。蓬溪村虽然北面对鹤盛溪的反弓，但迎着反弓的是霞港头坚硬高耸的石壁，而一条不大的小溪却在蓬溪村的东面形成腰带，并且潴而成大约2000平方米的一个湖，在风水上正好是"水聚天心"，大吉。芙蓉村在南宋时候曾经出过十八位高级京官，村人至今津津乐道那"十八金带"，归因于芙蓉村的"前横腰带水，后枕纱帽岩，三龙捧珠，四水归心"。芙蓉村陈氏大宗祠里有一副楹联，写的是：

地枕三崖崖吐名花明昭万古；

门临象水水生秀气荣荫千秋。

说的就是这个风水。

关于沉积岸和冲刷岸影响村落安危的认识，就这样被风水迷信吞没了。风水师说"腰带水"有情，仅仅从它的环抱姿态引申而来，幸好没有违背常识的判断。

因为楠溪江流域多火山流纹岩，质地坚硬，有的地段，"反弓水"对江岸的冲刷危险不大，所以仍有一些村落造在"反弓水"的位置。至于风水上的凶煞，可以用一些办法来"破除"，例如，迎着"反弓水"造一座关帝庙就足以"逢凶化吉"了。蓬溪村面对鹤盛溪的"反弓水"，就是这样处理的。关帝是"伏魔大帝"，是"恩主公"，能够镇辟一切灾祸。

村落造在沉积岸，除了可以减弱洪水对地基的冲刷之外，"腰带水"还能够形成一个村落的自然边界，从而造成领域感。领域感会加强村落居民的内聚倾向，这对于血缘村落来说是很重要的。自然边界也有利于减少邻村之间的纠纷。

（3）选址的第三个重要考虑是安全。这是因为，许多村落的始迁祖都是为了避乱才来到楠溪江的，而楠溪江又并非世外桃源。例如，下园村的始祖为避晚唐黄巢之乱而来；苍坡、枫林、溪口、芙蓉等不少村落的始祖为避五代末年闽国王延钧之乱而来；渠口村始祖在北宋末为避方腊之乱而来；豫章村始祖是随宋室南渡辗转江西而来的。他们饱经离乱，千里颠簸，来到楠溪江，为的是找一块平静的土地，休养生息。所以，他们时常钟情于闭塞的环境，或者易守难攻的环境。

一个例子是蓬溪村。它四面被高山峻岭包围，只在北面有个小小口子却又被鹤盛溪阻隔。要进入这个袋形谷地，唯一的路是架设在溪边悬崖峭壁上的栈道。

坦下村和塘湾村是另一类例子。它们三面环山，山势陡峭，不能通行。另一面不宽，筑起高峻的寨墙，很利于防守。为了选择这个地形，竟不顾村子的朝向，以致坦下村的住宅都朝西南而塘湾村的住宅绝大多数朝东北，虽然两村隔岸相望。

还有一些村子，背山面水，只有踏过长长的石矶步，才能到达寨门。如廊下村、东皋村、白岩村、鹤垟村等等。西岸村造在山之东、水之西，山水合围，加上一段寨墙，唯一的寨门开在山的南麓，形势十分险固。

选择这种闭塞的，或易守难攻的环境建村，固然是为了安全，但有时也是为了遁世深隐。花坛村的始祖祖操隐公，南宋时在永嘉任县尉，《珍川朱氏宗谱·始祖操隐翁朱公墓志》说他：

见世荒乱，民多聚盗，弃官不仕，家于温。初居城东花柳塘。初欲隐，但目击理乱，关心竟不能释。再迁罗浮，而大乱扣城。对其子曰："此不足以隐吾迹矣！"东观西望，乃定居于清通乡之珍川。其地山明水秀，禽鸟和鸣，林深谷邃，景物幽清。乃置功名于度外，付理乱于不闻。

后来果然"陶然林下"，过着桃花源里的生活。

有些村落位于开阔地上，没有天险可守，就只好全仗筑墙了，例如芙蓉村、岩坦村。

正是由于村落整体的设防，才使村落内部房屋能够开敞外向，一片太平景象。这是血缘村落的特点之一。

（4）选址的第四个重要考虑是要有良好的小气候。楠溪江各地的小气候，因为地形多变，差别很大，因此，村落选址的时候，就要选择比较有利的小气候。相传为金丞相兀钦仄注的《青鸟先生葬经》说，理想的居住环境应该是"草木郁茂，吉气相随。……或本来空缺通风，今有草木郁茂，遮其不足，不觉空缺。故生气自然，草木充塞，又自人为"。说的是好的小气候有利于草木生长，草木生长又能改善小气候，当然有利于农业生产和居民的日常生活。

鹤垟村的始祖谢氏洗五五公，从郡城迁来楠溪江塘下村，有一天，"雪后登山，望见兰台山前积雪先融，遂定居焉，后果蕃昌"（见《鹤垟谢氏宗谱》）。鹤垟村在一个被"腰带水"紧紧从三面抱住的向阳山坡上，前有"鹤溪漾碧"，后有"锦屏叠翠"，土地高燥，光照充足，所以"积雪先融"。

渠口村的发展也是适应了当地的小气候。村子的东、西、北三面有山环抱，挡住冬季的寒风。南面不远处有一座小山，比较低矮，在东南和西南形成两个豁口。一到夏季，季候风沿楠溪江干流北上，一部分到坦下村顺小楠溪河谷折向西北，正好吹进东南豁口。所以渠口村向西发展，迎着季候风，而在东部植林，因为这位置吹不到季候风，却正迎着西晒太阳的炙烤。

隔小楠溪相对的坦下村和塘湾村相距不远，一朝西南而一朝东北，朝西南的坦下村固然有"三阳"高照，朝东北的塘湾村，则因为在漫长而燠热的夏季可以有季候风而补偿了阳光不足的缺憾。

（5）村落选址的第五个重要考虑是风景优美。溪口戴氏大宗祠的门联，有一副：

水秀山明常出仁智；地灵人杰永传声名。

好山水就是好风水，好风水就出好子弟。

楠溪江本来就以风景之美著称，几乎处处都有好景致。从谢灵运、陶弘景以来，楠溪江人的环境意识很强，山水情怀成为地方的文化传统。宋、明两朝，楠溪江中游许多村落文风极盛，受过较高文化教养的乡村士绅们，对自然之美有很敏锐的感受力，科名之余，他们流连于秀水明山之间，吟咏不辍。这种文化氛围，当然影响到他们对村落的选址。

例如一座芙蓉峰，有三块高耸峭拔的悬崖，就有下园村、芙蓉村、岩头村、溪南村都拿它做建村的地标，拿它做乡里风光的借景。《岩头金氏宗谱》记载，岩头村始迁祖安福公（生宋淳祐庚戌，卒元延祐戊午，1250-1318年）"始居西巷，……延祐间来相芙蓉三岩之胜，遂居焉。"又例如塘湾，也是因为风景优美而被郑氏始祖迁祖选中的。《棠川郑氏宗谱·重修棠川郑氏宗谱序》记载，始迁祖"至其地，见夫奇峰突兀，怪石峥嵘，面临雷壁，背枕天岩。九峰围屏，共巽山而拱秀；双溪环带，合曲涧而流芬。福地奚多让乎？"于是在这里建村定居。

各个村落的宗谱里，都有非常夸饰的文字盛赞本村风景的奇特和秀美，字里行间，洋溢着对乡里的浓烈感情。《蓬溪谢氏宗谱·同治甲子（1864年）重修族谱序》描绘山川形胜道：

楠溪形胜，惟蓬川最奇。迎逆流四十馀里，过棠洄荡漾，潴而后泄。守水口者，则有若狮、象、龟、鱼，突怒峭竖，险恶畏人。又有文笔峰撑寿星岩，镇屏山对列赢屿。横槛诸如观音坐莲、美女梳妆、鹰捕蛇、狮捉象。仰天湖、瀑布泉、将军、仙人、牛鼻、虎头、燕巢、鸡冠等胜，亦皆秀异可观。余足迹所及，历数之，未有过于此者也。

蓬溪村的风景确实不寻常。它位于一个袋形盆地的西侧，四面被姿态奇幻的峰峦包围。盆地东部，正中有一座孤立的小山，树木茂盛，叫做凤凰屿。一条小溪自南向北流，在凤凰屿南麓汇成一个2000平方米的湖。然后夺路在盆地北端唯一的阙口处注入楠溪江的支流鹤盛溪。鹤盛溪从东来，向北去，在阙口处急转弯，刚刚把狭窄的阙口堵住。出入蓬溪唯一的路是溪西龙泉山峭壁上的一条栈道。村口也是水口，叫霞港头，有一座关帝庙，既为关锁水口，也为镇住"反弓水"。庙前有浓荫蔽天的古樟树，庙北则有"船崖"，巨石累累从削壁上迤逦而下，直趋水中。崖上镌刻"钓台"两个字，旁边摩崖刻诗：

观鱼胜濠上，把钓超渭阳；

严子如来此，定忘富春江。

凤凰屿南面临湖的岩壁上，有"把钓"、"索觞"两个摩崖石刻。湖东有圆锥形的"文笔峰"，从村里望去，倒影正好映在湖中，因而湖被称为"墨沼"，与文笔峰形成了"文笔蘸墨"的风水，认为大有利于科甲。

鹤垟、豫章、塘湾、上烘头、岭下、花坛、埭头、珠岸等等村子，也都是风景极好，而且各不相同。

（6）村落选址的第六个重要考虑是风水。风水之说，贯穿在前面的各项考虑里，不过，它还有单独的作用。

例如，形法派的堪舆家最推崇的一种环境模式是，背后有依托（祖山、少祖山）、左右山势均衡（左辅、右弼）、前景开阔（明堂）并且有屏障（案山、朝山）。由于楠溪江河谷地势局促，地貌复杂，符合这种模式的环境并不多。不过也并非没有，渠口村就是一个例子。

渠口村北靠霁山为祖山，东西以雷峰和凤山为辅弼，南面平展而有一座不大的虎屿山作为案山。虎屿山之南隔小楠溪有屏风形的前山为朝山。《渠川叶氏宗谱》里说，始迁祖叶恳在北宋末年方腊起义时，为寻找参与起义的弟弟叶惠而来到这地方，"夜梦白须老叟言曰：汝欲来此地乎？前有莲花一枝，应与汝焉"。次日见到虎屿山与前山之间溪水萦回，"其形若倒地莲花，遂挈家居焉"，建立了渠口村。渠口村叶氏大宗祠里有一副楹联：

应梦赐莲花，看一枝倒地垂形，一姓雅宜君子爱；

响卜详品字，象三日因文会意，三乡衍族丁口昌。

说的就是始迁祖当年选址时候的故事。

岩头村在双浚头从五鹈溪引水，但村子却建在双浚头之南1公里处。这是因为双浚头东西有山，气势局促，没有宽阔的"明堂"也没有均衡的左右"护砂"。而建村的位置，西有琴山作祖山，东有大片的开阔地，远处有朝山，近处有龟蛇二山作为阙。左右的护砂起伏逶迤，气局宏大。村民们相信，祖先选择了这个大开大阖的风水对村子的繁荣有很大的关系。《岩头金氏宗谱·重修族谱叙》里写道：

夫东瓯素称小邹鲁，其山川人物之盛为东南最，金氏尤甲于楠之两溪。向非厥祖之陟巘降原，观泉景日，又奚能庐旅处处爱众夏有如是耶！

前引的常川郑和蓬溪谢氏的宗谱也把山水之美引到风水术上去。

2.水系

村落的选址决定之后，规划与建设的起点就是兴修水系。

水是农业生产的命脉，在农业社会里，兴水利，除水害，是第一等的大事。所以治水的禹被尊为掌水的"大帝"，与掌天的尧，掌地的舜，合称为"三官大帝"。在楠溪江，田间村头，最多的就是三官庙，几乎到处都有。自古以来，治水也是地方官的首要任务之一，北宋王安石倡导的新政，就有一条"起堤堰，决陂塘，为水陆之利"。

水也是村庄赖以生存的命脉。居民饮食要水，洗涤要水，农产品加工和家庭手工业要水，救援火灾也要水。水又是改善小气候、美化环境的必要因素。因此，和其他各地一样，楠溪江各村落的宗族组织和乡贤士绅，都把主持和资助水利建设当作一件经常性的善事来做。

农田水系和村落水系通常是统一规划的。对村落来说，水系的功能，一是供水，把水引进村子里，尽可能方便家家户户日常使用；二是排水，把生活污水、雨水和山洪导出村外。供水和排水都靠自流，所以，沟渠系统就成了村落规划的先决因素，它决定了村落的竖向设计，也就是决定了每幢房屋的相对标高，它也迫使村落必须有一定程度的规划。楠溪江流域夏季多暴雨，雨过之后，村子里没有积水，就是因为建设了合理的沟渠系统的缘故。芙蓉村的北部，内外两道寨墙之间，过去虽然有过几幢大宅，被毁于对岩头村的械斗，但大部分并没有造过房屋，然而却已有整齐的石子路和沟渠。它们证明，村子的建设，是先修筑道路和沟渠，然后才在它们界划出来的地块里造房子的。

楠溪江中游村落的水系，大致有两大类，一类是平川地的，一类是山坡地的。此外还有两类混合的和少数很特殊的。

（1）苍坡、芙蓉、岩头这些平地上的村落，街道和水系与农田相似。方整的街道网把村子大体均匀地界划成若干地块，沟渠沿主要街道流经全村。在农田里为种庄稼的地块，在村子里则建造房屋。《大岩岩志》说水云村的农田"道路修整，沟塍画然"。道路和沟塍的规划建设都依仗农民在农田里引水灌溉的经验。北宋时任永嘉太守的杨蟠则在一首咏永嘉的诗里说：

水如棋局分街陌，山似屏帏绕画楼。

村民们熟悉农田的自流灌溉，把建设农田沟塍系统的方法移植到村落规划中来，是很自然的事。二者在原则上是一样的。

风水师则在水渠的流向上有讲究，如苍坡、岩头和芙蓉三村，水渠都从西北进村，除芙蓉村的从正东出村外，另两村的都从东南出村。堪舆家认为，"凡地西北高东南下水流出辰巳间吉"（见宋·王洙：《地理新书》）。这三村的选址和相应的沟渠规划基本上是合乎这个吉祥的地形的。芙蓉村主渠出东门前，沿"演乐台"南侧曲折，这是避免"去水无情"，而要它多情地滞留一下。村民们把这曲折叫做"锁"，锁住"风"和"气"。

沟渠傍主要街道的一侧，宽度大小不等。最宽的是岩头村浚水街西侧的水渠，有2.7米左右，最窄的是芙蓉村如意街南侧的，只略宽于0.5米。苍坡村的笔街南侧，水渠宽约1米。因为村子里绝大多数的房屋朝同一个方向，所以，水渠通常流经房屋的后门或侧门，门内便是厨房，在有门口的地方，架上石板通行。

水渠边上隔不远便有一两步石级，也有横跨水渠的石板，用来供妇女们洗涤时当搓板用，或者放置桶、盆和衣物。沿着长街短巷，年轻的妇女们蹲踞在清清的沟渠边浣洗。孩子们绕着鹅兜嬉戏，乖巧的小姑娘也会拿一块帕子，学妈妈的样子在石板上揉搓。潺潺水声应和着笑语声，在街上回荡。柔和温暖的生活气息，漫溢在整个村落。也只有在这样的村落里，才会有"秋风催木叶，万户捣衣声"的诗境。

由于水渠同时也流泄各家的生活污水和雨水，所以以前村子都有村规，保护渠水的一定的清洁度。例如岩头村，每天日出之前不许洗衣洗菜，让各家把澄清了一夜的水打回家去作饮食用水；渠内不许放鹅鸭，不许洗沾有粪便的衣物，每年清理一次渠道；等等。虽然有这些规定，有些村子的村民们的饮食用水，仍多汲自水井。苍坡村水井比较多，至少有十三口。岩头村因为渠水量大流急，所以村民挑清晨的渠水饮食，村里没有水井。

许多村子里都有池塘，由水渠的水汇潴而成。除了供洗涤等日常用途之外，还更有防火的作用。由于水池比较开阔，在村子里造成空间的变化，又由于水池特有的美学品质，所以，它们常常成为村子里重要的环境因素。芙蓉村中央，主要街道如意街的南侧，有芙蓉池，池中设一座芙蓉亭。枫林镇的中心，圣旨门对面，有圣旨门池，也是长方形的，池中造了一座亭子。渠口村的池塘比较大，在村子前沿，北岸边也有一座长方形的凉亭造在水中。这些水池和亭子，都是村子最重要的休闲场所，尤其是老年人交流几十年友情的场所。有些水池成为公共园林的主要因素，例如溪口村、苍坡村和岩头村的水池，它们的面积都比较大。

以水池为主体的村子里的休闲中心和广阔的公共园林，是楠溪江村落最有特色的景观之一。

（2）塘湾、坦下、埭头、水云这些山坡上的村落，水系的规划与平地村子大不一样，它们的水系最重要的作用是泄山洪。主要的泄洪道大多是天然的冲沟，两岸人工衬砌加固。村子的规划，既要利用泄洪沟排水，又要防山洪的冲击。

山洪来也猛，去也疾，而且洪沟很深，所以不便日用，村子里一般不修沿街引水的水渠，街道上只有二三十厘米宽的排水沟，把雨水和污水送进洪沟。村民的用水，一靠筑池塘汇蓄，如塘湾和埭头；二靠凿井或挖泉源池，如坦下和塘湾；三靠用毛竹当输水管从山上引泉水到家，如水云、埭头、塘湾等村子。

蓬溪村所靠的山比较高、比较深，所以，除了山洪之外，还有常年不断的溪水。蓬溪村的水系，因而也是复合式的，既有山洪冲沟，也有沿主街道的水渠，而且水量丰沛，水流湍急，比较清洁。

除了上面两种水系之外，还有少量特殊的水系。

例如溪南村，它只有一条三五米宽的水渠，弯弯曲曲，反复来回盘绕，村里的房屋就顺势造在这九曲十八弯的水渠两侧。村民日常用水靠这条水渠，街巷里只有大约30厘米宽的排水沟，把雨水和下水排入这条大渠。沿渠有石级、石板、石桥，供妇女洗涤或取水时使用。岸边多种树木，因而这道水渠在村子里形成一条宽阔的绿化带，大大美化了村子的生活环境和改善了小气候。另外在它的卢氏大宗祠前面还有一口池塘，恰好倒映着芙蓉三峰。由于房屋造在弯曲的渠道两岸，所以朝向偏侧，各各不同。楠溪江中游盆地里的村子，房屋大多共朝一个方向，溪南村是个例外。不过它东部建房比较晚近，街巷修直，房舍整齐，与其他村落相似，居民用水靠南边的水渠。

另一个例子是西岸村。它造在一座小山的东坡，面临大楠溪而隔着一片乱石滩。山小没有水源，江滩太阔不便于日常取水，所以村民只好开凿几处大口井。大口井很特别，先挖一个面积很大的深坑，坑壁和坑底都用大块卵石衬砌，坑的平面如同一只瓠瓜，在"瓜"的尽头上宽阔处是储水的井，"瓜"的长柄渐窄处是下井去的长台阶。这种水井因此就叫做瓠瓜井。掘井而饮，对整个村子的规划布局就没有多大的影响。

English Translation

IV. Site Selection and Water System

The construction of ancient villages in the middle reaches of the Nanxi River mostly underwent complete planning. Those built relatively early and whose early planning has been basically preserved to the present include Cangpo Village, Tangwan Village, Furong Village, and Yantou Village. Cangpo was founded in 955 CE; from the second year of Zhihe of Northern Song (1055), important constructions are all recorded in the "Cangpo Li Clan Genealogy," including a comprehensive planning in the fifth year of Chunxi of Southern Song (1178). These records generally correspond to Cangpo Village's current state—water systems, main streets, and public buildings are all present. For example, in the fifth year of Chunxi, the ninth-generation ancestor Li Song built a dam southeast of the village to store water, forming the East Pond and West Pond. After Li Song's death, his wife, Lady Liu of Huanxi, continued to complete the village wall, Brush Street, and village gate, and dug the ring-plain water canal. Tangwan Village was first built in the Song dynasty; the "Tangchuan (i.e., Tangwan) Zheng Clan Genealogy" contains a record mentioning the Front Street and Middle Street from the Shaoxing period, which remain Tangwan Village's main streets to this day. Furong Village was also first built in the Song dynasty, completely burned down at the end of Southern Song due to anti-Yuan resistance, and rebuilt in the first year of Zhizheng of Yuan (1341), generally maintaining its original layout. Yantou Village's planning dates to the mid-Ming, was largely destroyed in the mid-Qing, but its original state can still be traced; its water system and garden green spaces are the most accomplished among Nanxi River villages.

Although there are no documentary records, from the current state, the main contents of planning include site selection, water systems (canals, channels, ponds, wells), layout (street networks, functional zoning, public centers, garden green spaces), and defense systems (village walls, stream gates, watchtowers). Some of these planning elements may also be found in villages in other regions, but implementing them comprehensively and so orderly is rare elsewhere, thus becoming an important feature of today's Nanxi River villages. As for partially preserved planning and construction, almost every village has some.

1. Site Selection

Nanxi River villages, at their founding, all attached great importance to site selection. The main consideration in site selection was agricultural production and life under a self-sufficient natural economy. The basic elements affecting production and life were water, land, and mountains. Fengshui theories attaching auspicious or inauspicious meanings to water, land, and mountains were quite active.

Nanxi River people's understanding of their living environment was comprehensive and integrated. The "Quchuan Ye Clan Genealogy · Postscript to the Completion of the Recompiled Genealogy" writes:

Qukou is the place where our ancestor Lord Guangzong originated. Thirty-three generations have passed, over a thousand years. Several hundred families surround it, stretching for seven or eight li. Phoenix Mountain soars to its west, Thunder Peak stands to its east, Islet Mountain lies to its south, and beyond it the great stream encircles. A canal runs through the middle, capable of irrigating fields. To its north, layered peaks rise in varied forms. Paths lead in all directions, fields high and low stretch horizontally, arranged like a painting. Springs flow gently, sounding to the ear.
