The Hu clan of Mingyu formerly had a reading tower, east of the residences, extremely quiet. ... They once invited teachers to instruct the younger generation, hence the clan's members were numerous and well-dressed, all knowing to discipline themselves with propriety. ... (Note: later the reading tower fell into disuse, and literary fortune declined) In the mid-winter of the Gui Chou year of Jiajing (1553), Lords Yuanquan and Qiaoxi proposed to the assembly: Our ancestors cultivated the tradition of Zhou rites, and our clan has continuously maintained a sense of righteousness. Formerly our propriety did not match that of predecessors, because the family learning was lost. Now we should build a refined lodge to cultivate the younger generation, so that the fragrance of books may not be extinguished; those who wish to emulate may listen. All agreed. Thus they selected a site at the screen of the eastern mountain, where the soil was dry and firm. They gathered timber and materials, and within a year the lodge was completed. Its corridors were magnificent, its walls high and steep, appearing imposing from afar. It was named the Dongshan School. They allocated several shi of field rent as the ongoing expense for hiring teachers and educating students.

That an academy could be "magnificent in corridors and imposing in appearance" shows it was a very important building in the village.

Some gentry built private schools at home to teach clan子弟 or to read for their own enjoyment, such as the "Geishi Hall" built by Hu Bi of Yuzhang village during the Shaoxing reign of the Southern Song. In Yantou village, Lord Guilin during the Jiajing reign converted the "Senxiu Xuan" (Luxuriant Elegance Studio), located south of the Tahuhu Temple. Originally the temple's kitchen, its location was beautiful — facing Zhennan Lake in front and backing onto Youjun Pond behind — so Lord Guilin converted it into a study. In Lord Guilin's poem "On Senxiu Xuan, Part Two," there are two couplets:

Before the door stand willows like Tao Yuanming's;

In the courtyard opens the ink-washing pond of Wang Xizhi.

At the chess stone, the wind frequently arrives;

Brewing tea in the pine studio, the moon rises above.

The cultural quality of life in the studio was high. The studio itself is plain, three bays wide, with a total width of 10.7 meters and depth of 7.5 meters. The environment is quiet and elegant — truly a good place for reading — so the studio, though humble, "how could it be crude?"

### 2. Wenchang Pavilions and Wenfeng Pagodas

Another type of cultural and educational building most closely related to academies is the Wenchang Pavilion and Wenfeng Pagoda.

(1) The Wenchang Pavilion worships the "Wenchang Emperor." Wenchang was originally the "Keeper of Official Salaries" among the seven stars of the Big Dipper; later Daoists merged it with the Zitong Great Emperor to form the Wenchang Emperor, who "governs literary affairs and official careers." Because it concerned success in examinations, it was widely worshipped. In the fifth month of the sixth year of Jiaqing (1801), Emperor Renzong of Qing issued an edict: "The Wenchang Emperor is worshipped throughout the empire the same as the Holy Emperor Guan; it should be entered into the official sacrifices, equally illuminating literary governance." The importance of Wenchang Pavilions greatly increased. The Guangxu edition of Yongjia County Gazetteer, "Establishments — Altars and Temples," states that Wenchang temples "exist in every township, too numerous to record."

In the Nanxi River region, villages including Langxia, Huatan, Yantou, and Shuiyun originally had Wenchang Pavilions. They were generally closely related to academies. For example, the Wenchang Pavilion in Yantou village, founded in the Geng Shen year of Qianlong (1740), is north of the Tahuhu Temple, facing the Zhishui Lake, echoing the Senxiu Studio to the south, and separated from the Shuiting Shrine Academy by a gurgling water channel. Some Wenchang Pavilions also served as academies, such as the one in Langxia village. None survive today; their form and style can no longer be ascertained. The Wenchang Pavilion in Yantou village was destroyed in 1958.

Like academies, Wenchang Pavilions were mostly built in scenic locations. They often became one of the village's "Ten Scenes" or "Eight Scenes," subjects of poetry. In Huatan village's "Ten Scenes Poems," there are two poems "Ascending and Gazing at Wenchang":

The lofty pavilion rises among the clouds;

Streams and mountains enter the eyes wondrously.

... Wandering within a painting,

Looking up and down, one strokes one's beard.

The second:

... Bamboo sways, wind plays with shadows;

Flowers dim, birds sing in shade.

Ancient trees float calmly on the waves;

The empty pond holds the deep moon. ...

The care in selecting Wenchang Pavilion sites can be seen in the "Record of Relocating the Wenchang Pavilion" in the Zhenxi Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy. During the early Qing Yongzheng reign, the gentry of Langxia village raised funds to build a Wenchang Pavilion, but "its site was low and obstructed by trees." Zhu Wenxuan, a county student, was dissatisfied. One day, he invited friends to stroll on Guisong Ridge, and came to a mountain hollow "broad enough for more than a mu," where he "saw the pool water swirling and reflecting images, the graceful peaks rising to touch the clouds; the Writing Brush Peak pierces to its right, the Dipper Mountain crouches to its left; mountains and rivers encircle it, like embroidery and brocade." He exclaimed with delight: "This is truly the site for a Wenchang Pavilion! It can pacify the spirits and gather the qi!" Thus, in the second year of Qianlong (1737), the original Wenchang Pavilion was dismantled and rebuilt on this new site. With pool reflections and cloud-touching peaks, the new site's environment was exceedingly elegant. And the Writing Brush and Dipper mountains were associated with literary fortune; pacifying spirits and gathering qi accorded with fengshui requirements. Hence the Record notes that both of Wenxuan's sons passed the juren (provincial) examination.

Founding a Wenchang Pavilion was a major clan event, related to the rise and fall of the entire clan, and thus became one of the major charitable acts of the gentry. The "Biography of Lord Shenxuan" in the Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Combined Genealogy says that this Zhu Guangrun donated substantial funds to "found the literary pavilion, ... constructing an outstanding building, as splendid as a bird's spread wings and a pheasant's soaring flight." It was a two-story building, with the two side wings used as academy lecture halls. Its environment was exceedingly beautiful: "The finest scenery of Zhenchuan is ranked first here." This Wenchang Pavilion, an "outstanding building as splendid as a bird's spread wings," likely used a xieshan (gable-and-hip) roof — the highest architectural grade locally.

(2) Wenfeng Pagodas influenced a village's literary fortune through fengshui, and were folk culture absorbed into literate culture. The Qing author Gao Jiannan wrote in Xiangzhai Jingzuan (Compendium of Residence Geomancy): "In any capital, province, prefecture, county, or village, where scholars are unsuccessful and examination degrees are not obtained, one may select an auspicious site in the jia, xun, bing, or ding directions, and erect a pointed peak like a writing brush; as long as it is higher than other mountains, degrees will follow. Or erect a writing brush on a mountain, or build a tall pagoda on level ground — both are Writing Brush Peaks." The mountains on both banks of the Nanxi River are undulating, composed of volcanic rhyolite; after hundreds of millions of years of erosion, many villages can find conical rock peaks nearby that can be identified as "Writing Brush Peaks," such as in Pengxi, Yuzhang, and Langxia villages. Hence few villages built Wenfeng Pagodas. Among middle-reach villages, only Yantou and Tangwan each had one. The Tangchuan (i.e., Tangwan) Zheng Clan Genealogy, "Local Scenery," contains a Ten Scenes poem, one of which is "Xunji Mountain":

A single peak stands tall and erect,

Positioned in the southeast where auspicious qi gathers.

On Xunji Mountain an additional precious pagoda;

Literary style flourishes, the brush roams like a dragon.

This confirms that a Wenfeng Pagoda originally stood on Xunji Mountain southeast of Tangwan village.

The Wenfeng Pagoda in Yantou village was built by Lord Guilin during the Jiajing reign of the Ming dynasty, at the same time as the Shuiting Shrine Academy. It is located on Tang Mountain, which is southeast of the village — also the xun direction. The mountain is low and cannot "rise above other mountains," so a Wenfeng Pagoda was built to claim the fengshui.

Both pagodas are now completely destroyed. The Yantou Wenfeng Pagoda still has several surviving fragments: two eave pieces, two body pieces, one pingzuo (balcony) piece, and one base piece. From the fragments, this was a small solid pagoda of gray-white marble, hexagonal, in the pavilion style, with three sides on each level having flame-shaped niches containing seated Buddha statues. It was divided into several sections, each a single stone block; even the projecting eaves were carved from whole stones. From the base, the bottom level's body side is only 29.5 cm long. Of the two surviving body pieces, one has a side of 25 cm, the other 22.5 cm, with heights of 118 cm and 113 cm respectively. The two eave pieces are 12 cm and 14 cm high. If the pagoda had seven levels, its total height was about 2.5 meters. This pagoda was destroyed in 1985. According to Jin Kepan of the village elderly association, the pagoda had seven levels of projecting eaves.

Wenchang Pagodas were often paired with an ink pond or inkstone pool. The pagoda's shadow falling into the water is called "the writing brush dipping in ink" — a fengshui formation highly beneficial to literary fortune. The Wenfeng Pagoda in Yantou village cast its shadow into the pond within the Shuiting Shrine Academy — precisely this fengshui. Natural Writing Brush Peaks were the same. For example, both Yuzhang and Pengxi villages have ink ponds and inkstone pools to reflect their Writing Brush Peaks. The Yuzhang Hu Clan Genealogy says of the village entrance ink pond: "The Writing Brush Peak's inverted reflection is like a brush tip dipping in water; where elegant qi gathers, officials may emerge in succession, degrees obtained continuously, and writing talent never lacks generation after generation."

Furong village originally had a Confucian Temple in the southeast corner (xun position), also for the benefit of examination success. The temple was very small, with only three main rooms and a surrounding wall. It is now in ruins.

### 3. Minor Buildings

Most Nanxi River villages have minor buildings such as memorial arches (paifang), pavilions, terraces, and gateways across streets. They often also have important cultural and educational significance. The Guangxu edition of Yongjia County Gazetteer, "Historic Sites," says of memorial arches: "The ancients marked their residences and neighborhoods, establishing their reputation; what can be emulated and transmitted is related to governance and education." Hence memorial arches were generally built with great care, located near ancestral halls or village gates, forming part of the village's ritual center.

(1) Huatan village had the most memorial arches — twelve in total:

"Wu Fu" (Black Office): Built during the Zhengtong reign of Emperor Yingzong of Ming, before the Dunmu Shrine, for Zhu Liangxian, Supervisor of War Office.

"Huang Men" (Yellow Gate): Same as above.

"Yi Shi Zan Ying" (Generations of Official Honors): Erected by Zhu Tian, a jinshi of the Jiajing reign of Ming, forming a group with the above two arches as the approach to the Dunmu Shrine.

"Xiang Xian" (Village Worthy): Built by He Wenyuan, Prefect of Wenzhou in the Zhengde reign of Ming, for Lord Sining, an educational official.

"Xian Tai" (Censorate Terrace): Built in the Yi Chou year of Hongzhi (1505), by Li Duan, Prefect of Wenzhou, and Liu Jing, County Magistrate of Yongjia, for Zhu Liangyi, Vice Director of the Ministry of Works. Before the Xiantai Ancestral Hall. (The hall is now a primary school.)

"Zhong Xiu" (Gathered Excellence): For Zhu Tian, a juren. (Zhu Tian later became a jinshi during the Jiajing reign.)

"Gong Zhi Chun Liang" (Public, Upright, Honest, Good): In the winter of the Bing Yin year of Jiajing (1566), by Cheng Wenzhu, County Magistrate of Yongjia, for Zhu Shuangxi.

"Xi He" (Harmony): By the gentry Lord Xihua Wang, for Zhu Lianxi.

"Xi Shan Di Yi" (First Under Stream and Mountain): By Zhu Yichuan, a juren who served as Magistrate of Fengyang County; plaque calligraphy by Zhu Tian.

"Wei Gong Xuan Li" (Serving the Public with Vigor): By County Magistrate Lord Wu, for Zhu Xiaoshan.

"Yuan Fei Yu Yue" (Kites Soar, Fish Leap): By Zhou Ling, Secretary of the Central Secretariat, for Zhu Youdu, within the Xiyuan Academy.

"Song Bai Han Zhen" (Pines and Cypresses, Chastity in Cold): In the second year of Chongzhen (1629), by Censor Xu Ji, for the chaste widow Madam Shao.

These twelve arches were arranged along the main road running east-west on the south side of Huatan village, creating a landscape with clear rhythm and rich layering. This was one of the most unique village landscapes in the Nanxi River region. Unfortunately, only one Xiantai arch and the "Xi Shan Di Yi" arch remain today.

Except for "Song Bai Han Zhen," all the arches were built to honor scholars. They had either obtained jinshi or juren degrees, held high office, enjoyed great reputation locally, or performed great good deeds for the village. In short, all were sufficient to bring glory to the clan and "be emulated and transmitted."

"Xi Shan Di Yi" is said to have been requested by Wang Zan — Hanlin Academy Compiler, Chancellor of the National University in both capitals, and Vice Minister of Rites — and bestowed by Emperor Xiaozong of Ming upon Zhu Moqu. Zhu was the director of Huatan's Fengnan Academy and Wang Zan's teacher. The existing "Xi Shan Di Yi" arch is at the west end of the main road and is actually a street gateway. To its east, on the north side of the road, is the Zhu Clan Major Ancestral Hall, originally built in the Ji You year of Chunyou of Song (1249) and expanded in the Chenghua reign of Ming, named Guifang Hall; on the south side is a tea pavilion that also serves as a Three-Official Temple. The three form an architectural complex, the starting point of the main road and the village's primary ritual center.

The "Xiantai" arch stands before the Xiantai Shrine. It is three bays wide, 5.95 meters high, with a total width of 6.28 meters, in a powerful and solemn style. The two central columns are square stone; at each end are a pair of round wooden columns, placed front and back. The arch uses bracket sets (dougong) with a very special structure. Above the large horizontal beam of the central bay, two column-head bracket sets each project two steps forward and backward to support a beautiful moon-beam-shaped horizontal member. Above this member are three layers of descending ang (lever arms) supporting the eave purlin. The ends of this member extend to become the eave purlins of the side bays, which are supported by the pairs of round wooden columns at each end.

The jinshi memorial arch in Yantou village is of the same style as this Xiantai arch. It was bestowed by Emperor Shizong of Ming upon Jin Zhao, who served as Assistant to the Left and Right in the Court of Judicial Review and later became Prefect of Ruizhou. It was built to the left of the main gate of the Jin Clan Major Ancestral Hall, at a right angle to the gate but parallel to the main street entering from Yantou's north gate. The jinshi arch has four columns and three roofs, with a total width of 8.46 meters; the central bay is 4.60 meters wide. The columns are 4.40 meters high, and the total height is 7.63 meters. Its scale and mass are larger than the Xiantai arch in Huatan village — very majestic, vigorous, and upright. The two central columns are square stone; the two outer columns are square wood; all columns have drum-shaped stone braces front and back. Above the large horizontal beam of the central bay are two column-head bracket sets, also projecting two steps to support a moon-beam-shaped member, above which are three layers of descending ang, with the rear tails of the front and back ang crossing to support the ridge purlin. Each side bay has one column-head bracket set with a single qiao (arm) and double ang. The column-top bracket sets are complex and irregular, with 45° diagonal projections, mostly decorative. All three roofs are in the xuanshan (gable) style, with the side gables projecting about 1.65 meters. A pair of relatively thin square wooden columns, placed front and back, support the projecting purlins.

Both the Xiantai arch in Huatan village and the jinshi arch in Yantou village were built in the mid-Ming dynasty, with consistent form and style. Both have column inclination (cejiao), structurally functional descending ang and dingtou gong (bracket arms), and use gracefully curved moon beams and the complete curves of eaves and main ridges formed by column height variation and header blocks. These practices were common in Song dynasty architecture but had long disappeared from official Ming and Qing architecture. The gate of the Dunmu Shrine (Wu Fu) in Huatan village and the village gate of Cangpo village have roughly the same structural form and methods as these two arches.

(2) The main gate of Yantou village is the north gate, originally named "Rendao Gate" (Gate of Benevolent Way), built to commemorate the founding ancestor Liu Jinzhi (1095-1166), who was fair in dealings, broadly practiced benevolence, and repeatedly relieved disaster victims. It has long been destroyed. In Fenglin town, the "Xu Yinpei Shangyi Gate" (Gate of Xu Yinpei's Righteous Deeds), built during the Chenghua reign of Ming, is still completely preserved. It houses the imperial edict of Emperor Xiaozong of Ming praising the brotherly affection of the Xu brothers, hence commonly called the Shengzhi Gate (Imperial Edict Gate). Xikou village has the "Mingwen Li Gate" (Clear Literature Village Gate), originally built in the Southern Song to hang the plaque bestowed by Emperor Guangzong of Song upon the academy directed by Dai Meng. The existing one is clearly not the original. These three gates all became part of the village's public center. Feudal ethical education and values quietly permeated people's hearts through the architectural environment.

The Shengzhi Gate is three bays wide, a two-story pavilion style, with a xieshan roof above and a waist eave below. It stands on the north side of Fenglin's main street; a secondary street perpendicular to the main street runs northward through its gateway but is not long. Across the main street, opposite, is a pond called Shengzhi Gate Lake, with a rectangular pavilion in it. This small architectural complex became Fenglin's leisure center; throughout the day, people sit chatting on the platforms on both sides of the Shengzhi Gate's gateway and in the pavilion in the lake.

The Mingwen Li Gate is on the east side of Xikou's main street, also at a T-junction. It is relatively small, only three bays wide, single-story, with a xuanshan roof. Its architectural grade is relatively low, and it may not be the original. Not far beyond the gate is the park's large pond.

(3) The Nanxi River region also has an important pair of minor buildings. Farmers and elders recite them far and wide, expressing simple rural affection. These are the Wangxiong Pavilion (Gazing-at-Brother Pavilion) in Cangpo village and the Songdi Pavilion (Sending-off-Brother Pavilion) in Fangxiang village.

The seventh-generation ancestors of the Li clan in Cangpo village were two brothers, Qiushan and Jiamu. According to the Genealogy, in the second year of Jianyan of the Southern Song (1128), the older brother Qiushan moved to Fangxiang, about two li away across a small stream. The brothers were deeply affectionate and visited frequently, "gathering in the peach and plum gardens, recounting the joys of family bonds." Thus each brother built a pavilion, facing each other from afar, to express their attachment.

The Wangxiong Pavilion is built on Cangpo village's 2.5-meter-high village wall. It is square, 5.0 meters on each side, with four golden columns and twelve eave columns, forming four clusters of columns at the corners. Between the eave columns are beauty-rest chairs (meirenkao). The roof is in the xieshan style, with simple bracket sets in the beam framework. It faces East Lake to the north and gazes south toward the Songdi Pavilion in distant Fangxiang village, with a broad view. Together with the Renji Temple to its west, it forms Cangpo village's important public center, and with the Renji Temple, major ancestral hall, village gate, and several towering ancient cypresses on the village wall, constitutes the lively and dynamic silhouette of Cangpo village's front — one of the most moving architectural landscapes of ancient Nanxi River villages. The pavilion is slightly rotated, presenting the most varied form and playing a prominent role in this picture.

The Songdi Pavilion has the same form as the Wangxiong Pavilion, beside a stone slab bridge over a small stream, gazing toward the affectionate Wangxiong Pavilion and offering a fine view of the front of Cangpo village's scenery.

Although these two minor buildings are no longer Song originals, their style remains vigorous and simple. On their beauty-rest chairs, leisure-seekers often sit, discussing past and present; the story of brotherly affection, like spring breeze and gentle rain, seeps into the villagers' hearts. A couplet on the Wangxiong Pavilion reads: "Propriety values human bonds, clarifying ancient teachings; the pavilion transmits a fine story, continuing the family tradition" — expressing the humanistic significance of this pair of small buildings.

Many Nanxi River villages also had pavilions and terraces for enjoying scenery and reading and composing poetry. The most famous was the Linqing Tower in Heyang village. One of the "Eight Scenes of Heyang" poems describes the Linqing Tower:

The perilous tower stands high above the green stream;

Wearing a black hat, one ascends in the year of white hair.

... The quiet curtain rolls up, sitting with spring mountains;

The precious incense burns out, sleeping by the plunging waterfall.

In this tower was lived a high-level cultural life. Unfortunately, such cultural buildings have been completely destroyed.

### 4. Stages

Since the Southern Song, the coastal region of eastern Zhejiang has流行 southern-style dramatic performance, called Wenzhou variety plays or Yongjia variety plays. The Ming author Zhu Yunming wrote in Weitan (Vulgar Talk): "Southern drama emerged after the Xuanhe reign, during the Southern Song crossing, called Wenzhou variety plays." Xu Wei wrote in Nanci Xulu (Record of Southern Lyrics): "Southern drama began in the reign of Emperor Guangzong of Song; ... some say it already originated in the Xuanhe period, and flourished from the Southern Song crossing, called Yongjia variety plays."

Variety plays, such as The Lute (Pipa Ji) written by the Wenzhou native Gao Ming, although literary works, had deep connections with folk culture. According to the Guangxu edition of Yongjia County Gazetteer, "Local Customs — Popular Mores," the people of Wenzhou had "loved song and dance" since the Sui and Tang dynasties, and "as for the lantern festival of the first month and the dragon-boat races of the fifth month, they competed in novelty and extravagance, sparing no expense. Men and women went to watch, in splendid attire, filling the cities." The Ming author Lu Rong wrote in Shuyuan Zaji (Miscellaneous Records from the Bean Garden), describing the popularity of local drama in Zhejiang during the Chenghua reign: "In Haiyan of Jiaxing, Yuyao of Shaoxing, Cixi of Ningbo, Huangyan of Taizhou, and Yongjia of Wenzhou, there are those who practice the performer's art, called 'drama disciples'; even sons of good families do not consider it shameful." During the Daoguang reign of Qing, every town and mountain village in Wenzhou established luantan (chaotic tune) drama troupes, recruiting farmers' sons to learn acting. In Yongjia County there were seven drama troupes: Fenglin, Yongqiang, Nanxi, Wushi, Gangtoupu, Fengtou, and Wuniu. At least three — Fenglin, Gangtoupu, and Nanxi — were in the middle Nanxi River region.

With local drama flourishing, many performance venues were needed. Most stages were within ancestral halls and temples, with performances often taking place on the occasions of annual sacrifices to gods and ancestors.

Independent stages not attached to ancestral halls or temples were very rare; only Shuiyun village has one, built in the Ren Chen year of Guangxu of Qing (1892), called the Chishui Pavilion (Red Water Pavilion). The Chishui Pavilion is located outside the road that winds along the contour line at the front of the village, precisely on a scarp with a drop of nearly two meters. Its plan is in a "convex" shape, composed of a leisure pavilion 10.2 meters on the long side and 9.6 meters on the short side, and a stage 4.3 meters square. The pavilion is close to the road but separated by a ditch, connected by a stone slab bridge over a meter long; at the bridgehead is a stone slab to block pigs and dogs. On ordinary days, the pavilion is a villagers' leisure place; on its beauty-rest chairs, people sit bare-chested and barefoot all day, discussing everything from ancient and modern times to rice, millet, mulberry, and hemp — leisurely and intimate. During performances, the pavilion becomes the backstage.

The stage faces outside the village, about 18.0 meters from the stream. Using the terrain drop, the stage surface is exactly about one and a half meters above the open space in front, where the audience watches performances. Between the stage and the pavilion is a taishi wall (decorative partition), with a shrine niche on the pavilion side worshipping the Three Great Emperors. On left and right are the "Chu Jiang" (Exit as General) and "Ru Xiang" (Enter as Minister) doors for entering and exiting. The pavilion has beauty-rest chairs; the stage has only a small railing over twenty centimeters high along the edge, to avoid obstructing the view. Each part has a xieshan roof, with main ridges parallel to each other, and the structure uses a joined-roof (goulianda) construction. The ceiling and taishi wall are covered with bright colored paintings, mostly narrative themes related to drama. The abundance of paintings and华丽 of colors are unmatched in the Nanxi River region. All columns have painted couplets, composed by the local Qing dynasty juren Chen Ziwan. Those on the pavilion read:

Beyond the fish-watching railing, peach-blossom waves;

Before the horse-tying steps, willow-leaf wind.

Those on the stage read:

Hear the coughing and sighing in the air — is it song?

It is living situations made manifest, not mere drama.

One side is leisurely and elegant, with delicate verses; the other does not put on a stern face to preach morality. This performance venue combined with a leisure pavilion has an atmosphere somewhat different from stages in ancestral halls and temples.

本节术语 Key Terms

文教建筑 科甲簪缨 珠贯蝉联 理学家 诗文作者 文风之盛 义塾 书院 读书楼 文昌阁 戏台 教化为目的 乡间社学 古党庠术序 读书准备考功名 教化 茗川胡氏大宗祠谱 田川胡氏义塾规 人文蔚起 高拔五桂之芳 门第常新 三槐之瑞 沼芹叠采 云路同登 吏治所首重 民风所视以转移 楠溪义学 县丞何森 枫林 传播儒学正统
十一、其他建筑类型
中文原文重构

楠溪江的建筑类型很丰富，一一胪列缕述，实在没有可能。但有些类型，虽然不如住宅、宗庙的重要，不写却会严重失楠溪江建筑面貌之真。在这一章里，择要写一写防御工程、路亭和商业建筑，以免缺失过多。

一、防御工程

楠溪江的村落里，大多数民居和一些公共建筑是外向开放的，村子的公共中心、绿地、池塘和街巷系统也是外向开放的。它们向人坦露胸怀，像乡民一样纯真、笃诚，随时招呼邻里乡亲甚至过路人到家里闲话尝新。陌生人走进这样的村庄，不会觉得被人用警惕的眼光打量着，严密防范。

然而，这些笼罩在安祥和气氛之下的村落，有不少，尤其是河谷平原里比较富庶的，都用厚厚的石寨墙包围起来，精心地设计了它的防御功能。

一个单姓的血缘村落，为了在异己的自然力量和社会力量之前求生存、求发展；在宗族关系干预之下，内部的对抗可能比较缓和。但村落的内聚力愈强，就愈会以一个宗族的整体来处理村落之间的事务，甚至个别村民之间的争端会酿成村落和村落之间的严重冲突。宗族关系又是封建的君臣关系的基础，所以每当国家改朝换代的时候，偏僻的楠溪江山村里也会有忠臣义士，演出悲壮的故事。至于整个封建时代都会有的农民起义或者草寇野匪，当然也不会见到进士牌楼就揖让而去。

即使在"科名鹊起"的南宋，温州太守杨简在《劝农文》里也说，温州的大患之一是"风俗好争"。元、明以后，风尚渐漓，到乾隆年间，《永嘉县志·风俗》里引《旧志》说：当地"其俗剽悍，其货纤靡，其士风任气而矜节"。老百姓剽悍好争，读书人任气矜节，这个"弦歌之声不绝于耳"的楠溪江，就常常免不了要有激烈的冲突。

楠溪江比较大的动乱，最早是北宋末年方腊义军的到来。方腊事败之后，本地人俞道安又率起义民奋战了几年。

楠溪江的第一次大规模战争是元兵抗元。这次战争的英雄是芙蓉村的陈虞之。《两源陈氏宗谱》载："虞之公字云翁，登宋咸淳乙丑（1265年）进士第。始为扬州教授，淮东帅府转两浙漕干，除刑工二部架阁文字，迁秘书省校勘兼国史院编修，辟广王府记室参军。积借承议郎。元兵至温，公与族侄规公率子侄乡人千馀迎战于绿嶂洋，退登芙蓉岩，拒守三载。戊寅闻帝昺溺死，公自刎，岩遂溃。规公被执，不屈而亡。时从死者八百馀人，被掳者五十馀人。"同时，廊下村也有太常寺簿，咸淳四年戊辰（1268年）进士朱猛善和一位省元，率乡勇一千多人筑城抗元，"然其起事仓卒，力不能支，于是奔溃逃遗，众乃瓦解"（见《珍川朱氏宗谱》）。芙蓉村和廊下村以及其他不少村子都被元兵焚惨。

元代，楠溪江山寇蜂起，有些村落聚兵自卫，结果称霸一方。元末，方国珍军曾经影响楠溪上游毗邻乐清的廊下、花坛一带。本地也有一些"互相攻杀"的武装力量，"焚劫最惨"。

明清易代之际，楠溪江有"不轨之徒，出没其间"。康熙十四年（1675年），三藩之一的耿精忠部与清兵曾在楠溪江大战。战后，清兵把许多村落"烧荡一空"。同时，又有"白巾"在楠溪江中游和珍溪上游骚扰，"惨毒万状，民不聊生"。太平军也曾经到珍溪一带，各村组织乡勇，"义旗四出，奏凯而旋"。

组织乡勇义兵，抗元抗清，抵御太平天国，剿灭山寇，都靠宗族的力量。宗族也使用这些军事力量为争田、争水、争柴山而挑起村落之间的大规模械斗。正在他们"协力"抵御太平军期间，枫林村和岩头村就为了争柴山（名为"金山头刀税事"）而反复械斗，杀人放火。岩头村的许多建筑物，包括三列堂皇的"进士第"被完全焚毁。芙蓉和岩头、芙蓉和溪南、塘湾和泰石等，都因争柴山或溪水而成了世仇，甚至把仇恨写进《宗谱》，嘱子孙永不忘记。

由于不断发生各种各样的尖锐斗争，楠溪江有些村落，尤其是比较富裕有力的，造起了坚固的寨墙，完善的防御工程。目前保存得最完整的是芙蓉村、廊下村和坦下村的。其次是岩头村、苍坡村、花坛村的。许多村落的寨墙都只剩下断壁残垣。苍坡村的南墙、岩头村的东墙本都是蓄水的堤，当地叫做"垟"。前者可能造于南宋，后者造于明中叶。寨墙筑于何时失记。有些村的墙兼作抗洪的堤，如西岸、港头、宅、渡头、坦下、廊下、花坛和塘湾的。有些村子背后以陡峭的山为屏障，只在前面筑墙，如坦下、塘湾和廊下村。大多数的围墙都综合这样多种的功能。《棠川郑氏宗谱·新城记》说塘湾村筑城的经过：

"此地左有和龙、合冈，右有天岩、翼吉。四山环绕，名胜纷呈。所惜者独缺其前一面未能蔽御江风，以固我囿者也。于是乙未之冬，三时之暇，朝鸣、朝望诸翁有筑城之议。此议一倡，举地乐从，老者鸠工，壮者累石，同力合作，不数月而告厥成功。额其门曰通德，即取郑公讲学之芳名焉。又其东曰惠门，即王右军所谓惠风和畅之义焉。总记之曰新城，即筑自近日之意焉。是城也，可以为屏藩，可以为锁钥，可以为村坊之风脉，可以为洪水之堤防。一举告成，百端藉利，殆有合乎王公设险以守其国之道欤？"（文成于光绪戊申年，1908年）

这城筑于光绪二十一年（1895年），正是塘湾与南邻泰石村械斗高潮时期。1978年因建公路，此城被拆。

因为寨墙有切实的防御作用，所以它是村落明确的边界，寨墙之外没有散落的住宅。寨墙所包的范围，因而通常大于当时的村落，有的大了许多，如花坛村的南部和廊下村的西部，都有大片的空地，仍作农业生产之用，而后才陆续变为宅基地。芙蓉村的南部和北部，各有内外两道寨墙，两墙之间很少房屋。

兼作抗洪或蓄水之用的墙很厚，而且高。岩头村的东墙，最高处达3.5米，厚10~13米。苍坡村的南墙高2.5米，厚14米。为了防御，常常在作为堤坝的墙体之上再加一道0.5米左右的女儿墙，或者叫胸墙。港头、周宅和渡头临江一侧有一堵两阶的堤坝，第一阶的外侧有一米多高的胸墙，它与第二阶之间形成了一条巷道，仿佛有两道墙。纯粹为防御用的寨墙稍薄一点，矮一点。高度一般是1.8~2.0米，底脚厚1.2~1.5米，顶端厚0.6~0.8米。

墙是用大块蛮石砌的，下部石块比较大，往上渐渐减小。石块的长度平均在0.3米左右。大多数的墙上设方形孔洞，宽约15~18厘米，高约20厘米左右，距地多为65厘米上下，少数在1米以上。间距差别很大，近的不过3~4米，或者6~7米。这些孔洞是用来瞭望和施放火铳用的。

最雄伟的是廊下村的寨墙，它全长1500米，从西南到东北，保护村子的半边，另外一半则是山坡。墙外珍溪急泻而过，所以它兼作防洪的堤坝。外壁陡峭，从河床起算，高达8米，下部坡脚很大。内壁分三阶，每阶宽大约2米有余，高约1米，抗洪或者作战的时候，人员和装备可以在各阶上顺墙快速运动。

寨墙辟门，方便出入。每村必有一座正门，称作"溪门"，形制比较庄重。芙蓉村东门是一座两层的楼阁，三开间，上覆歇山顶。木构架有斗栱，单翘双昂，昂作象鼻式，曲线很柔和。门的现状很通敞，次间设美人靠，明间没有台基，阙作车行道。但从柱子及地面痕迹看，明间原有闸门，楼梯曾傍北侧次间的前檐，则可能有过前檐墙。当初它也是有很强的防御性的。

苍坡村的溪门朝南，在东、西两池之间。这是一座牌楼式的门，三开间，中央一间前面设台阶，可以穿过，左右次间各为高台基，不通行。门的木构架与岩头进士牌楼、花坛宪台牌楼和敦睦祠大门三座明代建筑物大致相同，很可能也是明代的遗物。它的斗栱宏伟壮硕，双翘双昂，上层的昂前托挑檐檩，后尾压在脊檩的下面，是重要的斜向构件。下层的昂与上层的昂之间，在外侧垫着一个斗，在内侧则没有，所以二者不平行。所有的构件，关系十分明确。虹梁曲栱，形体非常流畅。比例匀称，构图饱满紧凑，这是一件木构建筑的珍品。这座门本身的防御性不很强，但它的前面是一口半月形的水池，后面又是宽阔的东池和西池，只有一条石板桥在两池之间通向村里，所以，从整体看，它易守难攻，防御性还是不弱的。

现存的大多数寨门是石券门，在门洞里或门洞上造谯亭。例如廊下村的东北门和西南门，岭下村、坦下村和上烘头村的寨门，等等。石券门洞在相当于券的圆心位置设一根横梁，下面装板门，中央立门杠关死。廊下村的两座门的设计特别巧妙：东北角的寨墙向内曲折形成一个方形的凹袋，深12.0米，宽5.0米。东北门的石券辟在它的西侧，门洞宽约3.6米。这凹袋类似瓮城，攻门的敌人三面被围，而且很难直冲券门。凹袋之前是陡坡，陡坡之下是宽阔的珍溪，只有町步可走，攻门因而更加困难。西南门略微简单一点，没有凹袋，不过西寨墙在这里左右错了几米，石券门向北辟在这错开的地方，守门人可以打击攻门人的背后。门前也是陡坡和溪流。

谯亭大都是方形的，四棵金柱、十二棵檐柱。歇山顶。檐柱间设美人靠，开敞和易，本身没有防御性。如今有不少的改为三官庙，在后檐墙上砌神龛。岭下村和坦下村寨门的谯亭都很优美，飞檐远扬如展翼，美人靠、挂落等细节繁简得体，风格很典雅。优美典雅的谯亭有些是防御系统里的重要角色，如坦下的谯亭，可以瞭望村前的大片开阔地。廊下东北门的谯亭，正对着村门前的町步，监视过来的人。

芙蓉村的南门，东皋村的北门，渡头村的东门，都在门外有亭子，形式与谯亭一样，但大约都没有防御的功能了。它们轻巧的木构架和飘洒的飞檐，与粗犷沉重的蛮石砌筑的券门在一起，强烈的对比之下，画面很触目动人。

次要的寨门，或者只有一个券洞，或者横置一根石梁，都很简单。但有一些更简单的豁口，防御设计却很细致。溪南村的北墙，正对着世仇芙蓉村，中段有一个通达溪边的豁口。口西侧的石墙向前拐出，再向东延伸几米，正好掩住豁口，又与东侧的石墙形成了一个向东的开口，于是，进这个豁口就要经两次转折，而向前拐出掩住豁口的墙上还有监望和放火铳的孔洞。花坛村东门的设计更加周到。寨墙在临近溪边留了一个4.8米宽的豁口，在它的内侧大约5.5米处，正对豁口造了一面10.2米长的照壁；照壁上有几个瞭望和放火铳的孔洞，正好封锁住豁口。照壁前还有一个大致半月形的水池，深可及腰，以阻滞敌人对照壁的进攻。照壁的北面有一座谯亭，已经改成三官庙。亭内用砖和灰塑做的神龛和木作藻井，非常华丽精致，色彩也很鲜艳，在同类建筑中独一无二。它们构图饱满，比例匀称，是上好的建筑艺术作品。

时过境迁，村落的寨墙现在完全没有了防御作用，那些没有防洪蓄水功能的寨墙，年久失修，大多倒坍，或者因修路、扩村而拆除了。开敞的村落边界和村内开敞的街巷、住宅一起，给人一种祥和安谧的气氛，教人忘记楠溪江在古老的日子里，不但有过文化水平很高的耕读生活，也有过仇恨。残存下来的寨墙遗迹，斑斑驳驳，曲曲折折，只增加村落景物的古意。有些段落，甚至诗情画意，盎然成趣。例如下园村的寨门，位于高坡之上，坡下有三个串连的水池，门、墙、池、路，一色用蛮石垒成，连作整体一片，隙地里生长着浓密的大树。鲜衣美饰的姑娘们挎着鹅兜，到池边浣洗，映照着苍劲的寨门，古今交融，很能引发人的思绪。

二、路亭

楠溪江流域，除了中游以下的主流和几个大支流可以通小船和竹筏之外，全部交通，就只靠蜿蜒在山岭之上、沟壑之间的石子小路了。荷重负担，跋涉在这些小路上，异常艰辛。为了给旅人和樵子们歇脚，乡民们沿路造起了一座座凉亭。亭子大多是集资兴建的，由宗祠组织，少量由个人捐造，为父母积德祈福，叫做"孝子亭"。

山路上，亭子之间相距大约五里，选址多近清冽的泉水。亭边通常植树，浓荫覆盖，夏季可供路人一个凉爽的休息。楠溪江流域至少有过一千多座路亭，现在还有274座，其中有不少是明、清两代遗构。从路口乡的十二盘到渠口乡的泰石村，一路亭子保存得很完好，其中有11座造于明代，方形的石柱上还隐约可见"明万历己卯冬月建"或"大明崇祯七年八月建"等铭刻。

路边的亭子，一般比较简单，有些不过一间，硬山搁檩。大一点的有三间。河谷平川上，尤其邻近村落的，亭子多为正方形，面阔在4~6米左右，四棵金柱，十二棵檐柱，上覆歇山顶，形式比较精致，不过形制和形式变化不多。岩头村北双浚头的一座，用悬山顶，溪南村的一座，用粉墙封住前檐，留一个方形的门，但两侧仍然敞开。在碧绿的田野里，它的粉墙耀眼地明亮。西源村有一座八柱的重檐八角攒尖亭，造于清顺治十七年（1660年）。碧莲镇也有一座重檐八角攒尖亭，多一圈内槽柱子，一共十六棵，造于清道光十七年（1837年）。在越雁荡山脉去乐清县的山路边，有圆形和六角形的亭子各一座。上烘头村的路亭和埭头村至水云村中途的名为"肃王殿"的路亭，背靠山脚，前檐有两层，上层进深大于下层，坐实在山坡上，且有侧门直通山坡。这些是变化比较大的。那座肃王殿飞檐飘洒，大方而灵动。从它的廊下远望山脚的田畴村落，眼界空阔。

亭子里设美人靠或者栏杆凳，给疲累的过客休息，也自然成了他们辨认道路、计算脚程的标志。暴雨的黄昏，烈日的正午，那仿佛微笑着迎候归客的亭子，能给攀跻而来的路人多少亲切的慰藉。有些近村的亭子，每年从夏初端午到秋末重阳由村人供应药茶和暑药。柱子上挂着一串串金黄的草鞋，有大有小，赶路人坐落下来，翻过脚板，看草鞋破了，自己伸手取一双来换上。有些亭子设锅灶，旁边堆足柴草，赶远脚的人，用竹筒带着糙米和干菜，打一勺山泉水，点上火慢慢煮着，吃饱了，几袋烟一抽，便又精神焕发。岩头村南门口的乘风亭，贴一副对联，写的是：

茶待多情客；饭留有义人。

殷勤的主人称过客为"多情"和"有义"的人，心地多么仁厚。小小的路亭，从它们的建造到它们的设施，问饥问渴、问冷问热，乡人们互相间的关怀和体贴，滋润心田。亭子默默无言，丝雨中、夕阳下，它们给楠溪江的山水带上了温暖的笑容，行人们在这笑容中认得了父老乡亲。这就是乡风民俗。

几乎所有路亭都兼作三官庙，用砖墙封上后檐，设神龛。在重要的水源，也往往有一座兼作三官庙的凉亭。溪南村南边的泉源井，渡头村东门外的大口井，西岸村东北角的瓠瓜井，芙蓉村西门外一里左右的水渠头等等，都有这样的凉亭。因为三官里有一位水官大禹，要祈求他保佑村里清泉常流。

村庄妇女们洗涤的泉头渠边，往往也有凉亭。妇女们到水边来，多带着孩子，她们劳作的时候，孩子们可以在亭子里游戏。而且楠溪江夏季多骤然而来的暴雨，亭子是妇孺们避雨的场所。芙蓉村南门外，渠水很旺，浣衣女很多，虽然不是水源，也有一座兼作三官庙的凉亭。这些凉亭，地形、树木、井泉都很优美，成为小小的风景点。

浙东各地常见的风雨桥，在楠溪江却不多。北部的黄南村有一座，跨在穿村而过的急流上，五开间，连系着溪涧两岸。这也是一种路亭，成了村子的公共中心。农事间隙，人们常到这里聚谈家常。

三、商业建筑

在历史漫长的农业社会里，自然经济在楠溪江占统治地位，商品交换很不发达，没有什么商业建筑。几副货郎担就满足了多少乡民对小商品的需求。

到了清代，商品经济渐渐发展。楠溪江边少数几个村落里产生了手工业。利用江流航运，以山货土产换取温州商品的贸易也活跃起来，于是楠溪江中游出现了一些初级的商业中心，其中比较重要的有枫林、岩头、渠口、花坛等村子。这些商业中心有几十里的服务半径，周围许多村子依靠它们的货郎担，不再发展自己的商业。这种情况一直保持下来，到20世纪90年代大多数村子还没有商业。

这些村子大多在水陆交通线上，路程合适，是山货土产和温州商品的集散地。它们大多也是从沿海的永嘉或者乐清到山区的仙居或者缙云去的道路必经之地。乐清产海盐，向浙中、浙西，甚至皖南、赣北等内陆地区去的盐贩，在横穿楠溪江流域的几条道路上络绎不绝。于是，位置适当的村子，除了商店之外，还有了旅店、饭店、茶馆等等。到了清代末年，尤其是民国年间，有些村子里，如枫林和岩头，形成了紧凑集中的商业街道。

在这些村子里，最早出现了商业建筑。

商业建筑的形成，最初受到自然经济条件下根深蒂固的重农轻商思想的阻滞。《苍坡李氏宗谱》里，明文规定街面上不许开店，小买卖只能在家里做。后来，有些人家，在沿重要街道的厢房的前端或者外侧，开一个洞口做买卖。再后来，把这前端或者外侧翻改成店面。店面其实很简单，无非是前脸敞开，开关全用排板，临街设一个柜台。有一些店面，柜台相当于一段槛墙，台面以上用排板。

家里售货，临街开洞口，翻造店面，这三种类型在商业很不发达的芙蓉村、塘湾村等主要街道上都有。

岩头、枫林、东皋、廊下等初级商业中心的早期商店，形制仍然很简单，而且千篇一律。它们造在村中心的主要街道上，尤其是街道的交会点上，临街而建，面阔一、二、三开间的都有。店面敞开，柜台贴近前沿，顾客在街上买东西。二、三开间的店铺，也有让顾客进入店堂的，把柜台从前沿折返过来，延伸到店堂里就行了。关闭店面时，每间用六块或八块排板，也还有用柜台作槛墙，只在台面以上装排板的。稍大一点的店铺，把店号刻在木质的匾上，挂在店面枋子正中，或者刻在木牌上，竖立在曲尺形柜台的里端，正对外面。一般没有专门的幌子或者"童叟无欺"之类的牌子。

这种早期商铺仍然是店宅合一。大多是后半间里住人，前后或者用货相隔开，或者用板壁。许多店铺是两层的，但楼层低矮，裸露着椽子和屋瓦，夏季闷热，冬季寒冷，通常并不住人，只用作储藏。有些手工业品小店，如卖秤的、卖圆木家具的、卖篾编的，等等，店面的一部分甚至大部分是作坊。因为买卖大都是预订，并不需要陈列或者堆放成品。产品都是传统式样，很少创新，连样品都不必要。

旅店、饭店、茶馆都并没有、也不必要发展出自己特殊的形制。廊下村前街的西端，在几间店面的楼上有一家饭店，岩头村东门、献义门里，有一座茶馆，都不过是比较宽敞的空间而已。茶馆的形式很像路亭，但尺寸大一些。

岩头村的中心，旧进士街（今名金苍路）南端与横街相交的丁字路口上，有三座"苏式店面"，两座占据转角，一座在横街南侧，斜对着西北角上的一座。它们是整个楠溪江中游农村里最华丽的店铺。两层楼，楼上的槛墙外满满镶贴着复杂精巧的木栏杆式的装饰。上层略略挑出于下层，槛墙下作垂莲柱，雕饰很细致。把角的两座，充分利用它们的位置，向进士街和横街两面敞开。这三座店铺的风格显然是外来的，与楠溪江建筑的朴实不相协调，但这种对乡土建筑单纯的统一性的破坏，恰恰是商品经济的商业文化的特点。

进士街是岩头村最重要的一条街道。它的北端是由仁道门、金氏大宗祠、进士牌楼和贞节牌坊形成的全村最重要的礼制中心。它的南端是全村的"主星"所在。"主星"就是地面上用细卵石镶成的龙凤图案，关系到风水。这三座"苏式店面"居然挟"主星"而与大宗祠相抗衡，反映着商品经济体制与封建宗法制的抗衡。从宗谱里规定不许在街道上造店面，发展到店面作为全村最华丽的建筑与大宗祠抗衡，表明商品经济一旦产生，就必然会突破宗法制度，并且逐渐战胜它。

商品经济的进一步发展是产生了商业街。岩头村丽水街是统一规划、短期内建成的一条商业街。丽水街造在岩头村东缘的蓄水堤上。这道蓄水堤造于明代嘉靖三十五年（丙辰，1556年），长约300米，称为荷峰或者长埠，是桂林公主持的大规模水利和村落建设的一部分。它西侧形成了一个大水库，称为丽水湖或者长湖，与南面的进宦湖、镇南湖等等一起，是岩头村农田抗旱的保障。同时它们也是岩头村公园的一部分。岩头村"十景"之一就有"长堤春晓"。民国年间编写的《岩头金氏宗谱》里说："吾族昔为风水所迷，河堤之上只许种树莳花与建亭点缀风景而已，不与筑屋经商。凡为商贾者，咸于村内街头巷口之住屋为之。"风水关系到宗族的命运，河堤上不许筑屋经商，应该是一个很重要的规定。但是，由于从乐清赴缙云的道路在河堤上经过，"挑盐过缙云，一天一银钱"，盐贩比较富有，同时，岩头盛产稻米，收获季节大批外地农民涌来帮工。因此，在清代末年，河堤上已经有了零散的商摊，《岩头金氏宗谱》里说："民元以来，商业日渐发达，四处商贩云集，市场扩大，河峰一带自南而北，路之东西，悉已筑为商店"，经济利益冲击之下，"昔日之风水迷梦，今则破除之矣。"宗族的命运，毕竟决定于"一天一银钱"，而不是阴阳八卦。

大约在"悉已筑为商店"之后，丽水街才重新规划，一次重建，成为绵延300米，店面大约九十间的商业街。每间面阔3米上下。

河堤的宽度在13.0米左右。商店建在它的东侧，进深大约10.0米，两层。店前有3.0米宽的小街。街的另一侧就是长湖。整条街都用披檐遮盖，为了尽可能加宽小街，西侧的廊柱都立在一块块向外挑出大约30厘米的天然长条石块的端头上。柱子间设美人靠，美人靠悬空临水。所有的店铺都向小街敞开，顾客在街上选购商品，小街成了商店的扩展。

街面用块石铺。隔不远就有一道台阶下到水面，阶石都是天然长条石，一端砌进堤岸，一端挑出大约60厘米，每块之间分开，块块脱空，非常玲珑轻巧。

店铺都隔成前、后间，前间是店面，后间是卧室、厨房等等。楼上低矮，大多用于储藏。有些店家，向后延伸出一个后院，则院子就在堤外，比店面低许多。

丽水街南段微微向西弯曲成弧形，造成柔和的变化，消除了长街的单调，并且加强了它和湖水的亲切交往。它的南端，是寨墙的南门、门边高台阶上的乘风亭、亭前通向村里的明代嘉靖戊午年（三十七年，1558年）造的三跨石梁桥和桥头一棵数百年的大苦槠树。浓荫蔽天的老树下，几块天然石板随意地搭在水里，身着五彩衣衫的姑娘们在石板上搓洗衣服，雪白的鸭子悠悠地向水底扑啄她们的影子。桥的南侧，就是以琴屿为中心的公园，芙蓉花映着芙蓉峰，溢过桥来。丽水街的北端是献义门，长湖在这里宽不过10米，西岸就是一座茶馆，方方的，轻巧的美人靠映在水中。闲闲地啜茶吸烟漫话今古的老人们的身影，也在水光中倏忽荡漾。丽水街上虽然熙熙攘攘，但美人靠外，一片水色，秋芦夏荷，看不尽的风光。对岸远处，竹树掩映之下，粉墙黛瓦，参差几户人家。晚风拂来，淡淡的炊烟的苦味，教人心地平和，给奔逐于利途的人一丝温馨。一条商业街而有这样的情境，足见楠溪江文化中人文精神的强有力。

枫林镇的圣旨门街是楠溪江中游最繁华的商业街，风格与岩头村的丽水街完全不同。它以圣旨门为中心，向东西两侧延伸，全长400多米，是横贯全镇的轴线。从清代光绪年间起，圣旨门两边陆陆续续造起了店铺，因为枫林镇是一个山货土产的集中地。店铺造在街道两侧，街道是封闭的，但有小型广场、转弯、交叉路口等造成空间的变化。圣旨门街没有经过统一规划，商店先后建成，形式和形制的变化比较大，但大体仍旧有显著的共同点。店面是开敞式的，一间三间不等。一般为两层，上层往往用牛腿挑出，牛腿前有垂莲柱。牛腿和垂莲柱等处是雕饰的重点，不过比较简洁，并不华丽。由于街面热闹，店面经济效益高，所以居住部分往往与店铺分离，而造在第二进。一方面是为了增加营业面积，一是便于出租或者出让店面。

圣旨门街平均宽度约为8~10米，这条街充溢着强烈的商业气息，连曾经象征封建宗法制下家族最高荣誉的圣旨门也被商业侵占，只剩下明间的通道了。楠溪江的文化的人文精神，毕竟抵挡不住商品经济的冲击。

English Translation

The architectural types of Nanxi River are so rich that it is impossible to enumerate and describe them all one by one. However, some types, though less important than residences and ancestral halls, would seriously distort the true appearance of Nanxi River architecture if omitted. In this chapter, we selectively describe defensive works, roadside pavilions, and commercial buildings to avoid excessive omissions.

I. Defensive Works

In Nanxi River villages, most residences and some public buildings are outwardly open, and the village public centers, green spaces, ponds, and street systems are also outwardly open. They bare their bosoms to people, as pure and sincere as villagers,随时招呼邻里乡亲甚至过路人到家里闲话尝新。A stranger entering such a village would not feel watched with vigilant eyes or strictly guarded.

However, many of these villages shrouded in peaceful and harmonious atmosphere, especially the more prosperous ones in the river valley plains, are surrounded by thick stone village walls with carefully designed defensive functions.

A single-surname kinship village, in order to survive and develop before alien natural and social forces; under the intervention of clan relations, internal conflicts may be relatively缓和. But the stronger the village's cohesion, the more it will handle inter-village affairs as a whole clan, and even disputes between individual villagers may escalate into serious conflicts between villages. Clan relations are also the basis of feudal monarch-subject relations, so whenever the dynasty changed, there would be loyal ministers and righteous scholars in the remote Nanxi River mountain villages, performing tragic and heroic stories. As for peasant uprisings or bandits that existed throughout the feudal era, they certainly would not bow and yield upon seeing a jinshi archway.

Even in the Southern Song Dynasty when "academic names rose rapidly," Yang Jian, the prefect of Wenzhou, wrote in "Exhortation to Agriculture" that one of Wenzhou's major afflictions was "the custom of being contentious." After the Yuan and Ming dynasties, the custom gradually deteriorated. By the Qianlong period, "Yongjia County Annals · Customs" quoted the "Old Annals" saying: the local "custom is fierce and aggressive, its goods are delicate and extravagant, its scholars are willful and proud of their integrity." Common people are fierce and contentious, scholars are willful and proud of integrity, this Nanxi River where "the sound of string songs is incessant" often inevitably has fierce conflicts.

The relatively large turmoil in Nanxi River earliest was the arrival of Fang La's rebel army in the late Northern Song Dynasty. After Fang La's defeat, local Yu Dao'an led rebel people to fight for several more years.

The first large-scale war in Nanxi River was resistance against the Yuan army. The hero of this war was Chen Yuzhi of Furong Village. "Liangyuan Chen Clan Genealogy" records: "Lord Yuzhi, styled Yunweng, passed the jinshi examination in Xianchun Yichou of Song (1265). He first served as professor of Yangzhou, then transferred to Liangzhe transport commissioner in Huaidong commander's office, then appointed to the Ministry of Punishments and Works as archive clerk, then promoted to secretary of the Secretariat兼 compiler of the National History Academy, then recruited as recording staff of Prince Guang's mansion. Accumulated to Chengyi Gentleman. When Yuan troops arrived in Wenzhou, he and his clan nephew Lord Gui led over a thousand nephews and villagers to fight at Lüzhangyang, then retreated to Furong Rock, defending for three years. In Wuyin, upon hearing that Emperor Bing had drowned, he committed suicide, and the rock then fell. Lord Gui was captured and died unyielding. At that time over 800 died with him, over 50 were captured." Meanwhile, in Langxia Village, there was also Zhu Mengshan, a太常寺簿 and jinshi of Xianchun Wuchen (1268), and a provincial graduate, who led over a thousand village militia to build a city and resist the Yuan, "but their uprising was hasty, their strength could not hold, so they fled and scattered, and the multitude collapsed" (see "Zhenchuan Zhu Clan Genealogy"). Furong Village, Langxia Village, and many other villages were burned and devastated by Yuan troops.

In the Yuan Dynasty, mountain bandits swarmed in Nanxi River, and some villages gathered troops for self-defense, eventually dominating one side. At the end of the Yuan, Fang Guozhen's army once influenced Langxia and Huatan areas in upper Nanxi adjacent to Yueqing. There were also some local armed forces "attacking and killing each other," with "the most tragic burning and plundering."

During the Ming-Qing transition, Nanxi River had "lawless people appearing and disappearing." In the 14th year of Kangxi (1675), Geng Jingzhong's troops, one of the Three Feudatories, fought a great battle with Qing troops in Nanxi River. After the battle, Qing troops "burned and swept away" many villages. Meanwhile, "White Turbans" harassed the middle Nanxi River and upper Zhenxi, "cruel beyond description, people had no means of livelihood." The Taiping Army also once reached Zhenxi area, and villages organized militia, "righteous flags went out in all directions, returning in triumph."

Organizing village militia and righteous soldiers, resisting Yuan and Qing, defending against the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom, and exterminating mountain bandits all relied on clan power. Clans also used these military forces to provoke large-scale village fights over farmland, water, and firewood mountains. While they were "jointly" resisting the Taiping Army, Fenglin Village and Yantou Village repeatedly fought over firewood mountains (called "Jinshantou knife tax incident"), killing and setting fires. Many buildings in Yantou Village, including three grand "jinshi mansions," were completely burned. Furong and Yantou, Furong and Xinan, Tangwan and Taishi, etc., all became hereditary enemies due to disputes over firewood mountains or stream water, even writing the hatred into "Clan Genealogies," instructing descendants never to forget.

Due to continuous various sharp struggles, some Nanxi River villages, especially the more prosperous and powerful ones, built solid village walls and完善 defensive works. Currently the most completely preserved are those of Furong Village, Langxia Village, and Tanxia Village. Next are those of Yantou Village, Cangpo Village, and Huatan Village. Many villages' walls are only broken remnants. Cangpo Village's south wall and Yantou Village's east wall were originally water storage embankments, locally called "yang." The former may have been built in the Southern Song, the latter in the mid-Ming. When the village walls were built is unrecorded. Some villages' walls also serve as flood embankments, such as those of Xi'an, Gangtou, Zhai, Dutou, Tanxia, Langxia, Huatan, and Tangwan. Some villages use steep mountains as barriers behind, only building walls in front, such as Tanxia, Tangwan, and Langxia. Most walls combine multiple such functions. "Tangchuan Zheng Clan Genealogy · New City Record" describes the construction of Tangwan Village's city:

"This place has Helong and Hegang on the left, Tianyan and Yiji on the right. Surrounded by four mountains, scenic spots abound. Unfortunately only the front side is missing, unable to shield against river winds and secure our enclosure. Thus in the winter of Yiwei, during leisure from the three seasons, Lords Chaoming and Chaowang proposed building a city. Once this proposal was made, the whole land gladly followed, the elderly organized workers, the strong piled stones, working together with united strength, and in less than a few months it was completed. Its gate is named Tongde, taking the elegant name of Lord Zheng's lecturing. Its east gate is called Huimen, meaning the gentle breeze as Wang Youjun said. Overall it is called New City, meaning newly built recently. This city can serve as a screen and barrier, as a lock and key, as the village's fengshui vein, and as a flood embankment. Completed in one effort, benefiting a hundred things, perhaps it conforms to the way of kings and dukes setting up defenses to guard their states?" (Written in Guangxu Wushen, 1908)

This city was built in the 21st year of Guangxu (1895), exactly during the climax of fights between Tangwan and its southern neighbor Taishi Village. In 1978, due to highway construction, this city was demolished.

Because village walls had practical defensive functions, they were clear village boundaries, with no scattered residences outside the walls. The area enclosed by the walls was thus usually larger than the village at the time, some much larger, such as the southern part of Huatan Village and the western part of Langxia Village, which had large open spaces still used for agricultural production, only later gradually becoming residential land. Furong Village's southern and northern parts each had inner and outer village walls, with few houses between the two walls.

Walls serving as flood control or water storage are very thick and high. Yantou Village's east wall, at its highest point, reaches 3.5 meters, 10-13 meters thick. Cangpo Village's south wall is 2.5 meters high, 14 meters thick. For defense, a parapet wall of about 0.5 meters, or called breast wall, is often added on top of the embankment wall. On the river side of Gangtou, Zhouzhai, and Dutou, there is a two-step embankment, with a breast wall over one meter high on the outside of the first step, forming an alley between it and the second step, as if there were two walls. Purely defensive village walls are slightly thinner and lower. Height is generally 1.8-2.0 meters, base thickness 1.2-1.5 meters, top thickness 0.6-0.8 meters.

Walls are built with large rough stones, with larger stones at the bottom, gradually decreasing upward. Stone length averages about 0.3 meters. Most walls have square holes, about 15-18 cm wide, about 20 cm high, mostly 65 cm above ground, a few over 1 meter. Spacing varies greatly, close ones only 3-4 meters, or 6-7 meters. These holes are used for lookout and firing matchlocks.

The most magnificent is Langxia Village's wall, with a total length of 1500 meters, from southwest to northeast, protecting half the village, the other half being hillside. Outside the wall, Zhenxi rushes past, so it also serves as a flood embankment. The outer wall is steep, calculated from the riverbed, up to 8 meters high, with a large lower slope foot. The inner wall is divided into three steps, each step about 2 meters wide and 1 meter high, allowing personnel and equipment to move quickly along the wall on each step during floods or battles.

Village walls have gates for convenient entry and exit. Every village must have a main gate, called "stream gate," with a more solemn form. Furong Village's east gate is a two-story pavilion, three bays, covered with a hip-and-gable roof. The wooden frame has bracket sets, single cantilever double ang, with ang in elephant trunk form, with very soft curves. The gate's current state is very open, with beauty rests in the secondary bays, no platform in the central bay, used as a carriageway. But from column and ground traces, the central bay originally had a sluice gate, and the staircase was once beside the front eave of the north secondary bay, so there may have been a front eave wall. Originally it also had strong defensiveness.

Cangpo Village's stream gate faces south, between the east and west ponds. This is an archway-style gate, three bays, with steps in front of the central bay for passage, while the left and right secondary bays each have high platforms and are not passable. The gate's wooden frame is roughly the same as three Ming Dynasty buildings: Yantou jinshi archway, Huatan Xiantai archway, and Dunmu Ancestral Hall gate, and is likely also a Ming Dynasty relic. Its bracket sets are magnificent and robust, double cantilever double ang, with the upper ang's front supporting the eave purlin and its rear pressing under the ridge purlin, serving as an important diagonal member. Between the lower ang and upper ang, a block is placed on the outside but not on the inside, so the two are not parallel. All members have very clear relationships. Rainbow beams and curved bracket sets have very fluent forms. Well-proportioned, with full and compact composition, this is a treasure of wooden architecture. This gate itself is not very defensive, but in front of it is a crescent-shaped pond, and behind it are the broad east and west ponds, with only a stone slab bridge between the two ponds leading into the village, so overall it is easy to defend and difficult to attack, and its defensiveness is not weak.

Most existing village gates are stone arch gates, with watchtowers built in or above the gate opening. For example, Langxia Village's northeast and southwest gates, Lingxia Village, Tanxia Village, and Shanghongtou Village's gates, etc. The stone arch gate opening has a crossbeam at the position equivalent to the arch center, with plank doors below, and a door bar in the center to lock it. Langxia Village's two gates are particularly cleverly designed: the northeast corner's village wall bends inward to form a square recess, 12.0 meters deep and 5.0 meters wide. The northeast gate's stone arch is opened on its west side, with the gate opening about 3.6 meters wide. This recess is similar to a barbican, where attacking enemies are surrounded on three sides and find it difficult to charge straight at the arch gate. Before the recess is a steep slope, below which is the broad Zhenxi, with only stepping stones to cross, making attack even more difficult. The southwest gate is slightly simpler, without a recess, but the west village wall shifts left and right by a few meters here, and the stone arch gate opens northward at this offset, allowing defenders to strike attackers from behind. Before the gate are also steep slopes and streams.

Watchtowers are mostly square, with four golden columns and twelve eave columns. Hip-and-gable roof. Beauty rests are set between eave columns, open and amiable, with no defensiveness themselves. Now many have been converted to Three Officials temples, with shrines built on the rear eave wall. The watchtowers at Lingxia Village and Tanxia Village gates are both very elegant, with far-flying eaves like spread wings, and details such as beauty rests and hanging ornaments appropriately simple or complex, with a very elegant style. Some elegant watchtowers are important roles in the defense system, such as Tanxia's watchtower, which can overlook the large open area in front of the village. Langxia northeast gate's watchtower faces the stepping stones before the village gate, monitoring people coming over.

Furong Village's south gate, Donggao Village's north gate, and Dutou Village's east gate all have pavilions outside the gate, in the same form as watchtowers, but probably no longer have defensive functions. Their light wooden frames and flying eaves, together with the rough and heavy rough-stone arch gates, under strong contrast, create a very striking and moving scene.

Secondary village gates either have only one arch opening or a horizontal stone beam, all very simple. But some simpler openings have very detailed defensive designs. Xinan Village's north wall, directly facing its hereditary enemy Furong Village, has an opening in the middle leading to the stream. The stone wall on the west side of the opening juts forward, then extends eastward for a few meters, exactly covering the opening, and forms an eastward opening with the east stone wall, so entering this opening requires two turns, and the wall jutting forward to cover the opening also has lookout and matchlock holes. Huatan Village's east gate is even more thoughtfully designed. The village wall leaves a 4.8-meter-wide opening near the stream, and about 5.5 meters inside it, directly facing the opening, a 10.2-meter-long screen wall is built; the screen wall has several lookout and matchlock holes, exactly blocking the opening. Before the screen wall is also a roughly crescent-shaped pond, waist-deep, to hinder enemy attacks on the screen wall. North of the screen wall is a watchtower, already converted to a Three Officials temple. The brick and plaster shrines and wooden caisson ceiling inside the pavilion are very gorgeous and exquisite, with bright colors, unique among similar buildings. They have full composition and well-proportioned dimensions, being excellent architectural artworks.

With the passage of time, village walls now completely lose their defensive function, and those without flood control or water storage functions, due to long neglect, mostly collapsed, or were removed due to road construction or village expansion. The open village boundaries, together with the open streets and residences inside, give a peaceful and serene atmosphere, teaching people to forget that in ancient days Nanxi River not only had a high cultural level of farming-reading life, but also had hatred. The remaining village wall remnants, mottled and winding, only add ancient charm to the village scenery. Some sections are even full of poetic and picturesque charm. For example, Xiayuan Village's gate, located on a high slope, with three connected ponds below the slope, gate, wall, pond, road, all built with rough stones, forming an integrated whole, with dense trees growing in the gaps. Girls in bright and beautiful clothes carrying goose baskets go to the pond to wash, reflecting against the vigorous village gate, blending ancient and modern, very thought-provoking.

II. Roadside Pavilions

In the Nanxi River basin, except for the main stream below the middle reaches and several large tributaries where small boats and bamboo rafts can pass, all transportation relies only on stone paths winding over mountain ridges and between gullies. Carrying heavy burdens, trekking on these paths is extremely arduous. To provide rest for travelers and woodcutters, villagers built rows of pavilions along the roads. Pavilions are mostly built with collective funds, organized by clans, a few donated by individuals, to accumulate virtue and blessings for parents, called "filial son pavilions."

On mountain roads, pavilions are about five li apart, mostly located near clear spring water. Trees are usually planted beside pavilions, with dense shade covering, providing cool rest for passersby in summer. The Nanxi River basin had at least over a thousand roadside pavilions, and now there are 274, many of which are Ming and Qing Dynasty relics. From Twelve Plates in Lukou Township to Taishi Village in Qukou Township, the pavilions along the way are well preserved, of which 11 were built in the Ming Dynasty, with inscriptions such as "Built in the winter month of Wanzhou Jiming" or "Built in the eighth month of the seventh year of Chongzhen of the Great Ming" faintly visible on square stone columns.

Roadside pavilions are generally relatively simple, some no more than one bay, with gable roof and purlins resting directly on walls. Larger ones have three bays. In river valley plains, especially near villages, pavilions are mostly square, with a width of about 4-6 meters, four golden columns, twelve eave columns, covered with hip-and-gable roofs, with relatively exquisite forms, but not much variation in shape and form. One at Shuangjuntou north of Yantou Village uses a gable roof, one in Xinan Village uses plastered walls to seal the front eave, leaving a square door, but both sides remain open. In the green fields, its plastered wall shines brightly. Xiyuan Village has an eight-column double-eave octagonal pyramidal pavilion, built in the 17th year of Shunzhi of Qing (1660). Bilian Town also has a double-eave octagonal pyramidal pavilion, with an additional ring of inner columns, totaling sixteen, built in the 17th year of Daoguang of Qing (1837). On the mountain road crossing Yandang Mountains to Yueqing County, there is one round and one hexagonal pavilion each. Shanghongtou Village's roadside pavilion and the one midway between Daitou Village and Shuiyun Village called "Prince Su Hall," backed against the mountain foot, have two layers of front eaves, with the upper layer deeper than the lower, sitting firmly on the hillside, and with side doors directly leading to the hillside. These are relatively large variations. That Prince Su Hall has flying eaves, generous and agile. From its corridor, looking far at the fields and villages at the mountain foot, the view is vast.

Inside pavilions, beauty rests or railing benches are set for tired passersby to rest, naturally becoming landmarks for them to identify roads and calculate distances. At dusk in rainstorms, at noon under scorching sun, those pavilions seemingly smiling to welcome returning guests can give how much intimate comfort to travelers who have climbed up. Some pavilions near villages, from early summer Dragon Boat Festival to late autumn Double Ninth Festival, are supplied with medicinal tea and summer medicine by villagers. Strings of golden straw sandals hang on columns, large and small; travelers sit down, turn over their feet, see if straw sandals are worn, and reach out to take a pair to change. Some pavilions have stoves, with firewood piled beside; long-distance travelers, carrying brown rice and dried vegetables in bamboo tubes, scoop a spoonful of mountain spring water, light a fire and cook slowly, eat their fill, smoke a few pipes of tobacco, then become refreshed again. Chengfeng Pavilion at the south gate of Yantou Village has a couplet pasted, reading:

Tea awaits affectionate guests; rice is left for righteous people.

The diligent host calls passersby "affectionate" and "righteous," how kind-hearted. Small roadside pavilions, from their construction to their facilities, asking about hunger and thirst, cold and heat, the mutual care and consideration among villagers nourish the heart. Pavilions are silent, in silk rain and sunset, they bring warm smiles to Nanxi River's landscapes, and travelers recognize their fathers and fellow villagers in these smiles. This is village custom and folk tradition.

Almost all roadside pavilions also serve as Three Officials temples, with rear eaves sealed by brick walls and shrines set up. At important water sources, there is often also a pavilion serving as a Three Officials temple. The spring source well south of Xinan Village, the large-mouth well outside the east gate of Dutou Village, the gourd-shaped well at the northeast corner of Xi'an Village, the canal head about one li outside the west gate of Furong Village, etc., all have such pavilions. Because among the Three Officials there is Water Official Yu the Great, to pray for him to bless the village's clear springs to flow constantly.

Beside spring heads and canals where village women wash, there are often pavilions. Women coming to the water often bring children; while they work, children can play in the pavilions. Moreover, Nanxi River has sudden heavy rain in summer, and pavilions are places for women and children to take shelter from rain. Outside the south gate of Furong Village, canal water is abundant, with many washing women, though not a water source, there is also a pavilion serving as a Three Officials temple. These pavilions, with beautiful terrain, trees, wells, and springs, become small scenic spots.

Wind and rain bridges common in eastern Zhejiang are rare in Nanxi River. Huangnan Village in the north has one, spanning the rapid stream passing through the village, five bays, connecting both sides of the stream. This is also a type of roadside pavilion, becoming the village's public center. During farming breaks, people often gather here to chat about family matters.

III. Commercial Buildings

In the long historical agricultural society, natural economy dominated in Nanxi River, commodity exchange was very underdeveloped, and there were few commercial buildings. A few peddler loads satisfied how many villagers' demand for small commodities.

By the Qing Dynasty, commodity economy gradually developed. Handicraft industry arose in a few villages along Nanxi River. Trade using river shipping to exchange mountain products and local goods for Wenzhou commodities also became active, so some primary commercial centers appeared in the middle Nanxi River, among which the more important were Fenglin, Yantou, Qukou, Huatan and other villages. These commercial centers had a service radius of tens of li, and many surrounding villages relied on their peddlers, no longer developing their own commerce. This situation persisted, and by the 1990s most villages still had no commerce.

These villages are mostly on land and water transportation lines, with suitable distances, serving as distribution centers for mountain products, local goods, and Wenzhou commodities. They are mostly also必经 places on roads from coastal Yongjia or Yueqing to mountainous Xianju or Jinyun. Yueqing produces sea salt, and salt peddlers going to central Zhejiang, western Zhejiang, even southern Anhui, northern Jiangxi and other inland areas were incessant on several roads crossing the Nanxi River basin. Thus, villages in appropriate positions, besides shops, also had inns, restaurants, teahouses, etc. By the late Qing Dynasty, especially during the Republic of China, some villages, such as Fenglin and Yantou, formed compact and concentrated commercial streets.

In these villages, commercial buildings first appeared.

The formation of commercial buildings was initially hindered by the deeply rooted tradition of valuing agriculture and despising commerce under natural economy. In "Cangpo Li Clan Genealogy," it was explicitly stipulated that no shops were allowed on street surfaces, and small business could only be done at home. Later, some families opened an opening at the front or outside of wing rooms along important streets for business. Still later, they converted this front or outside into shop fronts. Shop fronts are actually very simple, nothing more than open front faces, opened and closed entirely with plank boards, with a counter facing the street. Some shop fronts have the counter equivalent to a section of sill wall, with plank boards above the counter surface.

Selling goods at home, opening an opening facing the street, and converting to shop fronts—these three types all exist on main streets in commercially underdeveloped villages such as Furong Village and Tangwan Village.

Early shops in primary commercial centers such as Yantou, Fenglin, Donggao, and Langxia still had very simple forms, and were all alike. They were built on main streets in the village center, especially at street intersections, facing the street, with widths of one, two, or three bays. Shop fronts are open, counters close to the front edge, and customers buy things on the street. Two- or three-bay shops also allow customers to enter the shop hall, by folding the counter back from the front edge and extending it into the shop hall. When closing the shop, each bay uses six or eight plank boards; there are also those using the counter as a sill wall, only installing plank boards above the counter surface. Slightly larger shops carve the shop name on a wooden plaque, hung in the center of the shop front beam, or carved on a wooden board, erected at the inner end of the L-shaped counter, facing outside. Generally there are no specialized shop signs or signs like "no deception for old and young."

These early shops were still shop-residence combined. Mostly people lived in the rear half, separated front and rear by goods or plank walls. Many shops are two-story, but the upper floor is low, with exposed rafters and roof tiles, hot in summer and cold in winter, usually not for living, only for storage. Some small handicraft shops, such as those selling scales, round wooden furniture, bamboo weaving, etc., have part or even most of the shop front as a workshop. Because most sales are by order, there is no need to display or stack finished products. Products are all traditional styles, with little innovation, not even needing samples.

Inns, restaurants, and teahouses did not, and did not need to, develop their own special forms. At the west end of Langxia Village's front street, above several shop fronts, there is a restaurant; inside Yantou Village's east gate and Xianyi Gate, there is a teahouse—both are nothing more than relatively spacious spaces. The teahouse form is much like a roadside pavilion, but larger in size.

At the center of Yantou Village, at the T-junction where the old Jinshi Street (now named Jincang Road) south end intersects with the cross street, there are three "Suzhou-style shop fronts," two occupying corners, one on the south side of the cross street, diagonally facing the one on the northwest corner. They are the most gorgeous shops in the entire middle Nanxi River countryside. Two-story, with the upper floor's sill wall fully inlaid with complex and exquisite wooden railing-style decorations. The upper floor slightly overhangs the lower, with pendant lotus columns under the sill wall, with very detailed carvings. The two corner ones make full use of their positions, opening to both Jinshi Street and the cross street. The style of these three shops is obviously foreign, incompatible with the simplicity of Nanxi River architecture, but this destruction of the simple unity of vernacular architecture is precisely the characteristic of commercial culture in a commodity economy.

Jinshi Street is the most important street in Yantou Village. Its north end is the village's most important ritual center formed by Rende Gate, Jin Clan Great Ancestral Hall, Jinshi Archway, and Chastity Archway. Its south end is where the village's "main star" is located. The "main star" is a dragon and phoenix pattern inlaid with fine pebbles on the ground, related to fengshui. These three "Suzhou-style shop fronts" actually leverage the "main star" to contend with the Great Ancestral Hall, reflecting the contention between the commodity economy system and feudal clan system. From clan genealogies stipulating no shop fronts on streets, to shop fronts as the village's most gorgeous buildings contending with the Great Ancestral Hall, shows that once commodity economy arises, it will inevitably break through the clan system and gradually defeat it.

The further development of commodity economy produced commercial streets. Yantou Village's Lishui Street is a commercial street built with unified planning in a short period. Lishui Street is built on the water storage embankment at the east edge of Yantou Village. This water storage embankment was built in the 35th year of Jiajing of Ming (Bingchen, 1556), about 300 meters long, called Hefeng or Changbu, as part of the large-scale water conservancy and village construction presided over by Lord Guilin. On its west side formed a large reservoir, called Lishui Lake or Chang Lake, together with the southern Jinhuan Lake, Zhennan Lake, etc., serving as the guarantee for Yantou Village's farmland drought resistance. At the same time they are also part of Yantou Village's park. One of Yantou Village's "ten scenes" is "Spring Dawn on the Long Embankment." The "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy" compiled during the Republic of China says: "Our clan was once confused by fengshui, on the river embankment only planting trees and flowers and building pavilions to adorn the scenery, not building houses for business. All merchants did business in residences at street corners and alley mouths within the village." Fengshui relates to the clan's destiny, and not allowing house-building and business on the river embankment should be a very important regulation. However, because the road from Yueqing to Jinyun passes over the embankment, "carrying salt to Jinyun, one silver coin a day," salt peddlers were relatively wealthy; meanwhile, Yantou produces abundant rice, and during harvest season large numbers of外地 farmers come to help. Thus, in the late Qing Dynasty, there were already scattered stalls on the embankment. The "Yantou Jin Clan Genealogy" says: "Since the Republic, commerce has gradually developed, merchants from all around gather, the market expands, from south to north along Hefeng, on both sides of the road, all have been built into shops." Under the impact of economic interests, "the former fengshui dream is now broken." The clan's destiny, after all, is determined by "one silver coin a day," not by yin-yang and eight trigrams.

Probably after "all built into shops," Lishui Street was replanned and rebuilt at once, becoming a commercial street stretching 300 meters with about ninety shop fronts. Each bay is about 3 meters wide.

The embankment width is about 13.0 meters. Shops are built on its east side, about 10.0 meters deep, two stories. In front of shops is a 3.0-meter-wide small street. On the other side of the street is Chang Lake. The entire street is covered by eaves; to widen the small street as much as possible, the west side's corridor columns are all erected on the ends of natural long stone blocks projecting outward about 30 cm. Beauty rests are set between columns, with beauty rests hanging over the water. All shops open to the small street, customers select goods on the street, and the small street becomes an extension of the shops.

The street surface is paved with block stones. Not far apart there is a step down to the water surface; step stones are all natural long stones, with one end built into the embankment and one end projecting about 60 cm, each separated, all detached, very exquisite and light.

Shops are divided into front and rear rooms; the front room is the shop front, the rear room is bedroom, kitchen, etc. The upper floor is low, mostly for storage. Some shopkeepers extend a backyard backward, then the yard is outside the embankment, much lower than the shop front.

The south section of Lishui Street slightly curves westward into an arc, creating gentle variation, eliminating the monotony of the long street, and strengthening its intimate interaction with the lake water. Its south end is the village wall's south gate, Chengfeng Pavilion on high steps beside the gate, the three-span stone beam bridge built in the 37th year of Jiajing of Ming (Wuwu, 1558) leading into the village before the pavilion, and a several-hundred-year-old large evergreen chinkapin tree at the bridgehead. Under the old tree with dense shade covering the sky, several natural stone slabs are casually placed in the water; girls in colorful clothes scrub clothes on the stone slabs, and white ducks leisurely dive to peck at their shadows in the water. South of the bridge is the park centered on Qin Islet, with hibiscus flowers reflecting against Hibiscus Peak, overflowing across the bridge. The north end of Lishui Street is Xianyi Gate, where Chang Lake is no more than 10 meters wide, and on the west bank is a teahouse, square, with light beauty rests reflected in the water. The figures of elderly people leisurely sipping tea, smoking, and chatting about past and present also ripple in the water light. Although Lishui Street is bustling, beyond the beauty rests is a expanse of water, with autumn reeds and summer lotuses, endless scenery. In the distance on the opposite bank, under the shade of bamboo and trees, plastered walls and black tiles, with several houses scattered. Evening breeze blows, with the faint bitter taste of cooking smoke, calming people's hearts, giving a trace of warmth to those rushing on the path of profit. A commercial street with such a scene sufficiently shows the strength of the humanistic spirit in Nanxi River culture.

Fenglin Town's Shengzhimen Street is the most prosperous commercial street in the middle Nanxi River, with a style completely different from Yantou Village's Lishui Street. Centered on Shengzhi Gate, it extends east and west, with a total length of over 400 meters, being the axis traversing the whole town. From the Guangxu period of Qing, shops were gradually built on both sides of Shengzhi Gate, because Fenglin Town was a concentration place for mountain products and local goods. Shops are built on both sides of the street, the street is enclosed, but with small squares, turns, intersections, etc. creating spatial variation. Shengzhimen Street was not uniformly planned; shops were built successively, with relatively large variations in form and shape, but generally still have notable common features. Shop fronts are open-style, from one to three bays不等. Generally two stories, the upper floor often overhangs with corbels (niutui), with pendant lotus columns in front of corbels. Corbels and pendant lotus columns are the focus of carvings, but relatively simple, not gorgeous. Due to the busy street and high economic efficiency of shop fronts, residential parts are often separated from shops and built in the second courtyard. On one hand to increase business area, on the other hand to facilitate renting or selling shop fronts.

Shengzhimen Street has an average width of about 8-10 meters; this street is filled with strong commercial atmosphere, even Shengzhi Gate, which once symbolized the family's highest honor under the feudal clan system, was encroached upon by commerce, leaving only the central bay passage. The humanistic spirit of Nanxi River culture, after all, cannot withstand the impact of commodity economy.

本节术语 Key Terms

防御工程 寨墙 石寨墙 寨门 溪门 石券门 谯亭 瓮城 凹袋 照壁 蛮石 女儿墙 胸墙 火铳 瞭望孔 美人靠 斗栱 歇山顶 牌楼式门 路亭 凉亭 孝子亭 硬山搁檩 悬山顶 重檐八角攒尖亭 风雨桥 药茶 草鞋 锅灶 三官庙

第四篇 匠作篇

Part Four: Craftsmanship
十二、大木作
中文原文重构

屋顶绝大多数是悬山式的，少数用歇山式。用硬山式或者封火山墙的极少，用了的年代都比较晚近。屋面用青瓦，正中起脊。

台基做法，大致是沿边用条石、块石甚至卵石砌台帮和阶条，里面填夯土。除了蛮石墙外，台基和地面是主要的石作工程所在。

所有木构件，除戏台之外，不论大小木作都不施油漆。

一、构架及构件

（1）构架分两大类，抬梁式的和穿斗式的。前者多用于宗祠、庙宇等重要公共建筑物的露明部分，后者多用于宗祠、庙宇的不露明部分和住宅。宗祠和庙宇，大多中央三间，即四榀屋架是露明的，因为梢间和尽间常隔成房间甚至做夹层。少数为五间六榀或七间八榀抬梁式露明，这时山墙位置的屋架即边榀，虽是穿斗式的，往往也露明。住宅一般是两层的，楼上主要用于储藏，所以屋架一概不露明，多用穿斗式。抬梁式屋架的用材和做工都很讲究，构件形式和尺寸比较规范化，而穿斗式的则大多比较随意，是草架，用天然形木料，只有祠堂、庙宇的山墙上露明的边榀很讲究，装饰性处理往往甚至强于中央抬梁式屋架。穿斗式与抬梁式的混合式梁架在祠堂、庙宇的山墙上也很常用。

宗祠和庙宇的正殿，大多是七架梁前后廊，但中央主梁为五架，因为按《鲁班经》"三架堂、七架厅，凶"。支承中央五架梁的柱子叫明柱。前檐廊上做卷棚轩，稍简单的做井口轩，后檐廊上都是井口轩。山区山村，所以当中四步，檩子上用大约5厘米×10厘米的枋子密排，既是椽子，又是望板。大宗祠和大庙宇，正殿进深大，明柱间仍为五架梁，而在檐柱和明柱之间再加一棵柱子，称前、后大步柱。大步柱与明柱和檐柱间都为两步。后檐廊置神橱，神橱加宽，在后檐柱外再加一排小柱子，与檐柱相距一步。两棵明柱之间虽然是五架梁，不过，除明间左右的两榀之外，其馀各榀梁架都是穿斗与抬梁混合式屋架，都有脊柱（当地称正栋柱）落地，所以每缝有七棵柱子，以致殿堂里柱子很多。

宗祠、庙宇、戏台、溪门、牌楼、亭阁等有斗栱，繁简不等。

（2）为了加大正殿的进深，有些宗祠或庙宇采用"双分房"，就是每一缝上用两榀屋架前后相接（即苏州所称鸳鸯厅），中间一棵柱子在前后都支承一个五架梁，在内部看有两个起脊，檩子之上却密排枋子封住。而在它们之间的山谷形空间里，再用草架支起檩子和正脊，形成包容前后两榀屋架的大屋顶。这种做法的优点，一是避免内部空间过于高旷，二是可以少用做脊柱的上好大木料。采用这种"双分房"的有廊下村的朱氏小宗祠、珠岸村的陈氏大宗祠、花坛村的敦睦祠和岩头村的塔湖庙，等等。

扩大内部自由空间的另一种方法是采用"偷柱造"，即不置明间的两棵前明柱，而在这地方架一根横跨三间的大梁，以支承中间两榀屋架上的五架梁。采用这种做法的有岩头村的水亭祠正殿。不过，横跨三间的大梁太长，容易劈裂（按：水亭祠的就已断裂），所以，经常的做法是，把明间开间放大到6米即一间半左右，在这开间里再添两棵后明柱和后檐柱，而前檐不添，用长约6米的梁承托添加的两根主梁。还有个别很复杂的"偷柱造"做法，如苍坡的水门底宗祠，不过基本原则不外乎以梁代柱，支承上面的构架。

甚至住宅也有"偷柱造"的，虽然屋架是穿斗式的。例如花坛村马湾相传建于宋代的老屋，在明间不但照例没有正栋柱，而且减去了两棵前明柱和两棵前檐柱，因此中央的檐檩和代替两棵明柱的梁都长达8.3米。显然，这座老宅的明间有很重要的礼仪性用途，它的梁架构件的尺寸都很大。

二、构件

屋架的主要构件是柱、梁、枋、檩。

（1）楠溪江的大木老司有自己的名称来称呼不同位置上的柱子。前、后檐柱称前、后小步柱，脊柱称正栋柱。在它们之间有前、后大步柱和前、后明柱。

柱子以圆形的居多，方形的也有不少。上下柱径不变，多数有天然木材的弯曲。有一些到柱顶边缘加工成四分之一圆弧。虽然楠溪江打石老司的技巧很高，石料也很丰富，但石柱很少，仅见于牌楼和塘湾郑氏大宗祠，调查中未见梭柱。

普通住宅，檐柱高约3米有余，柱径在22厘米左右。宗祠和庙宇，檐柱高可3.5米上下，柱径大约30厘米。乡土建筑，规范化程度比较低，因材施工，这些尺寸的变化幅度很大。

（2）除草架外，梁和枋子（按：当地人不区分梁和枋，概称为梁）大都为月梁（当地称欠梁）。最完全的是，除底面之外，两侧和上面，都呈极柔和、轻快、流畅的双曲面形，底面为单曲面。非常生动，丰满而富有弹性。它上面的曲线，中央曲率大而平缓，渐近两端，曲率渐小，曲线越紧，与宋代官式月梁相同。到头上，又在侧面作一个阴刻的曲线，反卷过去，十分有力，并且使月梁的形式独立而完美。稍简单一些的，底面平直。最简单的，两侧也平直，只有上面作双曲面。

大多数月梁，不论大小，都是由三块材料拼合的。一块在下面，两块在它之上相对，由银锭榫结合。采用这种做法，大约是为了便于加工，因为月梁的形式是很精致的。月梁的形式，既很简洁又很复杂，柔和轻捷，装饰效果很强，尤其是正面的枋子。苍坡村的溪门、花坛村的宪台牌楼，月梁都很灵秀俊逸。因为连雀门上很小的月梁也如此做，所以大约不是为了缺少合格的木料。

月梁两端的下面有细巧的"鸡舌"作装饰，它们是纯装饰的，不起结构作用。

屋架枋子的高度大致与柱子直径相等。它断面的高宽比约略是2:1。

露明的穿斗架，或穿斗、抬梁混合式屋架有一种斜向构件叫做"大头梁"或"泥鳅梁"。它们本身不对称，一头大一头小，一头圆一头方，轮廓复杂如鱼，圆转而有弹性，雕塑性很强。结构作用不大，但是很有装饰效果。

大木构架的构件和它们的组合形式，最有装饰效果的是前檐廊的卷棚轩。穿插枋，上面的两个坐斗，抱头梁，两棵小蜀柱（或者坐斗交麻叶云），角背，以及蜀柱外侧形式活泼而又华丽的斜向大头梁和泥鳅梁，再加上鹅项形（鹤颈形）的椽子或轩板，它们组成很完整的、紧凑而饱满的、匀称而富于变化的构图。有时，檐柱外侧还有雕刻复杂的牛腿或斜撑支承抱头梁头上的挑檐檩（当地叫方芩桁），又从支承点垂下莲花柱或花篮柱。它们和卷棚轩在一起，构图更加丰富。

椽子是圆形的。两层的住宅，上檐也有用方形椽子的。椽径大，椽档小，很密，上面直接铺仰瓦。祠堂和庙宇，前后明柱间的四步如为露明做法时，用密排的方木代替椽子。

（3）台基上，凡立柱子的地方必有一块柱顶石，方的。柱子脚下，又有一个构件，即柱础。楠溪江建筑的一个重要特色，就是还保存着许多木质的柱础。苍坡、周宅、港头、渡头、霞美、芙蓉、鹤垟、蓬溪、花坛、廊下等村子，都有不少住宅至今还有木质柱础。花坛的朱氏大宗祠和敦睦祠等祠堂也用木柱础。这些木柱础，从老化情况看，有的已经很古旧。不过，据霞美村木匠说，20世纪上半叶还有做木础的。楠溪江上游村落，如黄南、岩龙、佳溪、白岩等村，也有许多木础。

木础一般横放，以防毛细作用使它易于受潮腐烂，而且也更耐压。所以多取方形而不取圆形，在横放情况下，把木础做成圆形是既麻烦又不结实。木础通常不加雕饰，但如花坛朱氏大宗祠和敦睦祠的有些木础，上面有粗简的半圆线脚，下部略呈凹弧面，叫"大头方础"。住宅的木础，大多高25厘米，长宽各为30~35厘米。宗祠里的，尺寸随柱径而有许多差别。

目前情况，石的柱础多于木的。石柱础式样比较多，而且明显的有等级性。大致是：

第一级：鼓形，刻云龙纹。苍坡村现存六个，是从乾符灵山院取来的。寺院建于唐玄宗先天年间（712-713年），如今已平毁无余。它们高约42.5厘米，直径约52.0厘米。浮雕游龙非常天矫有力，神采飞扬，却又很简洁，是很有价值的艺术品。

第二级：虎爪礅。有圆有方，四周深浮雕作密排的虎爪。大量用于宗祠，但只有出过相当高级的官宦的房派宗祠或大宗祠才可以用。虎爪礅是现存柱础中级别最高的。

第三级：盘礅和篮礅。圆形，后者如花篮，有雕饰，主体常做瓜瓣式，下部有短短一段圆柱体。前者主体作一个圆柱体，也有雕饰。

第四级：有浅浮雕的大头方础。

第五级：碗礅、盆礅、园林礅。外形比较简单，不做雕饰。

第六级：无雕饰的大头方础。

第七级：方礅。有完全的立方体，也有在四棱斜削出一个小小的三角形面，使上表面呈切角正方形。

不同等级的柱础在建筑物里的位置大致是：住宅里，前檐柱（前小步柱）有用盘礅或篮礅的，用碗礅、大头方础等的也有，其他的位置一律用方礅。宗祠里，以正厅的明间里四棵明柱的柱础等级最高，尺寸也大。其次为全部前檐柱，再次为次间的明柱，再次为大步柱，最后是后檐柱。所以在一个正厅里，可以见到多种柱础。虽然它们的位置有一定规矩，但看起来还是显得杂乱。

三、斗栱

楠溪江流域，宗祠和庙宇大多使用斗栱，尤其在戏台和大门上。溪门和重要的亭阁中多用斗栱，旌表性建筑物如木牌楼，斗栱非常复杂华丽。住宅中用斗栱的也不少，但很简单。

斗栱也有柱头科、角科、平身科三类。平身科只用于戏台和牌楼，以及少数牌楼式的大门，做法与柱头科一样。不同于清代官式，平身科并不密排，各攒之间距离比较大而随意，看上去疏朗。宗祠和庙宇的殿堂只有柱头科而没有平身科。角科只用于歇山顶的建筑物，如各村的戏台和芙蓉村的溪门、苍坡村的望兄亭和方巷的送弟阁，等等。

住宅一般只在柱头上安一个坐斗，坐斗的形式比较古老，有圆的和圆角海棠瓣的。极少用方形的坐斗，檐廊里穿插枋和抱头梁上承接卷棚轩或井口轩的枋子或者檩子的小构架，有方形的坐斗。有些住宅，在柱头上出一翘托住挑檐檩。

升、斗和拱，基本做法很合程式。但它们的组合很多不合官式规则的变化。加入装饰性构件后更加变化自在，所以不论是构件还是成攒的斗栱都不可能准确地使用官式名称，无论是宋式或清式。

少数更讲究一些的，有正心瓜栱和厢栱。不论只出一翘的还是有瓜栱、厢栱的，都有升子（拱身称猫耳朵）。

宗祠的大门、戏台和重要的寨门，常用单翘重昂的斗栱，有时翘被装饰性构件替代，昂嘴也极富装饰性，细长而卷曲，称为象鼻。偶然也用琴面昂，很少。以芙蓉村陈氏大宗祠戏台为例，双重昂直接从坐斗挑出，无翘。平身科在承托挑檐檩的地方挂花篮柱。柱头科和角科下有一个斜撑从柱子上端伸出。角科也只有重昂。斜撑和花篮都作圆雕，斜撑甚至是个人物（仙、神）的雕像，姿态生动，不作建筑化处理。

最华丽的斗栱用于戏台和宗祠的藻井，如芙蓉陈氏大宗祠、廊下村凤南宫、渠口叶氏大宗祠和塘湾郑氏大宗祠的戏台和豫章胡氏小宗祠明间中央等。花坛村东门的三官庙（谯亭），也做这样的藻井。它们的基本做法是，从角科和平身科里拽斜向向上出若干跳的翘，每翘头上有横栱及枋子，最后为中央一块正方形的镜板。渠口、塘湾和豫章村的，上面是八角形藻井，则从角科伸出的翘在转换成八角形后就中止了，由八攒平身科的出翘继续向上。每个翘都是像流云卷草一样俯仰环曲，每个横栱两头都做"元宝斗"，像纱帽翅，而下面的梢子又刻成花篮、覆莲或者流苏。形制和做法都不同于细金斗栱。

楠溪江中游有四座建筑物的斗栱很特殊，它们是花坛村宪台牌楼和敦睦祠大门，岩头村进士牌楼和苍坡村溪门。两座门都是牌楼式的，所以可以说是四座牌楼。前三者据记载可以确认是明代建筑，苍坡村溪门虽无确证，但做法与前三者完全一样，可以判定为明代遗构。除了比例尺度硕壮雄健，造型简洁，结构脉络清晰之外，它们的斗栱还有两个特点：一是大量使用偷心造的丁头栱，一是用起结构作用的下昂。例如苍坡溪门平身科，从坐斗出三挑丁头栱，上面再架两层下昂。下层下昂前端承托挑檐檩外的一条檩子。后尾压在脊檩之下。下层下昂前端重复承托那条檩子，后尾直接嵌入上层下昂的体内，二者共同形成一个杠杆结构。花坛敦睦祠大门也是两层下昂，但下层下昂的后尾形同一翘，只给上层下昂起衬垫作用，而上层下昂则是杠杆结构。

多层丁头栱普遍用于小型建筑物上，主要是木构的院门。这种门只有一间，左右各一棵柱子，叫做"二柱门"。柱子前后，对称地跳出四五层偷心的丁头栱，前端承托檩子，上面做坡瓦顶。这些丁头栱，轻捷而有力，条理清晰，在十分简单明了的小门上起着很大的造型作用，使素朴典雅的小门显得高贵。周宅村土地庙前的二柱门，最下层第一跳丁头栱作装饰性的曲线形，使整个小品建筑更添了一分妩媚。

四、举折、生起和侧脚

楠溪江的建筑装饰不多，很朴素，匠师们重视房屋整体的造型。为了使房屋整体看起来生动飘逸，在大木构架上作了些特殊的处理，这就是举折、生起和侧脚。

房屋的木构架的外形是很轻快通透的。大面积的屋顶，如果不加处理，就会单调而呆板。因此，首要的问题是处理屋顶，使它生动飘逸起来。

处理的有效办法是使屋顶成为一个双曲面，上下是弯曲的，左右也是弯曲的。上下的弯曲靠举折，左右的弯曲靠生起。当侧脚应用于体形完整地呈现的建筑物如亭子、寨门等的时候，它们的屋顶就更加显得轻扬如鸟翼。
